# [Blank Leaf / Flyleaf]

This page is a blank leaf, likely serving as an endpaper or flyleaf for the manuscript. While it contains no formal written text or Latin inscriptions, the physical surface preserves several historical marks:

*   In the center-left area, there is a faint, illegible oval stamp. <note>Such stamps are typically institutional marks used by libraries or private collectors to indicate ownership.</note>
*   A small, dark mark or character is visible near the top left corner.
*   <margin>v-shaped mark</margin> — A small mark appears on the left margin.
*   At the bottom of the page, there is a rectangular area of lighter-colored paper. <note>This "ghosting" or light patch suggests that a paper label or bookplate was once attached here and has since been removed or fallen off.</note>

The entire surface shows signs of foxing <note>age-related spots and browning caused by chemical reactions in the paper or old mold</note> and the distinct, irregular texture characteristic of historical handmade paper.

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page serves as the printer's or editor's preface to a pocket-sized edition of Angelo Poliziano's "Miscellanea." It establishes the scholarly value of the text while explaining the additions made for this specific printing.</meta>

->### TO THE READER<-

**Although** there is almost nothing, even the smallest detail, in the highly polished writings of **Poliziano** <note>Angelo Poliziano (1454–1494), also known as Politian, was a brilliant Italian humanist and poet of the Renaissance</note> in which the highest erudition combined with the highest eloquence is not admired—for that man triumphed so successfully in both languages <note>original: "utraque lingua"; referring to Latin and Greek</note> and both styles that you cannot easily find anyone among the moderns to compare to him—nevertheless, he seems to me to have provided a principal specimen of his talent in these ***Miscellany*** commentaries. In these, immortal God, how profound is the learning, how varied the reading in both languages, how exact the judgment, and to put it simply, what all-encompassing doctrine is found in such a tiny book. By Hercules, these notes are not only suitable for **grammarians** <note>In the Renaissance, a "grammarian" was not just a teacher of rules, but a sophisticated literary scholar and linguist</note>—even if Poliziano modestly claimed only that role for himself—but they are also by no means to be despised by **lawyers** and **philosophers**. This is especially true since there is no genre of writing in which Poliziano did not engage with the highest praise, and which he did not examine most diligently.

Therefore, so that these works might be more readily at hand for students, and could be carried without trouble by those walking about—either in a pocket or in the hand—we have compressed them into a <term>handbook</term> <note>original: "enchiridion"; a Greek term for a manual or small book meant to be kept close at hand</note>. They have been corrected and annotated with no small effort, as the reader himself will discover. We have also added an index of things and words that seemed especially worth highlighting. Finally, we have prefixed an addition: an interpretation of the **Greek passages** which were omitted by the author himself and explained by **Jacques Toussain** <note>original: "Iacobo Tusano" (1499–1547), a noted French scholar of Greek</note>. We did this so that the inattentive reader would not mistake our "dreams" (or those of another) for the lasting monuments of the author himself and, as it were, worship them as his own. Farewell.

->[Stamp: Bavarian State Library Munich]<-

<summary>This preface introduces a portable "handbook" edition of Poliziano's Miscellanea, praising the author's mastery of Latin and Greek while noting the addition of an index and Greek-to-Latin translations by Jacques Toussain.</summary>
<keywords>Angelo Poliziano, Miscellanea, Jacques Toussain, Humanism, Renaissance scholarship, Latin literature, Greek translation, Enchiridion, philology</keywords>

->OF THE GREEKS<-

### Fourth Gathering
<note>original: Quaternio D</note>

O dear son of Kleinias and Deinomache. <note>This address is directed at the famous Athenian statesman and general Alcibiades (c. 450–404 BCE), who was known for his aristocratic lineage and scandalous life.</note>

"But come now, change your theme and sing of the fashioning of the horse, the wooden one which Epeius built with the help of Athena." <note>This is a quotation from Homer’s Odyssey (Book 8, lines 492-493), where Odysseus asks the bard Demodocus to sing of the Trojan Horse.</note>

"The great wooden horse, where all the best men sat." <note>original: aristoi</note>

"But indeed, they occupy the very furthest corner, near the Syllae of the Moorish land." <note>original: Maurousidos gaies. This refers to the tribes of North Africa, often associated with the limits of the known world in ancient geography.</note>

As they were also mocked in comedy. <note>Suggesting that these people or themes were subjects of satire in the theater.</note>

### Fifth Gathering
<note>original: Quaternio E</note>

He who is clear in both his name and his purpose.

To Hermes and Hercules, the overseers of the wrestling school <term>palaistra: a public place for training in wrestling and athletics</term>, he is not in the least bit suited; but for Cotytto <note>A Thracian goddess whose festivals, the Cotyttia, were notorious for their perceived lewdness and secrecy</note> and the other Athenian spirits of the dust <term>konisaloi: personifications of the dust raised in the wrestling ring, often depicted as phallic, lewd demons</term>, he serves as a temple-warden.

And he alone did not wish to gaze upon the pleasure of swine.

**INTERPRETATION.**

The pleasant garments of those dressed in purple, the kings, were turning red. <note>original: "purpuratorum rubefiebat ueſtis," likely referring to the deep crimson or purple dyes associated with royalty.</note>
No funeral dirges for the mournings, no lyre is pleasing. <note>original: "naula," a Greek-derived term for a funeral song or a traveler's fare.</note>
Those dressed most softly.

**Ascopera**: a traveler’s pouch or a small bag. <term>Ascopera (a leather bag or knapsack used by travelers or pilgrims)</term>
Echo, dear to me, respond with something. *...what?*
I love Corisca, but she does not love me. *...she loves.*
But the time does not allow for an opportunity to approach her. *...it allows.*
Therefore, you tell her that I love her. *...I love.*
And you give her a guarantee of money. *...you give.*
Echo, what is left? To obtain my wish? *...to obtain.* <note>This is an "Echo Poem," a popular literary device where the final syllables of a line are repeated as an "echo" to answer a question or change the meaning.</note>
I am truly measuring the character of the elderly hostess.
The envy of Apollo spoke secretly into my ears.

I do not admire that singer, who does not even sing as much as the sea. <note>original: "põtus" (pontus). This likely refers to a singer whose voice lacks the vastness or depth of the ocean.</note>
Even if you gnaw me down to the root, I will still produce enough fruit
To sprinkle upon you, O goat, when you are sacrificed. <gloss>supply: "enough."</gloss> <note>This is a famous epigram by the Greek poet Evenus, where a vine warns a goat that it will still produce wine for the goat's own sacrificial ceremony.</note>
Even if you gnaw me, etc.
Enough to pour over the sacrificed Caesar. <gloss>supply: "it may suffice."</gloss> <note>original: "Cæſari imolato." This appears to be a adaptation of the goat-and-vine epigram, perhaps applied to a political context.</note>
The Corinthian women have come out of their houses. <note>This and the following lines are Latin translations of the famous speech from Euripides' tragedy, *Medea*.</note>
Do not hold anything against me as a crime; for I have known many
who were considered honorable, some indeed far from the eyes of mortals,
others truly in foreign lands; but men, through a life of idleness,
have brought infamy and a reputation for sloth upon themselves.
Would that the ship Argo had never flown forth. <note>The opening line of the tragedy *Medea*, referring to the ship that brought Jason to Medea's land.</note>

# **OF THE GREEKS**
<note>original Latin: "GRAECORVM"</note>

...to the land of Colchis, through the dark Clashing Rocks,
and that the pine in the glens of Pelion had never fallen,
hewn by the axe, nor had it put oars into the hands
of the best of men, who went after the all-golden fleece
for Pelias. For then my mistress,
Medea, would not have sailed to the towers of the land of Iolcus,
her heart struck with love for Jason.
<note>These are the famous opening lines of Euripides' tragedy, *Medea*. The speaker, Medea's nurse, wishes the Argo had never sailed, preventing the tragic events that follow.</note>
It causes a disturbance.
<note>original: "θορυβοποιεῖ". This may be a stage direction or a scholarly comment on the effect of the previous lines.</note>

And an aged servant, thinking perhaps
that the wrath of Pan or some other god had come upon her,
raised a cry of prayer.
<note>Another excerpt from *Medea* (lines 1171-1173), describing a servant's reaction to Medea's rival's sudden agony, which she initially mistakes for a "divine frenzy" or "panic."</note>

Athamas, driven mad by the frenzied whip of Pan,
<note>Athamas was a mythological king driven to madness. The "whip of Pan" refers to the sudden, overwhelming fear or madness—the origin of our word "panic."</note>
and always those within are listening.
The thundering sound of the Pan-like, Cronian whip resounds,
where would that the boundary of the shield [were] before the mind.
Who shall extinguish the Isaurian manliness,
<note>Isauria was a rugged region in Asia Minor; its people were often described by ancient writers as fierce or rebellious.</note>
who killed his grandfather and took away the cheese.
<note>The Greek here is slightly fragmented or may refer to a specific local myth or lost comedy.</note>

## **Gathering H**
<note>original Latin: "Quaternio H". This refers to the eighth "quire" or section of the physical manuscript, used by bookbinders to keep pages in order.</note>

*Wasps*
<note>A reference to Aristophanes' comedy, *The Wasps*.</note>
And I shall flash like lightning, they make a clicking sound.
It is the custom for lightning to make a clicking sound.
The teamster makes a clicking sound.
Or if there is any inarticulate noise, as in the case of clucking.
<term>clicking/clucking <note>original: "ποππυσμάτων" (poppysmaton). A term for a specific sound made by the lips, often used to soothe horses or as a superstitious reaction to lightning.</note></term>
I shall be in good health.
It is time to move on to the swift things without tension.
<note>This appears to be a concluding remark by the scribe or commentator, perhaps indicating a change in the pace of the lessons or the copying work.</note>

<meta>This page appears to be a scholar's notebook or a collection of "interpretations" (Interpretatio), likely translating or commenting on classical Greek drama into Latin. The first section is a direct translation of the opening lines of Euripides' *Medea*, while the latter sections reference Aristophanes' *Wasps* and other mythological fragments.</meta>

# INTERPRETATION

In the land of the Colchians, near the Cyanean Clashing Rocks <note>original: "Cyaneas symplegades" — the legendary "clapping rocks" at the entrance to the Black Sea</note>, 
Neither in the forests of Mount Pelion had there ever fallen
The cut pine-tree, nor had the hands of the most
Distinguished men driven the oars, those who sought
The Golden Fleece for Pelias; for then my mistress, 
Medea, would not have sailed to the towers of the Iolcian land,
Her mind struck by love for Jason.

She is disturbed. <note>This describes Medea's state of mind in the prologue of the play</note>
And a certain old woman among the servants, perhaps thinking 
That either the wrath of Pan or some other god was at work,
Howled. <note>original: "Ululauit" — a ritualistic or fearful shriek</note>

Athamas was driven by the insane lashing of Pan,
For always into his very ear
The booming of the Saturnian panic-inducing whip resounds.
Whose mind was once set on fire by the boss <note>The central protruding part of a shield</note> of a shield.
Who extinguished the arrogance of the same name
With the grandfather-killer <note>original: "auicida" — likely a variation of *avicida*</note>, and took it away from the Tyrian woman.

# The Wasps <note>Referring to the comedy by Aristophanes</note>

And I shall flash like lightning with those who make smacking sounds <note>original: "poppyfantibus" — from *poppysma*, a sound made with the lips to calm horses or avert omens</note>.
The custom is to make a smacking sound during lightning.
The woman driving the chariot makes a smacking sound.
Or if there is any inarticulate noise, as in smacking or clucking sounds.
Of healing or healthy things.
To truly pass the time toward swift things without tension and force.

->OF THE GREEKS<-
<note>original: "GRAECORVM"</note>

...and of violence; these are but races and shadow-fights.
<note>The text begins mid-sentence, discussing "skiamachia" (shadow-fighting), a term used for sparring or fighting an imaginary opponent.</note>
Similarly, the phrase "we shall fight in the shade" 
<note>A reference to the famous Spartan retort at the Battle of Thermopylae when told the Persian arrows would blot out the sun.</note>
indicates nothing bad. However, the compound word *shadow-boxing* 
<term>shadow-boxing (skiamachein): to engage in a mock-fight or to struggle with an imaginary foe.</term>
[does not necessarily imply the same...]

The Instrument.
<note>original: "ὄργανον" (organon). This often refers to Aristotle's works on logic or a specific textbook.</note>

Euhemerus, an old braggart, airing out <note>or "writing"</note> unjust books.
<note>Euhemerus was a Greek mythographer who argued that the gods were originally historical kings or heroes.</note>

Cretans are always liars; for Cretans even built a tomb for you, O Lord, 
but you did not die, for you live forever.
<note>A famous poem by Callimachus, also quoted in the New Testament (Titus 1:12). The "lie" refers to the Cretan claim that Zeus had a grave, which was considered blasphemous by those who believed gods were immortal.</note>

To play the Cretan against a Cretan.
<note>A proverb meaning "to out-cheat a cheater" or "to lie to a liar."</note>

The Idaean Dactyls of Crete, whom once the nymph 
Anchiale, in the Dictaean cave, grasping 
the earth of Oiax with both hands, brought forth.

upon which all the statues of the laws gleam.

We were once robust youths.

Once upon a time, the Milesians were brave <note>A proverbial expression used to describe those who have fallen from their former glory</note>.

A great contest was once proposed concerning Theodore Gaza <note>c. 1398–1475, a famous Greek humanist and translator who was instrumental in the revival of Greek learning in Italy</note> and the Muses of Italy and Greece. To the latter <note>the Heliconian Muses</note> he owed his birth, and to the former <note>the Ausonian or Italian Muses</note> the rewards of his upbringing. For Greece bore him, but Italy nurtured him. He excelled equally in the wisdom and the language of both. This dispute he did not resolve while living, nor certainly in death; for he wished to lie in Italy’s *Magna Graecia* <note>The historically Greek-settled region of Southern Italy</note>, so that the glory might be shared by both nations.

[Large decorative initial 'C' containing an illustration of a rabbit or hare] WHEN I WAS RECITING these *Miscellanies* <term>Miscellanies: from the Latin *Miscellanea*, a genre of literature consisting of a collection of various scholarly notes, observations, and short essays on diverse topics.</term> of ours to you a few days ago, Lorenzo de’ Medici, while we were out riding, I believe you were delighted by the very novelty of the topics and the not unpleasant variety of the reading. You began to urge me to publish at least one Century <note>original: "Centuria." Poliziano organized his scholarly observations into groups of 100, modeled after ancient Roman legal or military divisions.</note> of them (for each individual book is organized into one hundred chapters). 

I am now doing this, moved partly by your authority—than which none on earth is greater for me—and partly by reason. This is because some people have already swept up <note>original: "cum puluisculo conuerrunt," a Latin idiom meaning to snatch something up entirely, including the very dust, implying others were plagiarizing or circulating his notes without permission.</note> these works of ours, so that they have long taken too much pleasure in the title of our labors, for others' benefit as well as their own. Although I know these books will be met with great envy and will be the subject of much talk, as works which...

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page begins the Preface (Praefatio). The text starts mid-sentence, suggesting the initial letters of the first word were lost or were part of a decorative drop-cap "M" for "Magni."</meta>

# PREFACE

...[ma]gni authors of great name might freely pronounce. But that is not our example. For almost all writers defend their own parts in such a way that they attack those of others, and they frequently seek an opportunity for praise from the errors of others. Thus, even those leading men (if I may call them so) and the "vanguard" of letters are often mocked by lesser and common men of the lowest rank, such as myself, or a **Cluvienus** <note>Cluvienus: A name used by the Roman satirist Juvenal to represent a mediocre or unremarkable poet.</note>. However, there is an old saying: *Conscience is a thousand witnesses* <note>original: "Conscientia mille testes"</note>. 

Conscience knows quite well that nothing here has been given over to hatred, nothing to bitterness; we have brought forth everything candidly and simply, nothing with a spirit of snapping at others or with malice, nothing with a desire for barking, and nothing with a wish for showing off. Our mind has always been more inclined toward approving (if it were allowed) than toward disapproving. We have conducted this business clearly without <term>tricks or hidden tunnels</term> <note>original: "strophis aut cuniculis." "Cuniculis" refers to the military practice of digging secret mines or tunnels to collapse a wall; here it means underhanded or deceptive tactics.</note>, but with a free examination and the open face of truth. 

And we did not seek this so that we might sprinkle some small stain upon learned men, but rather we took care lest the faith of students be endangered under the weight of their authority. Finally, this wedge has been expressly aimed at those, and this <term>battering ram</term> <note>original: "aries." A heavy siege engine used to break down gates or walls.</note> has been deliberately tuned against those, who harden their faces against the appearance of truth in favor of their own local—yet imaginary—understanding. We especially sting with the point of our pen those who have not learned to <term>turn the stylus</term> <note>turn the stylus (stilum vertere): A Latin expression meaning to erase or correct what has been written. The flat end of a stylus was used to smooth over wax tablets to delete text.</note>—that is, those who have not learned to strike out their own errors. 

As for this disorderly and confused collection, presented like a wild forest or a <term>farrago</term> <note>farrago: originally a mix of various grains used for animal feed; here it refers to a miscellaneous collection of ideas or writings.</note>, because it is not handled continuously...

<summary>In this preface, the author defends his critical approach, arguing that his goal is not to insult learned men but to protect students from errors held under the weight of authority. He emphasizes his sincerity and his willingness to correct mistakes, unlike his stubborn opponents.</summary>
<keywords>literary criticism, conscience, authority, Cluvienus, stylus, truth, education</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface or introduction to a collection of "Miscellanies." The author is establishing his literary lineage and justifying the fragmented, diverse nature of the work by citing classical and early Christian precedents.</meta>

-># OF MISCELLANIES<-

...and I write not continuously, but by leaps and bounds, and by plucking bits here and there; so far from being sorry for this, I have specifically sought no title other than that of *Miscellanies*. In these, I follow the Greek author Aelian <margin>Aelian</margin> <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD), a Roman teacher and rhetorician who wrote in Greek.</note> and the Latin author Gellius <margin>Gellius</margin> <note>Aulus Gellius (c. 125 – after 180 AD), author of the "Attic Nights," a famous collection of diverse notes on grammar, philosophy, and history.</note>, whose books are more charming <margin>Variety is charming.</margin> for their variety than for their strict order. Indeed, even the commentaries of Clement of Alexandria <margin>Clement of Alexandria</margin>, that pontiff and apostolic man, were titled *Stromateis* <note>original Greek: Στρωματεῖς, meaning "Patchwork" or "Miscellanies"</note>—which you might call "painted tapestries"—and they certainly suggested to us no title other than this very variety. Furthermore, I will remain silent regarding the commentaries of Aristoxenus <note>A Greek philosopher and pupil of Aristotle, primarily known for his work on music and rhythm.</note>, which Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher.</note> cites with a nearly identical title in that volume he composed on the *Harmonics* of Ptolemy. Finally, if variety itself—the expeller of boredom and the stimulant of reading—is to be blamed in these *Miscellanies*, then the nature of things <note>The author refers here to *Rerum Natura*, the natural order of the universe.</note> could be rebuked at the same time; I profess myself to be a disciple of such diversity. 

But if these writings descend to every small detail, it is permitted to do this by the example of the Romans: Caesar, Varro, Messala, Cicero, Pliny, Quintilian, and others like them. To emulate them, even in error, is honorable. Therefore, what was fitting for those "patricians" <term>Patricians: The high-ranking ruling class of ancient Rome; used here to mean the prestigious master-authors of antiquity.</term> should not be unbecoming for me, a man of the "lesser families" <term>Lesser families: *Minorum gentium*, referring to those of lower social or intellectual rank compared to the greats.</term>. An equal fame of names either inserts Greek phrases or interprets them as convenient, now according to the sense, and now word-for-word, both of which I do here. Now, if any part seems insufficiently explained, or perhaps even too harsh...
                                                                      A  ij

<summary>The author defends the disorganized and varied structure of his "Miscellanies," arguing that such diversity prevents boredom and follows the tradition of esteemed classical and ecclesiastical scholars.</summary>
<keywords>Miscellanies, Aulus Gellius, Aelian, Clement of Alexandria, variety, classical tradition, literary style, Stromateis</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the continuation of a preface (Praefatio) to a scholarly or literary work from the Renaissance period. The author is defending his prose style, specifically his use of rare or archaic vocabulary.</meta>

->**PREFACE**<-

...and [if things] appear obscure, certainly that person is neither of a sufficiently vigorous mind, nor of solid and faithful learning. If any barbaric and obsolete terms are detected anywhere, we have indeed scattered them intentionally, even to please the unrefined and unlearned, so that the book might become more saleable by scraping together a bit of favor from all sides. If any should say that the words found here are more remote than can be believed to be taken from common usage, I suspect they will change their opinion once they have advanced right to the middle of your reading. For there are not lacking those for whom even (as Marcus Tullius <note>original: "M. Tullius"; referring to the famous Roman orator Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note> says) an alabaster jar <term>alabaster jar (alabastrus)</term> full of ointment seems to stink. 

Furthermore, in this type of writing, which does not hawk itself to the common people but is prepared for only a few, the use of such hidden equipment <note>The author uses the metaphor of "equipment" or "furniture" (supellectilis) to refer to his specialized vocabulary.</note>, especially when modest, is by no means disapproved of by good men. For it should not be a disgrace to renew things that have almost faded away, provided that these very things 

<margin>To grow old/decay</margin> 
<note>original: "Vetustesco"; refers to the process of becoming old in a sense of wearing out.</note> 

might not just grow old, but 

<margin>To grow old/mature</margin> 
<note>original: "Veterasco"; refers to becoming established or seasoned with age.</note> 

grow seasoned anew. If some diversions of speech, perhaps less known to a man who has read only ten pages of Cicero and nothing else, should occasionally offend anyone, I certainly appeal from him to the learned, who, I believe, will especially praise what he disapproves of. Nothing, perhaps, is more intolerable than for an unlearned man to pass judgment on you, while he yet seems to himself most learned. If someone, where something is refuted, desires a mass of either authorities or reasons, let him know that victory [is sought] there by us...

<summary>In this preface, the author defends his use of archaic and specialized vocabulary, arguing that sophisticated readers will appreciate his effort to revive "faded" words rather than sticking strictly to a narrow Ciceronian style.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Latin style, Cicero, vocabulary, linguistics, scholarship, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page appears to be from the introductory section of a 16th-century scholarly work, likely a collection of "Miscellanea." It follows a discussion on the varied nature of the text, justifying its lack of a singular, rigid structure.</meta>

-># OF MISCELLANIES.<-

...that the satisfaction of victory is not sought. If some chapters are thought a bit long, and others again quite short, let us believe this also to be the law of this new work: that it contains nothing uniform, is nowhere equal to itself, always limps along with variety, and observes this one rule—that it brings nothing back to a strict rule, nor to the plumb line or the mason’s level. <note>The author is using architectural metaphors to argue that literature does not need to be perfectly symmetrical or "level" to be valid.</note> If a freer style is judged to be of an arrogant nature, perhaps a kinder interpreter will attribute it instead to a simplicity which is not unbecoming to noble-minded men. Furthermore, wherever custom opposes us <margin>Custom.</margin>, then Truth, which does not deal in trifles, or a better custom acting as Truth’s deputy, will defend us. For that which is accepted against authority is said to be "taken from custom," and the "error of custom" is often criticized by the learned. As a certain author beautifully says: "Custom without truth is the antiquity of error." <note>original: "Consuetudo sine ueritate, uetustas erroris est." This is a famous maxim by Saint Cyprian of Carthage, often used to argue that long-standing tradition is no excuse for persisting in a mistake.</note> Finally, if we have occasionally sprinkled in a few things that are thorny and anxious, or—to be more accurate—somewhat tart, drawn either from philosophy (of which we have long been a student) or from that circle of disciplines <term>the liberal arts; the "encyclopedia" of knowledge</term> which serve the study of wisdom, yet these things will perhaps refresh and sharpen the reader’s palate, which might otherwise grow weary from too many sweets. For in order that all these things might harmonize through their variety and be joined together as if by a certain contrast, we have run almost "from the lime-kiln into the coal-mine" (as the saying goes) <margin>Proverb.</margin> <note>original: "de calcaria in carbonariam." A Latin proverb used to describe moving from one extreme to another, or from a "white" place to a "black" one.</note> and we have taken care that opposites should follow one another in turn, so that the complaint of that...
                                                                      A iij

<summary>In this preface, the author defends the irregular structure and "tart" scholarly content of his miscellany, arguing that variety and truth are more important than rigid literary tradition.</summary>

->PREFACE<-

modes might be removed. For we do not allow ourselves to be lacking in things that are more pleasant and delightful—I might even say seductive—rather than merely useful or necessary, <margin>Man. Humanity.</margin> since it is true (as Varro says <note>original: "ut ait Varro"; referring to Marcus Terentius Varro, the Roman scholar who wrote extensively on the Latin language and agriculture.</note>) that what suffices for a man is one thing, and what suffices for humanity is another, even without immediate profit. Meanwhile, beautiful things are sought after no less, by Hercules, than profit without beauty.

Therefore, just as some unpolished and rough things—hewn more by the axe than smoothed by the plane, and having felt neither the file, nor the joiner's tools, nor the scrapers—will delight those rustic and goat-like <note>original: "hircosos"; a term implying a coarse, unrefined nature, often jokingly associated with the smell of goats.</note> men, so on the other hand, a style that is occasionally vermiculated <term>vermiculated: derived from "vermiculus" (little worm), it refers to an intricate, mosaic-like style of writing or decoration that twists and turns.</term> and variegated with multi-colored tiles will captivate those more delicate sorts—the plucked and pumice-smoothed ones—lest by the combined voices from both sides, I am either raised to the heavens by equal applause or cast down to the ground by hisses. 

But for those who fall between these extremes and are marked by neither, a certain "Cinnus" <term>cinnus: a mixed drink or "medley"; used here by the author (citing Cicero) to describe a stylistic potpourri that blends different influences.</term> of various elements (as Cicero says) will suffice—not excelling in any one single thing, yet sharing in all. For not everyone has the same taste; each has his own palate. Indeed, I believe some will spit out a dish at a banquet as if it were distasteful or meager, only to soon lick it up greedily in the kitchen as if it were sweet and rich. 

We have certainly prepared these things not for the forum or the senate-house, but for the bedroom and the school. For that reason, we have striven—though I know not if we have achieved it—that if anything is here, it should be of an entirely easy and simple elegance, not a collection of laborious and painted allurements. It should possess a color and brilliance that is true and natural, not acquired or borrowed from—

->MISCELLANIES<-

...a suitable and ready appearance, neither burdensome nor excessive. Finally, in whatever way these works go out into the world, and however they are received, <margin>Of whatever kind.</margin> we certainly do not object. Yet, I do not think we shall escape that fate common to all who write—the risk of being spoken ill of—provided it pleases the readers.

Indeed, we expose ourselves to this gamble <note>original: "aleae," literally a game of dice</note> and seek no protection, having voluntarily waived our right to defense against criticism. Let judgment be passed against us; let us be slandered and harassed, rather than allow the public deception regarding literature to continue. To us, the honor of letters is more important than our own personal interests, even if it must be bought at the cost of our reputation or our leisure. For I ask you, what is the purpose of this profession of shared studies if we have reached a point where we allow the duty we owe to universal learning to fail because of some base suspicion? 

In what way can the sincerity of honorable literature endure or even exist if criticism is entirely abandoned, while we all equally dread the sting of envy? <margin>Envy.</margin> Although (as far as I am concerned) I wish my labor were met with contempt rather than envy. For that is almost my prayer, if indeed what Livy says is true: *Envy, like fire, seeks the highest places.* <note>The author quotes the Roman historian Livy (original: "Invidia velut ignis alta petit") to suggest that only great or prominent works attract jealousy.</note>

Come then, you finest cultivators of study, let each of you be eager for your part. Tear up this invincible weed by which the good fruits of humanity <note>term: "humanitatis," referring to the "studia humanitatis" or the humanities, the core of Renaissance education.</note> are choked. Pluck those things growing beneath the sacred stems like most destructive ivy, which [steal] all their sap...

A iiii

### PREFACE

...swallowing up erratic <note>original: *erraticis*; likely the end of a sentence from the previous page warning against unstable or wandering ideas</note> notions, break them off utterly and cut them down. There is no lack of those who corrupt the truth; let there also be no lack of those who restore it. I ask that some protector might arise—an avenger and patron for the endangered disciplines. For I, as one from the common crowd, will certainly test what freedom is permitted to me. Whether or not I achieve something worthwhile <note>original: *operaeprecium*; a Latin idiom meaning "worth the effort"</note>, I shall see later.

Wherever this work may flow, or however the matter falls out, I will take it in good part. I will not only swallow the poison of all malice and detraction, but—given the strength of my stomach—I will even digest it. I am certain that this is the very nature <note>original: *genium*; referring to the inherent character or guiding spirit of a thing</note> of truth: as soon as it appears in public, it is hateful and hostile to the common crowd. 

Therefore, although others may turn a blind eye and mutter <margin>They mutter; they do not dare to mumble openly.</margin> about telling the truth, I alone surely—whatever the outcome—will not hide my judgment. I will not suppress what I feel, nor will I further indulge such things with patience. Instead, I will whisper to this little book of mine, at least with some degree of freedom, in this way:

I have seen—I myself have seen, my little book <margin>From Persius <note>Persius was a 1st-century Roman satirist who wrote about the corruption of literature and morals.</note></margin>, and daily I see—many capital crimes committed in literature. Others' works are stealthily plundered; things are fabricated according to whim; whatever is convenient is ascribed even to reputable authors who would not even recognize the work; authors are alleged who do not even exist; furthermore, manuscripts are cited as ancient that appear nowhere at all. Every book is filled with the most laborious vanities. Falsehoods are substituted for truths, adopted things for native ones, and novelties for the established. Everything is substituted, polluted, adulterated, smeared over, encrusted, distorted, confused, thrown headlong, and diverted—with no good faith, no...

<summary>In this preface, the author laments the widespread corruption and forgery in contemporary scholarship and literature, vowing to speak the truth despite public hostility. He portrays himself as a lone defender of intellectual integrity against those who "plunder" and "adulterate" the works of others.</summary>

# MISCELLANIES

->PREFACE<-

...[published], and more from the spur of the moment than from painstaking care, although he manages the business of style with almost an abundance of effort and polishes his elocution—a man whom venerable truth and loyalty especially concern. Indeed, lest idle men <note>original: "maleferiati," referring to those who misuse their leisure time or act with ill intent</note> think that we have drawn these matters from the dregs, or that we have overstepped the boundaries of the grammarians <note>the "lines of the grammarians" refers to the established rules of language and scholarly discipline</note>, we have—following the immediate example of Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, in his *Natural History*, was famous for listing his sources at the beginning of his work, a precursor to the modern bibliography</note>—prefixed the names of the authors. However, we have chosen only those who are honorable and ancient, from whom these matters derive their legal standing and from whom we have borrowed <note>original: "versuram fecimus," a metaphor for taking out a loan, suggesting the author is "indebted" to these sources</note>. We have not included those whom others merely cite while their own works have perished through the ages, but rather those whose treasures we ourselves have handled and through whose writings we have traveled. 

Moreover, the antiquity of the manuscripts, the reliability of the coins, and the ancient remains carved in bronze or marble—which you, Laurentius <note>Likely Lorenzo de' Medici or another contemporary patron of the arts and collector of antiquities</note>, provided to us—greatly support our commentaries, in addition to the variety of books. Furthermore, to demonstrate by example that the tricks and frauds of charlatans <term>circulatorum: literally "those who form a circle," referring to street performers or quacks who peddle false information</term> must be abolished, and to restrain the license of those whose habit of straying aimlessly from the prescribed path is now spreading widely like a cancer, we have never failed to cite the testimony of ancient writing. This ensures that no spurious or superimposed addition might prevail, but that we represent everything in its true appearance, providing those marks through which the authority can be investigated thoroughly, right back to its root. And so that no [aspects] of this task of ours...

...if appendices are desired, we have also gathered the very headings of the matters <margin>Appendix</margin> <note>The author refers to supplementary material or summaries added to clarify the main text.</note>, enclosed in the briefest titles, yet in which the usefulness is greater than their small number. <meta>This page continues the author's defense of humanistic studies (classical literature and philosophy) against critics, likely following an introductory dedication to a patron.</meta>

# PREFACE

->...not only a clearer day, but they cannot even endure a faint shadow.<-

For no one doubts those whom the moth of spite consumes, whom the rust of envy gnaws away; those who turn another’s glory into their own punishment and another's happiness into their own torture; who waste away at the progress of others, and whom malevolence—sickening at the success of others—constantly tortures like an internal executioner. 

But even those "pulpit censors" (and I am not speaking now of the learned and the good) are often accustomed to prod us and make a noise against these studies. Indeed, full of emptiness, they defame the "more humane letters" <note>original: "literas humaniores" — a term for the study of classical literature, grammar, and rhetoric, which later became known as the humanities.</note> before the ignorant common folk, puffing out their cheeks in what I might almost call a ritual. With a coarse and fierce rusticity—which is the only thing they regard as holiness (as Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the translator of the Vulgate Bible, who often wrote about the tension between classical learning and Christian devotion.</note> says)—they rage against these studies and those who pursue them. With bombastic proclamations, gnashing their teeth and foaming at the mouth, they act so aggressively that they easily reveal that they even disapprove of, and almost hate, those truly holy souls: the Greeks, Basil, Chrysostom, and the two Gregorys <note>Referring to the "Great Cappadocian" Church Fathers: Basil the Great, Gregory of Nazianzus, and Gregory of Nyssa, who were famous for their Greek eloquence.</note>; and even the Latins, Cyprian, Ambrose, Augustine, and Jerome himself, and other leaders of our religion of that kind, who were laden with the disciplines of all nations and languages, just like the "wealth of the Egyptians." <note>A common metaphor in the Renaissance used to justify reading "pagan" Greek and Roman authors; it refers to the Israelites taking Egyptian gold during the Exodus to build the Tabernacle of God.</note>

<margin>Fierce little ones.</margin>

Because of this, your beloved Mariano of Gennazzano <note>Fra Mariano da Genazzano (1450–1498), a famous Augustinian preacher in Florence known for his polished, poetic style, often contrasted with the fiery, apocalyptic style of Savonarola.</note> seems all the more admirable to me. He is second to none in theology, and of all those we have heard preaching in the church, he is not only the most prudent but also the most eloquent...

<summary>The author defends the study of classical humanities against narrow-minded "pulpit censors" by arguing that the great Church Fathers themselves used secular learning. He introduces the preacher Mariano of Gennazzano as a modern example of this ideal combination of faith and eloquence.</summary>

->MISCELLANIES.<-

...eloquence is neither suspected by people striving for virtue <note>original: "ad bonam frugem"; literally "toward good fruit," a Latin idiom for moral improvement</note>, since it is recommended by an incredible severity of life, nor on the other hand is it a gloomy or formidable austerity, since it does not spurn poetic charms and that beautiful variety, brilliance, and delight of letters. Just as wool, intended to truly drink in the purple dye, is first treated with certain preparations, so I believe minds should be refined by such letters and teachings (as Cicero says in his *Hortensius* <note>A now-lost dialogue by Cicero that famously inspired St. Augustine to study philosophy</note>) and should be steeped and prepared to receive wisdom. 

Yet, I will not be reluctant because of those people either to sharpen with my pen whatever this work of my late-night studies amounts to, or to test what may be allowed against those <margin>He imitates Juvenal</margin> who conceive capital frauds in the business of literature. For I too forgive Philoxenus <note>A Greek poet who famously preferred to return to the stone quarries (prison) rather than praise the bad poetry of the tyrant Dionysius</note>, just as Cicero did when writing to Atticus, for preferring to return to prison rather than surrender his judgment; especially since it is fitting for writers to serve not the present age, but eternity itself, as Thucydides also said. 

And for that reason, I think books themselves are called *monuments* <margin>Why books are monuments.</margin>, because they ought to look more toward the memory of posterity—which will judge more incorruptibly—than toward the foolish little favors of the present time. Therefore, so that this wickedness of those who corrupt everything far and wide may—if possible—be not only extinguished but even crushed, and so that such a worn and prostituted shamelessness <note>The author uses the Latin "frons" (forehead/brow), which for Romans was the seat of shame; a "worn" brow implies one that can no longer blush</note> and such hardness of face may not become an example, I now [present] the following centuries <note>The "Centuries" are the 100 miscellaneous philological observations that make up this book</note>...

<summary>In this preface, Poliziano defends the value of a broad literary education as a preparation for wisdom and asserts the writer's duty to prioritize the judgment of posterity over contemporary popularity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Preface (Praefatio), specifically the author's defense of his intellectual labor and his dedication of the work to Lorenzo de' Medici.</meta>

->## PREFACE<-

Having undertaken, I believe, a beneficial attempt, we by no means promise that this first taste will be the final word. <note>original: "scepto (arbitror) salutari conatu"</note> Those subjects we now handle with a soft touch, which we reach for with a light and slow arm, we will later, I think, press and urge more strongly; and content for a little while in the meantime with preliminary skirmishes <note>original: "praelusoria, uelitarique pugna," referring to the *velites*, light-armed infantry who fought before the main battle line</note> and light combat, we will soon collect our strength and do battle with the full power of the army. Furthermore—since "the shirt is closer than the cloak" <note>original: "tunica propior pallio," a common Latin proverb meaning one's own interests or family must come first</note>—we will boldly and diligently reclaim our own works from plagiarists and petty thieves, and we will lay a legal hand upon them in a cause for freedom. Indeed, we wish for those things to be accepted as ours now—things which we ourselves have produced as leaders with great labor and industry. We will not allow them to be coaxed or twisted from us as long as it is permitted; they are, I say, our own—not our rejects, but our tokens <term>pignora: literally "pledges," but often used affectionately for children or one's most precious works</term>. For while we leave these works in the midst of the crowd, like missiles to be snatched up and trampled, we claim and defend those others for ourselves; we put these on display; we acknowledge and take up those others. Yet we do this in such a way that we do not wish to imitate the apes <margin>The love of monkeys for their young.</margin> who are said to admire their own offspring, however deformed, as if they were the most beautiful. Nor will these studies of ours, I think, though they are works of lighter effort—or more truly, playthings—be a disgrace to you, **Lorenzo de' Medici**, to whom they are now inscribed. They are inscribed, however, not so much to prove myself grateful for your favors or to repay my gratitude—because they had you as a helper and a counselor—but rather so that they might proceed under good omens, and that in them your...

<summary>In this preface, the author describes his current work as a preliminary "skirmish" before a more serious effort, defends his intellectual property against plagiarists, and dedicates the text to Lorenzo de' Medici.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory dedication of the "Miscellanies," likely addressed to Lorenzo de' Medici, concluding the preface by situating the work within the broader Florentine intellectual revival.</meta>

### MISCELLANIES

...let his memory be celebrated, so that the book, gaining authority from it, may be commended by the renown of a great name. Then, since the whole sum of my studies is owed to him, this very portion shall also rightly begin with him. And by Hercules, let these writings be like dessert <note>original: "bellaria" — sweetmeats or delicacies served at the end of a meal; the author is modestly suggesting his work is a light "treat" compared to heavy philosophy.</note>, received at the second course. For those weightier matters occupy the place and appearance of a proper dinner—the kind of works which either Marsilio Ficino <margin>Ficino.</margin> <note>A celebrated priest and philosopher who led the Platonic Academy in Florence.</note> (regarding Plato) or the Byzantine Argyropylus <margin>Argyropylus.</margin> <note>Johannes Argyropoulos, a Greek scholar who fled to Italy and was instrumental in teaching the Greek language and Aristotle’s philosophy.</note> (the interpreter of Aristotle) have gathered from the storehouse of philosophy. 

Regarding that man <margin>Pico della Mirandola.</margin> who is to be admired above all others—whom I no longer call Pico, but rather (as I am accustomed to call him) a **Phoenix** <note>original: "phoenice" — the mythical bird that is reborn from its own ashes, used here to describe Pico as a unique, once-in-a-generation genius.</note>, who now builds his nest in your laurel <note>The "laurel" is a pun on the name of the patron, Lorenzo de' Medici (Laurentius), whose name is derived from the laurel tree.</note>—my expectation is indeed so great that I would dare to echo the poet Propertius: ***"Yield, you Roman writers; yield, you Greeks!"*** <note>original: "Cedite Romani scriptores, cedite Graij" — a famous quote from the Roman poet Propertius, originally used to signal that a new work was so great it would surpass all previous literature.</note> 

Farewell, my most pleasant patron, and (as you are already doing) continue to favor the learned, continue to awaken literature, and continue to rescue Latin culture from neglect and Greek culture from destruction. In doing so, the memory of your name shall be fully protected from the injustice of oblivion.

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, the author praises the intellectual giants of the Florentine Renaissance—Ficino, Argyropylus, and Pico della Mirandola—while urging his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici, to continue his support of classical letters.</summary>

There are also other ancient wise men cited, whose authority is unknown, such as the writer of the *Poëmenicon* <note>original: "Poëmenicon"; likely referring to 'The Shepherd' of Hermas, an early Christian document</note>, and the commentators on Aristophanes, Xenophon, Aristides, Euripides, Callimachus, and Synesius. Finally, even the "barbarian" <note>In the classical sense, this refers to non-Greco-Roman cultures, specifically Hebrew and Chaldean scholars</note> Hebrew and Chaldean authors are not without their own contributions <term>asymboli</term> <note>original: "asymboli"; a Greek-derived term meaning 'without a contribution' or 'paying no share'—here meaning they have contributed significantly to the study of origins</note> regarding their own origins.

->**CHAPTERS OF THE FIRST CENTURY**<-
<note>A "Century" was a common Renaissance format for organizing scholarly or scientific essays into groups of one hundred.</note>

**CHAPTER I.**
Cicero is defended against slander regarding his explanation of a new word found in Aristotle, which is <term>Endelechia</term> <note>original: "Endelechia"; a term (ἐνδελέχεια) referring to continuity or duration, which Cicero famously discussed in relation to the immortality of the soul, sometimes confused with 'entelechy'.</note>.

**CHAPTER II.**
Regarding the "Carpathian slippers" <note>original: "crepidæ Carpatinæ"</note> mentioned in the works of Catullus; proving that this is the correctly written term and is wrongly altered by others.

**CHAPTER III.**
The words by which Horace indicated the <term>Camelopard</term> <note>The giraffe</note>, the appearance of that animal’s face, the various names by which it is known, and the first time it was seen in Italy.

and diffusions: And therein
something about the **Florentine Pandects**.
<note>original: "Pandectis Florentinis"; the famous 6th-century manuscript of Justinian's Digest held in Florence, central to Renaissance legal studies.</note>
      Chapter XLII.
That thumbs were pressed to show favor,
just as they were accustomed to be turned
when denying favor; and from this,
the meanings of **Horace**, **Juvenal**, 
and **Prudentius** are
explained.
      Chapter XLIII.
How many diphthongs the 
ancients possessed.
      Chapter XLIIII.
That one ought to read "**Pegasean nectar**"
in **Persius**, not "**song**."
<note>original: "Pegaseium nectar... nõ melos"; a correction of Persius, Prologue 14.</note>
      Chapter XLV.
That **Achilles** was younger than **Patroclus**,
contrary to what either **Aeschylus** 
recorded, or is commonly 
believed.
      Chapter XLVI.
The word **Cacoëthes** <term>a malignant habit or "itch," from the Greek kakoēthes</term> in **Juvenal**
is four syllables, not **cacoëthos**.

[Column 2]

And that one should read "**Lyde with her box stored away**," 
not "**unrefined**." At the same 
time, a passage in **Martianus** 
[Capella] is explained, I think, 
not carelessly.
      Chapter XLVII.
What **Pliny** recorded regarding the 
"hanging" titles of ancient artists,
which are recognized also from 
ancient monuments in Rome, 
as well as the appearance of 
the **Virgilian aegis**.
<note>Pliny noted that great artists used "hanging" or incomplete signatures (e.g., "was making" rather than "made") to show modesty.</note>
      Chapter XLVIII.
That others wrote about **Megabyzus** 
what **Pliny** wrote about **Alexander**: 
that he used to frequent **Apelles'** 
workshop.
<note>Refers to the anecdote where the painter Apelles tells a powerful visitor to stay silent so the apprentices don't laugh at his ignorant comments on art.</note>
      Chapter XLIX.
A comparison of the epigrams 
of the Greek **Posidippus** and the 
Latin **Ausonius** regarding the image 
of **Opportunity**, along with a 
most beautiful description <term>ecphrasis</term> 
by the Greek **Callistratus**.
<note>The image of "Opportunity" (Occasio/Kairos) was famously depicted with hair over her face but bald at the back, meaning she must be seized as she approaches.</note>

...lacking a name, and so he calls the soul itself *Endelecheia* <note>original: "Endelechian"</note> by a new name, as if it were a certain continuous <margin>continuity <note>original: ἐνδελε-</note></margin> and perennial motion. <margin>...cheia <note>original: χεια.</note></margin> But indeed, there is no one (he used to say) who is even slightly familiar with the reading of Aristotle who does not know that *entelecheia* <margin>perfection <note>original: ἐντελέ-</note></margin> is rather the Aristotelian word, by no means signifying what Cicero thinks—continuous <margin>...cheia <note>original: χεια.</note></margin> and perennial motion—but rather perfection, or a certain consummation. For whatever a thing is, it exists chiefly because of this *entelecheia*. Cicero, however, was deceived by the trick of similarity and explained not this Aristotelian word, but another one entirely adjacent and neighboring to it. For *endelechos* <note>original: ἐνδελεχῶς</note> means "continually" or "movably," and continuous mobility is *endelechismos*; <margin>continually <note>original: ἐνδελεχῶς</note></margin> it was from this that he thought Aristotle's *entelecheia* was derived, though the latter has not the letter *d* but rather a *t* in its second syllable. <margin>continuous mobility <note>original: ἐνδελεχϊσμός</note></margin> He added that the authorities standing against Cicero were as great as any <term>Peripatetic</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy, named after the "peripatos" or covered walkway where they walked and talked.</note> thinkers you please. ...had persuaded his followers, so much so that—though it is almost a sacrilege even to say—it was accepted as a given among us that Marcus Tullius <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the famous Roman orator and statesman.</note> neither understood philosophy nor Greek literature. Furthermore, he excelled with such sharpness in disputation that the defense of all truth seems rightly to rest upon this one man most of all. 

Let us consider, therefore, what these individuals at least thought of Cicero and how much they attributed to him in philosophy; then let us boldly believe that it is not for just anyone to stir up slander against a man who has been so singularly praised by the most highly esteemed men. Thus, Boethius not only repeatedly uses the testimonies of Marcus Tullius [Cicero] such that he presents them as the most firm and inviolable arguments, but he also does not blush to undertake an interpretation <note>Specifically, Boethius wrote a famous commentary on Cicero's *Topica* (*Topics*).</note> of Cicero’s own *Topics*—which is the greatest honor.

# MISCELLANY, Page 4

...an enemy to truth is he who, if he merely catches the scent of a few scattered and almost hurried Greek words even in [Cicero’s] own letters, would say that he ever found anything more elegant or more Attic <note>Attic refers to the refined, pure style of speech associated with Athens.</note> in all of Greece itself. This is proven in their terms for **melancholy** and **madness**, or likewise in the fact that they use the word **toil** <margin>toil <note>original: πόνος (ponos)</note></margin> too confusedly for both labor and pain. He points out that this is found in many places, and that the Greeks even lack a name for certain things with which they are most familiar...

->**B O O K**<-

<margin>Inept term</margin> ...nor has any of them, however much they tortured themselves by inquiring, <margin>term.</margin> been able to find or devise an inept vocabulary word. Indeed, is it not said that even that <margin>Apollonius Molo regarding Cicero.</margin> Apollonius Molo—the most celebrated of all rhetoricians of his time—having heard Marcus Tullius [Cicero] speak once in Greek, and an extemporaneous speech at that, stood fixed for a long time, and finally pronounced this in wonder? "I indeed both praise and admire you, Cicero, but I pity and am weary of the fortune of the Greeks, because the ornaments of culture <note>original: "humanitatis", referring to the liberal arts and refined conduct.</note> and eloquence, which were the only things we had left for ourselves, are now also—as far as I understand—claimed by the Romans through you." In truth, this judgment of him was recorded not by some Latin author, nor some inept person, but by Plutarch himself, a man of Greek talent and Roman gravity in literature. Therefore, in this very manner, the ancients have left us testimony regarding his knowledge of the science itself and his skill in the Greek language.

Come now, let us also weigh that <margin>Entelechy</margin> "entelechy" <note>Cicero famously transliterated the Greek term *entelecheia* (perfection/actuality) as *endelecheia* (continuity), a point of great debate among later scholars.</note>, the late slanderer of Cicero. Is he therefore being criticized here because he said Aristotle had introduced a certain fifth nature <note>The "quintessence," a heavenly substance distinct from earth, water, air, and fire.</note> beyond the elements, and thus called the soul itself by a new name? This is what Alexander of Aphrodisias <note>A renowned commentator on Aristotle who lived around 200 AD.</note>, most highly praised among all the Peripatetics <note>The "Walkers," a name for followers of Aristotle’s school.</note> even by Averroes <note>The 12th-century Islamic philosopher from Al-Andalus, known as "The Commentator" on Aristotle.</note>, showed in those exceedingly subtle books of *Natural Questions* which Saint Jerome boasted to have read, and Porphyry in the commentary which he wrote on Aristotle's ten categories <note>referring to the "Categories," Aristotle's work on the classification of all things.</note>...

<summary>This page defends Cicero's mastery of Greek culture and language, citing the praise of the rhetorician Apollonius Molo and Plutarch. It also begins a technical defense of Cicero's interpretation of Aristotle's "fifth element" and the term for the soul, "entelechy."</summary>

->MISCELLANIES.                                 5<-

...published questions and answers, and Themistius <note>A 4th-century philosopher known for his paraphrases of Aristotle.</note> [wrote] upon these in his paraphrase of the second book *On the Soul*, and Simplicius <note>A 6th-century Neoplatonist who wrote extensive commentaries on Aristotle.</note> where he interprets the Aristotelian volume of the same argument, and Aristides Quintilianus <note>An ancient Greek author of a famous treatise on music.</note> in the third book *On Music*, and finally, in a certain letter of his, Saint Ambrose <note>The 4th-century Bishop of Milan.</note> (to pass over others for now) have almost all indicated with one voice. And yet, regarding this point, there is no doubt even in the very words of Aristotle himself. 

Or is it rather blamed because he neither noted the word <term>endelechia</term> <note>original: *endelechiae*; a term meaning "continuity" or "persistence," often confused in the Renaissance with Aristotle's "entelecheia."</note> with its own letters, nor assigned it a meaning other than the one Aristotle intended (as we said above)? 

First, therefore, I ask of those critics: by what argument specifically do they conclude that <term>entelechia</term> <note>original: *entelechian*; from the Greek "entelecheia," meaning "actuality" or "completion."</note> rather than <term>endelechia</term> <note>original: *endelechian*</note> was written by Aristotle? For this new word is just as much a novelty as that one; the soul can be signified by the one no less than by the other, nor does Aristotle himself announce that "perfection" <note>original: *perfectionem*; the standard definition of entelecheia.</note> rather than that "continuous motion" <note>original: *motionem illam... continuam*; the definition associated with endelecheia.</note> is indicated by the new term. Let Porphyry, Themistius, Simplicius, or any of their peers have believed so...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the history and transmission of Aristotle's library, specifically focusing on the period after Neleus of Scepsis and the recovery of the texts by Apellicon and later Roman figures.</meta>

<margin>Neleus of Scepsis</margin> ...[Apellicon] himself, being more a devotee of books than of philosophy, and having dared to "correct" them according to his own judgment (or so he certainly thought), defiled the texts with numerous errors. Nevertheless, these works lay hidden in Athens until the time of Sulla (as Plutarch records). For the early Peripatetics <note>The Peripatetics were followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy, named for the "peripatos" or covered walkways of the Lyceum where they met.</note> lacked those standard works by which an entire philosophical system is built; they possessed only a few of the works they call <margin>Exoterics <note>original Greek: ἐξωτερικοί; "external" or "public" works</note> (External/Public Works)</margin> "Exoterics." Consequently, the later members of the school were able to philosophize more precisely because they were finally reading through the actual volumes of Aristotle (even if they were corrupted in certain places). 

<margin>Apellicon of Teos</margin> Indeed, Lucius Sulla <margin>L. (Lucius)</margin> was the first to transfer that library of Apellicon, which had been discovered in Athens, to Rome. After him, the grammarian Tyrannion—and almost his equal, Andronicus of Rhodes—dealt with them. It is this Andronicus who even denies that the books *On Interpretation* <note>original Latin: *Peri hermenias* (from the Greek title *De Interpretatione*), one of Aristotle's primary works on logic and language.</note> are truly by Aristotle, [having obtained] a great abundance of books...

->MISCELLANEA 6<-

...his philosopher’s books <note>Likely referring to Aristotle</note> were even made public by certain editors in various tables. Therefore, there is no reason for us to doubt the primeval purity of that reading, especially since the difference is so slight. Either we may hold a free suspicion in either direction, or (if we must move toward one or the other) it is safer to prefer Cicero. He lived long before Alexander <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias (active c. 200 AD), a highly influential commentator on Aristotle.</note>, who was the first of that group we mentioned to explain Aristotle. Alexander flourished during the time of the Emperor Severus, as his own work *On Fate* <note>original: "de fato"</note> indicates; he was almost a contemporary—or perhaps slightly older—than those greatest Academics, Origen and Plotinus. 

What, then, prevents Cicero himself from being able to see the source <margin>The source</margin> <note>original: "matricem"</note> of Aristotle's books which were published in his own age? Even if they were not entirely uncorrupted, they were certainly (as we have said) annotated <margin>annotated.</margin> <note>original: "conscribellatam," implying a text marked with notes or revisions.</note>. Furthermore, I would believe it was done deliberately by that most learned man [Cicero]; shielded by the free wall of his heart, he dared to justly adapt that interpretation to this new Aristotelian term. This interpretation agreed with Plato’s opinion in the *Phaedrus* concerning the eternal and—as Varro says—"divine" motion of the soul. Cicero himself mentioned this either in his *Tusculan Disputations* or in the sixth volume of the *Republic*. Indeed, Philoponus in his *Life of Aristotle*, Simplicius in his *Commentaries on the Soul*, and Boethius himself in the final book of *On Interpretation* <note>original: "Peri hermenias"</note> all assert that the opinions of both philosophers [Plato and Aristotle] are kindred and comparable; even...

<margin>The harmony of Plato and Aristotle.</margin>

It is recorded that Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) known for his "Introduction" to logic and his attempts to reconcile different philosophical traditions.</note> composed seven books in which this argument was principally gathered: that the **school of thought** <note>original: *hæresin*. In this period, the term refers to a "sect" or "chosen way of thinking" rather than the modern religious definition of "heresy."</note> of Plato and Aristotle is entirely the same. My friend Pico della Mirandola <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a brilliant Renaissance scholar who famously attempted to synthesize all known philosophy and religion.</note> also touched upon this in the preface of one of his disputations. Indeed—unless my own judgment fails me—he will prove it to be "truer than truth" in a most voluminous and (I believe) most beautiful work which he is laboring over and forging day and night regarding this very harmony, as we call it, between Plato and Aristotle.

Thus, it will be understood that our Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator whose Latin translations of Greek philosophy were often criticized by later scholars.</note> not only did not err in using that particular word, but perhaps wrote even more eruditely than others, or at least more appropriately for his own school. Consequently, what the authors promised concerning the doctrine of so famous a man—as suitable as those we have shown above—he himself will most generously and abundantly provide, even in that specific turning point where he especially suffers from **slander** <note>original: *calumniam*. This suggests Cicero was being criticized for his choice of technical vocabulary or his interpretation of Greek concepts.</note>...

MISCELLANIES. 7

->ON WHAT THE **CARPATINE** SANDALS ARE<-
->in Catullus; a word that, although correctly written, is wrongly changed.<-

### CHAPTER II.

Valerius Catullus <note>Gaius Valerius Catullus (c. 84–54 BC), a Roman poet known for his influential Neoteric style.</note> says the following in a certain epigram:
*Yet with that tongue of yours, should the occasion arise, you could*
*lick backsides and **carpatine** sandals.* <note>Original Latin: "Ista tamen lingua, si usus ueniat tibi, possis / Culos & crepidas lingere carpatinas." From Catullus, Poem 98, a poem mocking a man with a foul mouth.</note>

It has indeed been asked by many, but explained as yet by no one, what **carpatine** <margin>Carpatine</margin>—or **carbatine**—sandals are. For either is correct, though they are also called **carbasine**. <margin>Carbasine</margin> <margin>Sandals.</margin> However, certain charlatans and "shadow-dwellers" <margin>Charlatans & impostors.</margin> <note>The author uses the Greek term *planoi* (πλάνοι) and the Latin *tenebriones* to describe dishonest scholars who "work in the shadows."</note> in the world of letters, having erased the ancient word, substitute I know not what—perhaps *Cercopythas* or *Coprotinas*—words dragged out from the pigsty, not the schoolhouse; naked words, hollow names, and sounds of no consequence. From the Greek toolkit—as if from a well-stocked pantry—we shall bring forth authorities that are neither despicable nor of doubtful credit. By these, the original reading may be protected from harm and the fog of interpretation may be cleared away.

First, then, Julius Pollux <note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian whose *Onomasticon* is a major source for ancient Greek vocabulary.</note> himself, in his ninth book dedicated to the Emperor Commodus, says that **Carbatine** <margin>Carbatine</margin> sandals are a rustic shoe, so named after the Carians <note>An ancient people of Asia Minor; the author suggests the shoe originated there.</note>. Aristotle, moreover, in the second book of his *History of Animals*, writes that camels are shod with **carbatine** sandals so that they do not, of course, break down during a long journey with the army.

<summary>This page defends the original wording of a Catullus poem against scholars who wish to change "carpatine" to nonsense words, citing ancient authorities like Julius Pollux and Aristotle to prove it refers to a specific type of rustic footwear.</summary>

<keywords>Catullus, carbatine sandals, carpatine, footwear, Julius Pollux, Aristotle, philology, textual criticism, Carians, ancient shoes</keywords>

# B O O K

<margin>There are four very elegant little books extant in Greek under the title of *Poemenicon* <note>original: "Poemenicōn," likely referring to the "Pastoral Stories" of Longus, known as *Daphnis and Chloe*</note>. In the second of these, an old man is introduced with a pouch and <term>carbatinas</term> <note>a simple, rustic shoe made of a single piece of raw, untanned hide</note>. Lucian also, in the dialogue titled either *Alexander* or *The False Prophet*, mentions certain orators from Paphlagonia wearing these same shoes. Finally, Xenophon the Socratic, in the third book of the *Anabasis*, states that when the soldiers' old shoes had worn out, they wore "carbatinas" made from fresh ox-hides; a passage which Suidas <note>A massive 10th-century Greek encyclopedia and dictionary</note> also cites, though he conceals the name of the original author. Furthermore, a commentator on Xenophon adds that the "carbatina" is a "barbaric footwear."</margin>

"Finally," he says, "the ambassadors of the Axumites approached—not as subjects paying tribute, but as friends and allies. Showing their goodwill regarding his successful deeds, they offered various gifts themselves, but especially a certain animal of an unusual appearance and nature. Its height was that of a camel, and its color and hide were like a panther's, <margin>Variegated.</margin> variegated with spots as if they were blossoming. The hindquarters beneath the belly sat low to the ground, just as we see in lions. The shoulders, the forefeet, and likewise the chest were tall, out of proportion with the rest of its limbs. The neck was slender, and from the rest of its body's mass, it stretched out into a swan-like neck. The head was similar to a camel's. It was almost twice the size of a Libyan <margin>Ostrich.</margin> <margin>Libyan.</margin> ostrich <note>original: "Struthus"</note>, and it blinked, moving its eyes sternly as if they were painted underneath. Its gait, moreover, was far different from other land animals..."

<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing a description of an exotic animal's movement from the previous page. Based on the context and the citations of Heliodorus and Pliny, this page describes the giraffe, known historically as the camelopard.</meta>

->BOOK<-

...unlike terrestrial or aquatic animals, for it did not move <margin>Alternately.</margin> its legs one after the other in turn, but rather moved both right legs together, and then both left legs together. It carried its flanks suspended with these movements, with a motion so easy that the trainer could lead it wherever he wished with a slender halter tied to its head, as if it were the strongest chain. <margin>This animal.</margin> When this living creature was seen, it immediately struck the entire multitude with amazement; its name was derived from its appearance and the most prominent features of its body, so that the people spontaneously called it a <term>Camelopardalis</term> <note>original: "Camelopardalin"; a historical name for a giraffe, combining "camel" and "pard" (leopard) because of its long neck and spots.</note>. Thus far Heliodorus <note>A Greek novelist of the 3rd or 4th century AD, author of the "Aethiopica," which contains a famous description of a giraffe.</note>. 

However, Dio the Greek, an author himself, writes nearly as follows in the forty-third book of his *Roman History* when speaking of Caesar: "He was the first to bring the <term>Camelopardalis</term> to Rome and show it to the people. This animal is like a camel in other respects, but has unequal legs; the hind legs are shorter than the front ones, so that from the haunches it seems to rise gradually as if it were climbing. The rest of its body is supported very high by the front legs, but its neck rises to an almost extraordinary height. Its color is spotted, like a panther, which is why it is known by a name that combines the two." 

Pliny, indeed, when treating the subject of camels, adds this as well: "A certain resemblance to these is transferred into two animals. The Ethiopians call one the <term>Nabun</term> <note>Pliny's term for the giraffe, possibly derived from a local African name.</note>; it has a neck like a horse, feet and legs like an ox, a head like a camel, and white spots distinguishing its reddish color. From these traits it is called the Camelopardalis..."

In Plato it is written as follows: "O dear son of Cleinias and Dinomache." <note>original: ὦ φίλε παῖ κλενίȣ ϗ δ' εινομάχης. This is a reference to the opening of Plato’s *First Alcibiades*, addressing the young statesman Alcibiades by his lineage.</note> Likewise, when he says in that same place, "Dwell with yourself," does he not seem to have fully grasped the intent of that same dialogue? Indeed (as Proclus the commentator <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensive commentaries on Plato.</note> asserts), Plato here looked toward nothing else but the Delphic inscription, which warns that each person should know themselves. Therefore, to have discovered these things at last, or even to have compared them with one another, will produce not only a benefit for scholars but also a pleasure that is by no means common.

But indeed, ignorant men <note>original: *opici*; a term used to describe those who are unlearned or barbaric, named after the ancient Oscan tribe of Italy who were viewed as uncultured by the Greeks and Romans</note> began to strike out a word unknown to them; in place of that which is *durateus* <note>wooden</note>, they began to substitute various bits of nonsense, such as "stern-looking" <note>original: *dura tuens*—a Latin phrase that sounds similar to the Greek word but changes the meaning entirely</note> or "holding dire things" <note>original: *dira tenens*</note>. ### CHAPTER VI

That famous sparrow of Catullus, I believe, hides an allegorical <note>original Greek: "allegoricōs"</note> and rather obscene meaning, which we cannot speak aloud without violating modesty. I am persuaded to believe this by that epigram of Martial, of which these are the final verses:
*Give me kisses, but Catullian ones,*
*Which, if they are as many as he said,*
*I will give you Catullus's sparrow.*
For the poet would be quite tasteless <margin>Tasteless</margin> (which is a sin to believe) if he were to say <margin>poet.</margin> that he would give the boy a literal sparrow and nothing else—as I suspect—after those kisses. ->BOOK<-

...names began to be commonly used not so long ago (so to speak). However, the ancient Greeks (as far as I know) had no knowledge of this practice at all. But since this custom is so widespread among other nations—and especially among the Romans, so much so that it now seems almost like a native tradition—I will say a few things about these matters and explain how and for what reason they are arranged in this way.

I have heard of two explanations, both of which are certainly not difficult to understand, yet both require careful examination. For if someone takes the harmony called the **fourth** <note>original: "dià tessarōn"; a musical interval where the fourth note is the dominant. In this astrological context, skipping two planets to name the third is described as a "fourth" step (e.g., Saturn to Sun).</note>—which is believed to contain the entire power of all music—and relates it to these stars (which distinguish the whole beauty of the heavens) in the same order in which they move; and if they begin from that highest sphere assigned to **Saturn**, skip the next two, and then name the lord of the fourth; and then jump over another two and add the seventh; and proceeding in the same way, counts the gods presiding over those spheres in order and thus counts the days, they will find that they all correspond by a certain musical logic to that celestial arrangement and decoration.

However, this is only one explanation that is told. The other is of this sort: If you begin to count the hours of the day and night starting from the very first, so that you assign it to **Saturn**, then the next to **Jupiter**, the third to **Mars**, the fourth to the **Sun**, the fifth to **Venus**, the sixth to **Mercury**, and the seventh to the **Moon**, following that sequence of spheres <note>The author is describing the "Chaldean Order" of the planets based on their perceived distance from Earth: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon.</note>...

<summary>This page discusses the origins of the seven-day week and its planetary names, explaining that the custom was unknown to ancient Greeks but became fundamental to Roman life. The author introduces two theories for the sequence of the days: one based on musical intervals (the "fourth") and another based on the assignment of planets to specific hours of the day.</summary>

which is handed down by the Egyptians; and then you would do the same again, until you have passed through twenty-four hours, you will find the first hour of the following day connected to the Sun.
