# BOOK

## WHAT IS TO BE BELIEVED IN THE WRITINGS OF DOMITIUS
### and to what extent; and concerning his clear and shameful error regarding the word *attegias*.

### CHAPTER IX.

**D**estroy the huts <note>original: "attegias"; a rare word for a small cottage or hut, often associated with nomadic tribes.</note> of the Moors. This half-line from the fourteenth satire of Juvenal <note>A famous Roman satirical poet known for his biting critiques of society.</note>—let us now examine (if you have the time) whether Domitius explains it with scholarship, or fidelity, or even with clarity. But first <margin>Domitius Calderinus</margin>, a few words about the man himself, in case anyone is curious about these matters. Domitius Calderinus of Verona, who passed away ten years ago, was a man of sharp intellect and great industry in literature. He was a most diligent student of the Latin language, not unacquainted with Greek, and quite skilled in composing both prose and verse. However, as his own works testify, he was an excessive admirer of himself, and—once he had settled on an opinion—he was <margin>Stubborn.</margin> stubborn and unyielding even against the truth. Because of this, he was also rather boastful in his writing, a despiser of everyone else, a mocker of others at the slightest opportunity, and an indiscriminate critic. Consequently, he was embroiled in far too many feuds with the most learned men of his time and suffered under their quite obvious hatred. Yet, he held such great authority among the common people that in Rome, while still a young man, he claimed for himself the highest celebrity among the professors.

<summary>This page introduces a critique of the 15th-century scholar Domitius Calderinus, characterizing him as a brilliant but arrogant and stubborn intellectual who frequently clashed with his peers.</summary>

# M V BOOK 2

If any mention of that word is found to have been made, I will yield, and I will throw up my hands <note>A Latin idiom meaning to admit defeat.</note> right then; I will not refuse to be considered the most boastful of all men. But, setting aside all empty pretense, <margin>Priscian’s text</margin> let us consider the text of Priscian <note>Priscian was a famous Latin grammarian of the 6th century.</note> itself. These are not the verses of Dionysius <note>Dionysius Periegetes, a Greek poet who wrote a description of the inhabited world.</note>, nor are they a <margin>Translation.</margin> translation. Rather, they belong to Priscian, from the little book in which he interprets the poet Dionysius from the Greek. However, Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century Renaissance scholar often criticized for his aggressive and sometimes inaccurate emendations of classical texts.</note> corrupts and defiles them. For where Priscian wrote "to the waves of Tethys" <note>original: "ad Tethyos undas." Tethys was a sea goddess, often used metonymically for the ocean.</note>, [Domitius] erased and altered the earlier writing, and in its place substituted "the waves of the huts" <note>original: "attegias undas." An "attegia" was a type of small hut or cottage used by nomadic tribes.</note>. In my opinion, he would never have done this if he had considered the logic of the poem, or the lengths and pauses of the syllables. For he placed the word "huts" <term>attegias</term> with a long antepenultimate syllable—contrary to the testimony of the very author he is interpreting—and with a short final syllable, contrary to all authority. It is fortunate that Dionysius’s work survives; I believe these are his Greek verses on that subject:

->But truly, they inhabit the furthest tongue of land, near the pillars, the tribes of the Maurusian earth.<-
<note>original: "ἀλλ’ ἤτοι πυμάτην μὲν ἐπὶ γλωχῖνα νέμοντα / ἀγχῠ συλάων μαυρουσίδος ἔθνεα γαίης." This describes the people living near the Pillars of Hercules (Gibraltar) in North Africa.</note>

Even if you examine the whole work now, you will see that there is not even the slightest suspicion of "huts" <note>attegiarũ</note> in Dionysius. You will see one person cited for another, the true writing expunged and a false one substituted, and no regard given to the logic of the syllables or the poem. And will anyone now hesitate to disagree with him most freely, <margin>Prejudiced</margin> and to refute these trifles? Even if a <margin>opinion.</margin> previously held opinion regarding the man's genius and learning might make the case difficult.

...one stands by a sufficiently certain author. But it is characteristic of this man (for I shall now speak more freely) that when he can explain nothing, he entangles everything; whatever comes his way, he seizes upon, and is carried along by a conflicting conscience to wherever the impulse of a wandering opinion finally drives him. We suspect that the Old Comedy of the poet Eupolis is being hinted at, <margin>The comedy of Eupolis</margin> which the excellent authority of the ancients has persuaded us of. Indeed, Platonius, a Greek author, speaks of it thus when dealing with the differences between types of comedies: "We know," <note>original Greek: "ἴσμεν γοῦν..."</note> he says, "that Eupolis, because he produced the play *The Baptae*, was drowned in the sea by those very people against whom he had directed *The Baptae*." <margin>The Baptae</margin> <note>The "Baptae" or "Dippers" was a satirical play attacking the effeminacy of the followers of the goddess Cotytto.</note> Lucian also, in that speech titled *To the Ignorant Book-Collector*, writes to this effect: "Tell me," he says, "do you even read Aeschines' oration against Timarchus? Or do you truly know all those things and recognize every detail? Have you looked into Aristophanes and Eupolis? Have you read through the entire play *The Baptae*? Then did you think none of these things applied to you? And did you not blush when you recognized them?" But also Hephaestion in his manual <term>Enchiridion; a concise handbook of poetic meters</term>, when dealing with <term>acatalectic; a line of verse that has the full number of syllables in the final foot</term> verses, cites two lines from Eupolis's *The Baptae*. And Suidas <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world</note> cites one of the same under the entry <term>Atrypheros; a Greek term meaning "not delicate" or "hardy"</term>. Now, therefore, I believe it is first established that *The Baptae* was an ancient and biting <note>original: "mordacem," meaning sharp or stinging satire</note> comedy...

It is clear, therefore, that the Cotyttia were foreign rites usually observed by the Athenians, and it is also clear they were attacked by the comic poets. We are led by conjecture to believe that since mention is made here of the *Baptae*—which we know was a comedy by Eupolis—and of the nocturnal rites of the Athenians, it was in that play specifically that this kind of rite was lampooned. ...herself. And what the goddess was to Adonis, Mars was to Venus. A god loved a goddess, and a goddess pursued a mortal man. Their desire was equal, even if their status <note>The author uses the Latin "genus," meaning their race or kind—one being divine and the other mortal.</note> was different. Jealous Mars aimed to destroy Adonis, thinking that the death of Adonis would be the end of love. Mars strikes Adonis. Venus runs to his aid; in her haste, she falls among roses and entangles herself in the thorns. Thus her foot is pierced, and the blood that flowed from the wound colored the rose from her own essence. Thus the rose, which was previously white, was made as it is seen now. 

We ourselves touched upon this little story in our *Rusticus* <note>Poliziano refers here to his own Latin poem, *Rusticus*, published in 1483.</note>, in that verse: 
*The rose, modest in its Idalian* <note>referring to Mount Idalium in Cyprus, a site sacred to Venus</note> *heart, stains itself with blood.*

But also, the Maecenas <note>Caius Maecenas was a famous Roman patron of the arts; here the name is used as a title for a great patron.</note> of our age, Lorenzo de' Medici—who is as preeminent in poetry as he is in every other virtue—has embraced this whole story most beautifully in vernacular verses <note>This refers to Lorenzo "the Magnificent" de' Medici's poetry written in Italian rather than Latin.</note>.

He writes thus concerning Venus: 

*She alone did not wish to see the joy of boars, as if she were a prophetess; since Mars, in the form of a jagged-toothed boar, casting a death-bearing poison in a fit of jealous madness, was about to weave the fate of Adonis.* <note>original Greek: "μούνηρ ὃ συῶν οὐκ ἤθελε λεύσειν τερπωλὴν ἅτε μάντις, ἐπεὶ συὸς εἰκόνι μορφῆς ἄρης καρχαρόδων θανατηφόρον ἰὸν ἰάλλων ζηλομανὴς ἤμελλεν Ἀδώνιδι πότμον ὑφαίνειν."</note>

In these verses, Nonnus denies that Venus was delighted by boars <note>Classical sources often mention Venus's hatred of boars because of Adonis's death.</note>, because she had known that Mars would one day take on the shape of a boar and, out of jealousy, kill Adonis. Indeed, that the rose is otherwise pleasing to Venus is not kept silent by that most charming bard of epigrams.

The Tyrians tell the story (he says) that Hercules, seized by love for a certain local nymph named Tyro, <margin>Tyro.</margin> was being followed (they say) by a dog in the ancient fashion, by which dogs would accompany their master even to the public assembly. For this reason, this dog of Hercules, having caught sight of a purple-snail <term>purpura: the murex sea snail used to create the famous Tyrian purple dye</term> creeping among the rocks, bit into its flesh and stained its own lips with the scarlet blood. When Hercules then went to the girl, she was delighted by the unusual dye which she had seen as if blooming on the dog’s lips; she declared that she would have nothing to do with him thereafter <margin>The discovery from a living creature.</margin> unless he brought her a garment even more brilliant than the lips of that dog. Therefore, Hercules, having found the creature and collected the blood, brought the gift to the girl, becoming (as the Tyrians are fond of saying) the first inventor of the Punic <note>original: "Punicae"; refers to the Phoenician or Carthaginian technique</note> dyeing process. We, therefore, in our *Rusticus* <note>A Latin poem by Angelo Poliziano, published in 1483</note>, on account of this very story, used the term "Herculean shell" <margin>The Herculean shell.</margin> when we intended to signify purple. Where the sea-foraging dog, roaming on the shores,
found the wondrous fish in its inner hiding place with his grey jaws,
and stained his snowy cheeks purple; from within the shell,
reddening his lips with a liquid fire, which alone
was used to redden the bright sea-cloaks of kings.

->THE AUGURY OF SALVATION WHICH<-

**S**uetonius Tranquillus <note>Suetonius (c. 69–122 AD) was a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the first twelve Roman emperors.</note> says the following in his *Life of Augustus*: *He restored several of the <margin>The Augury</margin> ancient ceremonies that had gradually been abolished, such as the <margin>of Salvation.</margin> <term>Augury of Salvation</term> <note>original: "salutis augurium." A ritual to determine if the gods would allow the Roman people to pray for their collective safety.</note>, etc.* Now, what this "Augury of Salvation" actually was, I see is unknown even to some learned men.

We have found the matter explained in this way in the thirty-seventh book of Dio’s *Histories* <note>Dio Cassius (c. 155–235 AD) wrote a massive 80-volume history of Rome in Greek.</note>: "At that time, they also performed the so-called Augury of Salvation after a long interval of time. It is a particular kind of <term>augury</term> <note>A religious practice where officials (augurs) interpreted signs from the gods to approve or disapprove of proposed actions.</note> conducted so that, if God permits, they may ask for safety for the people; it is as if it were not even lawful to seek safety from the Gods unless the Gods themselves had first granted permission for this very request. One day each year was observed on which no army could set out for war, no one could prepare themselves for battle, and no one could fight. For this reason, in times of constant danger—and especially during civil wars—it was never performed. For it was primarily difficult to keep a day 'pure' from all these military activities, and it could seem quite absurd to bring incredible harm upon themselves through internal rebellions (which would be disastrous for the defeated and likewise ruinous for the victors) and then shortly after ask the immortal gods for safety. However, at that time, such an augury was indeed able to be performed. But not even that proceeded 'purely,' because adverse..."

### CHAPTER 14.

*Learn also to turn the festive naulia with both palms,*
*they are suited to sweet melodies.*
<note>These lines are from Ovid’s "Ars Amatoria" (The Art of Love), Book 3, instructing women on the skills needed to be charming, including music.</note>

<margin>Naulia.</margin>
<margin>Nablia.</margin>
<margin>Naula.</margin>
<margin>Nabla.</margin>
I believe **Naulia** is a diminutive form, or perhaps **nablia**, derived from those instruments we call either **naula** or **nabla**. Indeed, we have frequently noted in ancient records that the letters ‘b’ and ‘u’ were often used interchangeably by the ancients. Suidas <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia</note> also states that the *nabla* is a type of instrument. In another entry, he says that the **Psaltery** <note>A stringed instrument similar to a harp or zither</note> is a musical instrument which is also called the *naula*. Furthermore, Plutarch cites a verse from Sophocles: *neither the naula for lamentations, nor the lyre is dear* <note>original Greek: "οὐ ναῦλα κωκυτοῖσιν, οὐ λύρα φίλα"</note>, in that book he wrote concerning the "E" at Delphi; and Theodoretes <note>Theodoret of Cyrus, a 5th-century theologian</note> also mentions this...

Thus says Scipio. Then **Ambrose** as well, in his third book *On the Utility of Fasting*, teaches that psalteries are used at banquets so that lustful desires may be incited. Indeed, there is also frequent mention of female psaltery-players in comedies. Furthermore, the word itself is Hebrew; for in Greek it is called a *psalterium*, and in Latin a *laudatorium* <note>an instrument of praise</note>, just as **Saint Jerome** says in his letter to Dardanus, teaching that it is shaped like a square shield with ten strings. **Philip the Younger**—also called *Philippus Iunior*—who is believed to have never laughed, is said to have noted his father, **Philip the Elder**, laughing too shamelessly <note>The Latin *petulantius cachinnantem* suggests a loud, unrestrained, or immoderate laugh that the somber son found inappropriate.</note> with a turned-away face. <margin>Rituals of the Secular Games.</margin>
briefly touch upon the ritual in parts. As the games <note>These are the *Ludi Saeculares*, a major Roman religious festival held roughly once every 110 years, intended to mark the end of one *saeculum* (an age or lifetime) and the start of the next.</note> approached, heralds were sent throughout all Italy to announce that people were being summoned to games that they had never seen before and would never see again. Then, in the summer, a few days before the spectacles were produced, the <term>Fifteen Men</term> <note>original: *Quindecimviri sacris faciundis*; a prestigious Roman priestly college responsible for guarding the Sibylline Books and overseeing foreign religious rites.</note> for performing sacred rites, sitting on a platform in the Capitol and the Palatine temple,

<margin>Purifications.</margin>
distributed **purifications** <note>original: *piamina*; ritual objects used to spiritually cleanse the participants.</note> to the people, which consisted of torches, sulfur, and bitumen. However, slaves had no right to receive these. The people gathered not only in the places mentioned above, but also in the temples of Diana on the Aventine Hill, where it was the custom to give wheat, beans, and barley to each person.

<margin>In the fashion of.</margin>
Then, in the fashion of the **mysteries of Ceres**, <note>Ceres was the goddess of agriculture; her "mysteries" involved secret initiation rituals and night-long vigils.</note> night-watches were held. When the festival day finally arrived, they spent three days and three nights intent on the rites, primarily on the banks of the Tiber River itself. Sacrifices were undertaken to Jupiter, Juno, Apollo, Latona, Diana, and additionally to the Fates, and those goddesses called the <term>Ilithyiae</term> <note>Greek goddesses of childbirth who assisted mothers during labor.</note>, as well as to Ceres, Dis, and Proserpina <note>Dis (Pluto) and Proserpina were the rulers of the Underworld; their inclusion highlights the "secular" or generational nature of the festival, connecting the living to the ancestors.</note>. Therefore, at the second hour of the first night, the **Princeps** <note>The Emperor.</note> himself, at three altars built upon the river bank, sacrificed three lambs along with the Fifteen Men; once the altars were soaked with blood, they burned the bodies of the victims. If anyone should perhaps ask what kind of games or sacrifices were performed then, let him know that games were celebrated in every theater, and of every kind, and sacrifices were customary in all temples, by night as well as by day. For there is very little doubt regarding the stage plays and Circus games, since we remember that actors were present at the games held by Emperor Domitian; furthermore, we read that on the day of the Circus games, so that a hundred races <term>missus: a single "heat" or start in a day of chariot racing</term> could be more easily completed, each race was shortened from seven laps to five. Shall bow their necks to your yoke, and shall fear the scepters of the victor.

# AGON: THE WORD WHICH THE <term>VICTIMARIUS <note>An assistant at a Roman sacrifice responsible for the physical slaughter of the animal.</note></term> WAS ACCUSTOMED TO USE, ABOUT WHICH OVID ALSO WRITES IN THE FASTI.

## CHAPTER LX.

**O**vid in the first book of the <note>The *Fasti* is Ovid’s poetic calendar of Roman religious festivals.</note> *Fasti*:
"The cause of the name <note>Referring to the Agonalia festival.</note> may be the tucked-up attendant,
By whose blow the victim falls for the gods above;
Who, about to stain the drawn knives with warm blood,
Always asks '<term>Agon?</term>' <note>The ritual question "Shall I strike?" or "Shall I proceed?"</note> and does not act unless ordered."

Regarding this rite of slaughtering the victim, and likewise concerning the word which the priest's assistant used, I find mention made only in <note>Seneca the Elder, the father of the philosopher, who compiled famous rhetorical exercises in his *Controversiae*.</note> Seneca, in that same third book of the Orators and Rhetoricians, through these words: "Then, with the greatest uproar of the scholars: 'And you, whoever you are, executioner, when you have raised the ready axe, before you strike the father, look back.' <note>This refers to a hypothetical legal scenario used in Roman rhetorical schools.</note> <note>Asinius Pollio was a famous Roman statesman, orator, and critic known for his sharp wit.</note> Asinius Pollio used to mock these things beautifully. 'Let the son,' he said, 'stretch out his neck. Let the executioner stretch out his hand, then let him look back at the father, and say, *Agon?* [*Shall I strike?*]'"

ERROR IN THE MANUSCRIPTS OF PLINY regarding hemlock and wine. Also, the faulty reading in Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Roman provincial writer</note> of Aconite instead of Hemlock <note>original: "Aconitum pro Conio." Hemlock was famously used to execute Socrates.</note>.

### CHAPTER LXI.

<margin>A passage from Pliny.</margin> The words from the letter of Androcides to Alexander the Great—a man famous for his wisdom—are found to be incorrectly placed in these manuscripts of Pliny in this manner: *King, when you are about to drink wine, remember that you are drinking the blood of the earth. Just as hemlock is poison to man, so also is wine.* This is surely a trivial sentiment, too violent and forced; I would add even tasteless and false, to say that wine is a poison to man just as hemlock is. 

But indeed, in that most ancient Plinian manuscript of the Medici family, which we have cited often already, you may find it thus: <margin>Hemlock, wine, poison. The use of wine.</margin> *Hemlock is poison to man, and wine is poison to hemlock.* <note>In the ancient world, it was believed that concentrated wine could act as an antidote to certain poisons by stimulating the body.</note> For just as hemlock destroys a man, so a drink of pure wine destroys the venom of hemlock. And from this, Androcides strives to persuade Alexander <note>Androcides was a physician who advised Alexander the Great to limit his drinking.</note>...

<summary>This page corrects a common error in classical manuscripts regarding a letter sent to Alexander the Great, clarifying that wine was considered an antidote to hemlock rather than a poison similar to it. It also explains the ritual origins of the word "Agon" used during sacrifices.</summary>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the properties of wine, likely referencing the views of Democritus and other ancient authorities on its medicinal and poisonous qualities.</meta>

->MISCELLANEOUS WORKS. 80<-

...[Democritus counsels] that one should use wine more sparingly, as it is a most powerful thing, for it can be the "poison of poisons." <note>original: "veneni venenum." Poliziano is discussing the dual nature of wine as both medicine and toxin.</note> From this, he taught with a similar sentiment that wine is the "blood of the earth." Regarding these words, Pliny concludes that nothing is more beneficial for the body's strength, nor more ruinous to its pleasures, if moderation is lacking. Similarly, the same author [Pliny], in books 20 and 25, while describing hemlock as fatal to man, says: "The warming nature of wine is a remedy, provided it is administered before the poison reaches the vital organs." Dioscorides reported the same thing. 

Therefore, Saint Ambrose, in the book he composed on the *Utility of Fasting*, says: "The power of wine is even greater than that of poison." Finally, poison is expelled by wine, but wine is not expelled by poison. Furthermore, Plutarch, in his *Table Talk*, says almost the same things as Pliny regarding hemlock. Then Macrobius, in his *Saturnalia*, attempted to express that passage—as well as many others—almost word for word. 

However, in all the manuscripts of Macrobius that I have seen so far, <term>Aconite</term><note>Aconitum; a genus of poisonous flowering plants, also known as monkshood or wolfsbane.</note> is found instead of <term>Hemlock</term>, <margin>*Koneion* is not aconite, but Hemlock.</margin> just as it is in the very old manuscript belonging to Bernardo Michelozzi, a man of great learning and a friend of ours. But I believe those copies are erroneous; for I could not be persuaded that Macrobius was so unlearned that he would translate the Greek word *Koneion* <note>original: "Conion," from the Greek κώνειον (kōneion), meaning hemlock.</note> as "Aconite." I would more readily believe that he used the Greek term itself, just as Ambrose also used the word *Conium* in his <term>Hexahemeron</term>, <note>A theological treatise on the six days of creation.</note> <margin>Ambrose's Hexahemeron.</margin> which Rufinus—translating from Basil’s book of the same subject and title—rendered as "hemlock." It is indeed a slippery slip of the pen, to turn *conio* [hemlock] into *aconitum* [aconite].

->**ON THE RAM’S TESTICLES AND THE "PHYSICIAN FINGER" AS MENTIONED BY TULLY**<-

### CHAPTER LXII.

<margin>Cicero the Orator. A passage from Cicero.</margin>
**C**icero, in the third book of his work *To Herennius* <note>original: *Ad Herennium*; an influential ancient textbook on the art of rhetoric and memory</note>, while discussing the <term>art of memory</term> <note>a system of mental "places" and "images" used by ancient orators to memorize long speeches</note>, describes how to construct a specific mental image. He says: "We shall place the defendant by his bed, holding a cup in his right hand, writing tablets in his left, and ram’s testicles on his **physician finger**." In this passage, I see certain scholars—even those of no small reputation—hesitating over what we read: "holding ram’s testicles on the physician finger." 
<margin>The Physician.</margin>
Now, the "Physician" is the name of one of the five fingers; it is, I believe, the one next to the smallest <note>The ring finger</note>. For Porphyrio <note>A 3rd-century scholar who wrote commentaries on the poet Horace</note> comments on that line from Horace's last *Sermones*:
    *Who, if something happened to be hidden,*
    *Would point it out with his index finger.*
<margin>Names of the fingers.</margin>
He speaks thus because each individual finger was called by a specific name, and these names are: The thumb, the index, the "infamous" <note>original: *famosus*; the middle finger, so named for its use in obscene gestures</note>, the physician, and the smallest. Indeed, certain Greeks—of a more recent era, I believe—record the names of the fingers in the "Roman way" <note>original: *ρωμαϊστὶ*</note> as: *anticheir* <note>the "opposite hand," i.e., the thumb</note>, *deiktikos* <note>the "pointer," or index</note>, *iatros* <note>the "physician"</note>, *daktyliotes* <note>the "ring-wearer"</note>, and *otites* <note>the "ear-finger," used for cleaning the ear</note>. This is as if you were to say: Thumb, index, physician, ring-finger, and ear-finger. For my part, I neither confirm nor deny this. It only remains to be noted that the finger is called "physician" because it is in the middle, or...

### CHAPTER 64.

<margin>Ausonius</margin> These are the trimeter verses of the poet Ausonius:
*To whom shall I yoke, from the turnings of the millstones, those who turn the rocks with mechanical weight: the three-legged horses, their backs broken by the lash?*

What should we think he signifies here? Surely, I believe, he refers to those verses which are called <term>Choliambics</term> <note>From the Greek 'choliambos', meaning 'lame iambic'</note> or <term>Scazons</term> <note>From the Greek 'skazon', meaning 'limping'</note>. <margin>Choliambics. Scazons.</margin> You might call them "limping" verses, such as those found at the beginning of Persius, or that one by Martial, which he cited by name:
*Go, my Scazon, and meet my friend Apollinaris.*

For the Scazon is an iambic trimeter, consisting of six feet just like the standard "straight" iambic, but differing in this one respect (as Hephaestion <note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian who wrote a manual on meters</note> also writes): that while the straight iambic has its last foot...

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of poetic meters, specifically focusing on the variation of the iambic meter known as the "limping" iambic or choliamb.</meta>

->MISCELLANY. 82<-

...it occupies the position with an <term>iamb|a metrical foot consisting of one short syllable followed by one long syllable</term> or a <term>pyrrhic|a metrical foot consisting of two short syllables</term>; in the antepenultimate <note>third to last</note> position, it accepts all feet of which that meter is capable, being indifferent regarding the quantities of the syllables. But this **limping iambic** <note>Also known as a choliamb or scazon</note> strictly requires a <term>spondaic|a foot of two long syllables</term> or <term>trochaic|a foot of one long syllable followed by one short</term> ending. However, in the penultimate <note>second to last</note> foot, it requires not just any disyllable, but clearly an iamb or a spondee. As for what Hephaestion <note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian</note> says—that a spondee is received in the fifth position of the choliamb—I certainly do not recall ever finding this among Latin writers; he himself cites this Greek verse:
*Dragging it to the tip, like cooling a sausage.* <note>original Greek: ἐπ’ ἄκρον ἕλκων ὥσπερ ἀλλᾶντα ψύχων</note>
And Quintilianus Aristides, a Greek author, in his book *On Music*, speaking about the iambic <term>trimeter|a line consisting of three pairs of iambic feet</term>, says it becomes **Limping** when it receives a spondee at the end. Finally, Ovid also writes as follows about both types of iambics in the first book of *Remedies for Love*:
*Let the free iamb be drawn against opposing enemies,*
*Whether it be the swift kind, or the one that drags its final foot.*
<note>original Latin: Liber in adversos hostes stringatur Iambus, / Seu celer, extremum seu trahit ille pedem.</note>
Both, however, were created for slander and license; they are grim and bloody. For this reason, Ovid says they should even be "drawn" <note>like a sword</note> against opposing enemies. Likewise, Dionysius of Halicarnassus, in his book *On Style* <note>original Latin: de interpretatione; referring to his work 'On the Arrangement of Words'</note>, says that choliambics are a vehement or sharp meter. <margin>Limping iambics</margin> He also says shortly after that Hipponax <note>An archaic Greek poet known for his biting satire</note>, when he wished to provoke his enemies with insults, deliberately broke the meter, making it "limping" and **off-beat** <margin>off-beat</margin> <note>original Latin: abnumerum</note> instead of straight—that is, something consistent with bitterness and insult.

The author explains that this specific rhythm was intentionally 'broken' by poets like Hipponax to better suit biting insults and satirical attacks.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on poetic meter and the stylistic effects of different verse forms, likely following a section on iambic poetry.</meta>

28 BOOK

For just as a loose figure prepares the way for bitterness, so (as <note>Referring to an author mentioned on the preceding page, likely a classical grammarian or rhetorician.</note> he says) does a loose composition. For this reason, Ausonius <note>Decimus Magnus Ausonius (c. 310–395 AD), a Roman poet and rhetorician.</note> certainly names those <term>choliambics</term> <note>A "limping" iambic meter where the final foot is reversed, creating a staggering rhythm often used for satire.</note> as "nags" <margin>Nags.</margin>, a metaphor he had drawn from the chariot and the carriage to which nags are hitched. And to show that they are slow, he says they are taken from the windings of the mill-stones; furthermore, he says their backs are broken by the whip. Then he called these same verses "three-footed" <margin>Trimeters.</margin> because in Greek they are "trimeters," though they possess six feet, for every two feet are seen to be counted as one in iambic verse. He said they "roll stones with mechanical weight" for that reason—either as if he were describing the mill-stones which he had indicated they turn, or to show them to be violent and fierce through allegory, like those who twist and roll huge stones against an enemy. Concerning both types of trimeter, the same Ausonius speaks elsewhere in a short Greek verse:
<note>original: "εἰλιπόδην σκάζοντα κὴ οὐ σκάζοντα τρίμετρον"</note>
"The heavy-footed limper, and the trimeter that does not limp."
Which you may translate into English thus:
The club-footed Scazon, and the trimeter that does not limp. <margin>Scazons.</margin> <note>Another name for the choliambic, from the Greek word for "limping."</note>
Concerning these same scazons, it is written thus in a certain letter from the fifth book by Pliny the Younger: "Therefore, either break off your delays, or take care lest these same little books—which my <term>hendecasyllables</term> <note>A light, eleven-syllable verse form often used for casual or playful themes.</note> could not entice from you with flatteries—extort them from you by the insults of scazons."

<summary>This page explains the "choliambic" or "scazon" meter, using metaphors from the poet Ausonius and Pliny the Younger to describe its "limping," heavy, and often insulting or satirical character.</summary>
<keywords>Ausonius, Pliny the Younger, choliambic, scazon, trimeter, poetic meter, hendecasyllable, classical rhetoric</keywords>

## WHAT THE "PEACEMAKER" POSE IS IN STATUES, and how Statius refers to it in the first book of the *Silvae*. CHAPTER 65.

**S**tatius, in the first of his *Silvae* <note>The *Silvae* is a collection of occasional poems by the Roman poet Statius, written in the late 1st century AD.</note>, which was written about the great equestrian statue <margin>A passage from Statius</margin> [of Domitian]: *It is a joy to behold the face, where marks of war are joined with the bearing of calm peace.* <note>original: "Iuuat ora tueri Mixta notis belli, placidamq́; gerentia pacem."</note> Then again: *The right hand forbids battles.* <note>original: "Dextra uetat pugnas."</note>

Domitius <note>Likely referring to Domizio Calderini, a prominent 15th-century humanist commentator.</note> explains this passage as follows: "The right hand forbids battles," meaning that the figure in the statue is unarmed, or perhaps that the Temple of Peace is located to the right—an interpretation which Domitius himself says he does not like. Furthermore, while the idea that the "right hand forbids battles" because it is unarmed—or the story told about the Temple of Peace itself—is a common interpretation, even if it does not please the commentator, I will not be too bothered if my own opinion on this matter is not approved. 

For I believe a specific <term>pose</term> <note>The author uses "habitus," referring to the physical stance or gesture of a statue.</note> is being noted here, which <margin>The "peacemaker" pose.</margin> was called the "peacemaker" in statues. Regarding this, we read the following in Quintilian <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, a famous Roman rhetorician.</note>: *That pose is also used which is common for peacemakers in statues: where, with the head tilted toward the right shoulder and the arm stretched out from the ear, the hand is extended with the thumb pointing forward. <margin>A passage from Quintilian.</margin> This is especially pleasing to those who boast about their speaking style by raising their hand.* <note>original: "Fit & ille habitus qui esse in statuis pacificator solet, qui inclinato in humerum dextrum capite, brachioq́; ab aure protenso, manum infesto pollice protẽdit..."</note> Thus far Quintilian.

There still exists today (unless I am mistaken) a statue in that "peacemaker" pose in Rome, which is visited as one of the primary sights of the city, located in front of the Lateran Church. <note>The author is referring to the famous bronze equestrian statue of Marcus Aurelius. In the Renaissance, it stood near the Lateran Palace before being moved to the Capitoline Hill. At the time this was written, many believed it represented Constantine the Great.</note>

N ii <note>This is a "signature mark," a printer's code used to help bookbinders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page identifies the specific rhetorical gesture of the "peacemaker" in Roman sculpture, using a poem by Statius and a description by Quintilian to explain its meaning. The author identifies the famous equestrian statue (now known to be Marcus Aurelius) at the Lateran as a primary surviving example of this pose.</summary>

->A PASSAGE IN PLAVTVS’S *MOSTELLARIA*, AND LIKEWISE ANOTHER IN THE *MILES*, VINDICATED FROM ERROR.<-

## CHAPTER LXVI.

<margin>A passage of Plautus</margin>
In Plautus’s *Mostellaria* <note>The *Mostellaria*, or "The Haunted House," is a famous Roman comedy involving a clever slave and a supposedly haunted building.</note>, there is a passage written incorrectly in most manuscripts in exactly this manner: *Do you see the ornaments on the doors? I see them. Look how artfully they sleep. They sleep. Indeed, I meant to say that it fits.* 

But since no meaning can be drawn from these words, and a witty remark of Plautus is lost through the fault of the scribes <note>Librariorum: the copyists who transcribed manuscripts by hand, often introducing errors.</note>, I shall do something worthwhile, I believe, if I restore the healthy writing from the Plautine manuscript I have cited. It is precisely this: *Do you see the <term>joints</term> <note>original: "coagmenta"; refers to the seams or joints where the wood of the door is fitted together.</note> in the doors? I see them. Look how artfully they sleep. They sleep. Indeed, I meant to say they <term>blink</term> <note>original: "conniuent"; used here as a pun for being tightly shut.</note>.* 

<margin>To blink</margin>
For because those who are drowsy are said to "blink" or "close their eyes," as in the verse read in Cinna <note>Gaius Helvius Cinna, a Roman poet of the 1st century BCE.</note>: 
*Now the heavy eye begins to blink in great sleep.* 

<margin>Joints</margin>
Because the slave Tranio was showing that the joints in the doors were not at all loose—for that is what <term>joints</term> are—he says jokingly that they "sleep" first, and then even that they "blink," using a metaphor drawn from the eyes of those falling asleep. 

<margin>Another passage of Plautus</margin>
Similarly, I think another passage from Plautus’s *Miles* <note>The *Miles Gloriosus*, or "The Braggart Soldier."</note> should be corrected, which is erroneously read as follows: *It is a wonder that you live on oil and such cheap wheat. Why so? Because you are dim-sighted. By Pollux, you scoundrel, you are blind indeed, not dim-sighted.* For since no meaning can be drawn from this... <note>The text ends mid-argument here.</note>

<summary>The author corrects a scribal error in Plautus's "Mostellaria," restoring a pun where door joints are described as "sleeping" or "blinking" because they are so tightly fitted. He also begins a similar correction for a passage in the "Miles Gloriosus" concerning the term for dim-sightedness.</summary>

->BOOK<-

<margin>The concord of crows.</margin>
...I accept this all the more, led by the testimony of the Greek writer Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD), a Roman author who wrote "On the Nature of Animals," a famous collection of natural history and lore</note>, who writes almost exactly this about this bird in his third book on animals. "Crows," he says, "are most faithful to one another, and once they have entered into a partnership, they love each other with the greatest devotion. Nor would anyone see this kind of animal mating licentiously or at random. Indeed, those who are experts in such matters even say that if one of the pair dies, the survivor lives on as a widow. Furthermore, I also hear that among the ancients, it was customary to invoke the crow at weddings after the wedding hymn <note>original: "hymenaeum"</note>, and to offer it as a symbol of concord to those who had joined together for the sake of having children. Additionally, those who observe the perches and flights of birds <note>Augurs, who interpreted the will of the gods through bird behavior</note> say that for those seeking omens, the presence of only a single crow is an inauspicious sign." So says Aelian. But also, Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" (1449–1492), the Florentine ruler and celebrated patron of Renaissance humanism</note> recently showed me two gold coins of the Empress Faustina <note>Likely Faustina the Elder or Younger, Roman Empresses frequently depicted on coinage associated with "Concordia" or marital harmony</note> featuring a very clear little image with the actual inscription of "Concord."

...there are in it corrupt, faulty, and rashly written things due to the ignorance of scribes. But you may find the argument of the work itself partly in the Latin author Hyginus, and partly in the Greek commentators on Aratus, namely Theon and Achilles. Indeed, the Greek elegy of Callimachus itself <note>Callimachus was a renowned Greek poet of the 3rd century BCE; this refers to his poem "The Lock of Berenice."</note> does not survive to our time as far as I have discovered, but has been lost to the ages. However, in Theon, that same interpreter of Aratus, I find its beginning to be thus:

*Either Conon saw me, the lock of Berenice, in the air, or she herself dedicated me to all the gods.* <note>original: ἤ με κόνων ἔβλεψεν ἐν ἠέρι τὸν βερενίκης βόστρυχον, ἤ κείνη πᾶσιν ἔθηκε θεοῖς. This refers to the astronomer Conon identifying a new constellation as the queen's hair.</note>

Furthermore, in the commentaries on Apollonius Rhodius <margin>Commentaries on Apollonius</margin> collected from Lucilius Tarrheus, Sophocleus, and Theon, where the Chalybes <term>Chalybes: An ancient people of Asia Minor legendary for their early mastery of iron-working and smithing.</term> are discussed in the second book, one may find it written thus: *Callimachus also mentions them, praying that the race of the Chalybes might perish, those who brought forth from afar that evil plant [iron] which they revealed.* <note>original: μέμνηται αὐτῶν καὶ καλλίμαχος χαλύβων ὡς ἀπόλοιτο γένος τηλόθεν ἀντέλλοντα κακὸν φυτὸν οἳ μιν ἔφηναν.</note> From this, who would doubt that the verse should be read as: **Jupiter, may the whole race of the Chalybes perish**? <note>original: Iuppiter ut Chalybon omne genus pereat.</note> This is so that "Chalybon" is spoken in the Greek manner. Some unlearnedly read this as "of weapons" <note>original: Telorum</note>. The most common manuscripts retain "of the heavenly ones" <note>original: celitum</note>, which even goes against the rules of poetic meter. But I know that this observation of ours has already been made widely known for some time; however, that very learned man Pico <note>likely referring to the famous scholar Giovanni Pico della Mirandola</note>, from wherever he may be, knows it originated from us and freely acknowledges it.

# BOOK
## THAT **OARION** IS THE CORRECT READING
->in the works of Catullus, which those who corrupt good books read as **Aorion**<-

### CHAPTER LXIX.

<margin>Passage of Catullus</margin>
In that same elegy by Catullus <note>This refers to Poem 66, "The Lock of Berenice," which Catullus translated into Latin from a Greek original by Callimachus.</note> adapted from Callimachus, the name **Oarion** is read in place of what would normally be **Orion**. Since some have now begun to rashly attack this phrasing, which until now remained whole and unviolated, I must take a stand—with "every foot," as the saying goes <note>original Latin: "omni pede"; a common idiom meaning to use all one's effort or resources.</note>—against this clumsy boldness of the unlearned. I do so by the authority of Callimachus himself, who says the following in his *Hymn to Diana*, which still survives today:

*For neither Otus nor indeed Oarion sought a happy marriage.* <note>original Greek: οὐδὲ γὰρ ὦτος οὐδὲ μὴν ὠαρίων ἀγαθὸν γάμον ἐμνήστευσαν.</note>

But Nicander also writes similarly in his book *Theriaca* <note>A Greek poem about venomous animals and their cures.</note>:

*...bringing a cruel fate upon the Boeotian Oarion.* <note>original Greek: βοιωτῷ τύχουσα κακὸν μόρον ὠαρίωνι.</note>

And Pindar in his *Isthmian Odes*:

*For he did not receive a nature like that of Oarion.* <note>original Greek: οὐ γὰρ φύσιν ὠαριωνείαν ἔλαχεν.</note>

And elsewhere:

*That the mountain Oarion travels not far from the Pleiades.* <note>original Greek: ὀρείαν πελειάδων μὴ τηλόθι ὠαρίωνα νεῖσθαι.</note>

For this reason, Eustathius <note>A major 12th-century Byzantine scholar and commentator on Homer.</note> suggests in his fifth commentary on the *Odyssey* that the name is derived from the word *oarizein* <note>original Greek: παρὰ τὸ ὀαρίζειν; the verb means to converse, dally, or chat intimately.</note>. Therefore, the true reading is not **Aorion**, but **Oarion**.

<summary>This page presents a philological defense of the spelling "Oarion" in the poetry of Catullus, citing several ancient Greek authorities like Callimachus and Pindar to prove the spelling's legitimacy against contemporary critics.</summary>
<keywords>Catullus, Callimachus, Orion, Oarion, philology, Pindar, Nicander, Eustathius, textual criticism, Greek poetry</keywords>

->MISCELLANIES. 86<-

->## ON WHAT THE SYMBOLS OF THE CAP AND DAGGERS SIGNIFY ON THE COIN OF BRUTUS.<-

->### CHAPTER LXX.<-

<margin>An image of Brutus marked with a cap and two small daggers.</margin>

If anyone should find a coin of Brutus engraved with his likeness, and furthermore with a cap and two small daggers, let him no longer labor in explaining the reasoning and cause behind it; let him instead read the forty-seventh book of Dio's <note>Cassius Dio (c. 155–235 AD), a Roman consul and historian who wrote a massive history of Rome in Greek.</note> *Histories*. For from these symbols, Dio says, Brutus signified that the fatherland had been liberated by himself and Cassius. Indeed, it is a fact too common and manifest to need confirmation from other authorities that the cap <term>pileus: a brimless felt cap given to Roman slaves upon their legal emancipation; it became a universal symbol of Liberty</term> was customarily worn by those who had attained their freedom.

->## SOMETHING COMMONLY READ IN VIRGIL THAT IS CONTRARY TO THE RULES AND THE LOGIC OF METER.<-

<margin>A passage from Virgil.</margin>

It is shameful to report how manifest an error has taken root in the manuscripts of Virgil <note>The author uses the name "Maro," the family name of Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> in the eighth book of the *Aeneid*, yet it has not been noticed by anyone (to my knowledge) until we pointed it out:

The author is concerned with the word *potestur*, an archaic passive form of "to be able," which he likely considers a corruption or a grammatical anomaly in this context.</note>

<meta>This page continues a philological discussion regarding archaic Latin verb forms and poetic meter in classical texts, specifically focusing on a manuscript find that clarifies a reading in Virgil.</meta>

->BOOK<-

...in a library, remarkably old and written in large characters, you would not find the word "it is possible" <note>original: *poteſtur*; an archaic passive form of the verb "to be able"</note>, but rather a more common word. That this is the truer reading is proven by the fact that <margin>amber</margin> <note>original: *electrum*; a naturally occurring alloy of gold and silver, or amber</note> is everywhere pronounced with a long first syllable, among both the Greeks and our own writers. Nor do the commentators say anything further regarding this word *poteſtur*, which is otherwise new and unusual. 

<margin>It is possible.</margin> It does not, however, escape my notice that the ancients also used this form, such as Pacuvius in his play *Chryses*, and even Lucretius; similarly, the form "it was possible" <note>original: *poteratur*</note> was read by Nonius Marcellus <note>a 4th-century Roman grammarian</note> in the annals of Caecilius. 

<margin>Another passage of Virgil.</margin> But another passage in the same poet likewise suffers from a similar fault:

->*The scepters of the Palatine, and he sought the seat of Evander.*<-
<note>original Latin: *Sceptra Palatini, ſedemq; petiuit Euandri.* From Virgil's Aeneid.</note>

In that same volume, one finds written not the three-syllable word "he sought" <note>original: *petiuit*</note> but the two-syllable "he seeks" <note>original: *petit*</note>. The force of the meter compels this; otherwise, the first syllable of <margin>Evander</margin> <note>a heroic figure in Roman mythology</note> would be short, against all reason and custom. In truth, there is a <term>spondee</term> <note>a metrical foot consisting of two long syllables</note> in the fifth position of both verses. In the word *petit*, that second syllable is rightly lengthened, either because it falls at the end of a word or because it has been contracted from two syllables into one. 

<margin>A syllable ending in T is "common."</margin> Furthermore, Martianus Capella <note>a late Roman author known for his work on the liberal arts</note> teaches that any syllable ending in the letter "T" is "common" <note>meaning it can be treated as either long or short in poetry</note>, as we see in that line of Virgil:

->*For the sword of Evander struck off your head, Thymbrus.*<-
<note>original Latin: *Nam tibi Thymbre caput Euandrius abſtulit enſis.* From Virgil's Aeneid, Book 10.</note>

<summary>This page features a scholarly debate over the correct spelling and poetic scanning of words in ancient manuscripts, specifically arguing for certain archaic verb forms based on the requirements of Latin meter.</summary>
<keywords>Virgil, Latin grammar, poetic meter, philology, Evander, spondee, Martianus Capella, ancient manuscripts</keywords>

MISCELLANY. 67

->**ON THE PHILYRA AND**<-
->**ribboned crowns.**<-
->CHAPTER LXXII.<-

I see that what the <term>philyra</term><note>The inner bark of the linden (lime) tree, often used for making ribbons or writing material.</note> actually is remains unknown to many and is even wrongly understood by several commentators. <margin>Philyra</margin> For Horace uses that word in the first book of his *Odes* as follows:
"Woven wreaths bound with <term>philyra</term> <note>original Latin: "nexæ philyra coronæ"</note> displease me." <margin>Horace, Book 1</margin>
And Ovid says the same in the fifth book of the *Fasti*:
"The drunken guest dances, his hair bound with <term>philyra</term>."
From this, it is clear that its use among the ancients in making garlands was very well known. Now, first of all, if you compare what Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle, often called the "father of botany."</note> writes about the <term>philyra</term> in the third book of *On Plants*, or what Pliny relates in the sixteenth book of his *Natural History*, you will certainly recognize that the tree we call the <term>tilia</term> <note>The linden or lime tree.</note> is the same as what the Greeks call the <term>philyra</term>. <margin>Linden tree</margin> Even Theodore Gaza, <note>A 15th-century Greek scholar and translator influential in the Renaissance.</note> as learned and diligent a man as he was, translates it this way. Furthermore, certain thin layers or "tunics" from the inner bark of the linden tree itself—or made to look like it—were used to make either <term>lemnisci</term> <margin>Ribbons</margin> (that is, small ribbons hanging from crowns) or little books; these were specifically called <term>philyrae</term> using the Greek term. Pliny testifies to some of this in the same book with these words: "Between the bark and the wood are thin layers consisting of multiple membranes, from which linden-wood bands are produced; the thinnest of these..."

<summary>On this page, the author clarifies the identity of the "philyra" tree mentioned by classical poets, identifying it as the linden (tilia) tree whose bark was used for decorative ribbons on crowns.</summary>
<keywords>philyra, linden tree, tilia, Horace, Ovid, Pliny the Elder, garlands, ancient botany, ribbons, Theodore Gaza</keywords>

# MISCELLANIES. 88

In the second book of *The Marriage* <note>original: "nuptijs secūdo"; referring to Martianus Capella’s 5th-century work *The Marriage of Philology and Mercury*, a foundational text for the liberal arts</note>, he says: "One could see what books, and what great volumes, works of how many languages flowed from the mouth of the maiden." Some appeared to be made of papyrus, which had been coated with cedar oil <note>Cedar oil was applied to ancient scrolls to protect them from insects and decay</note>. Other books were wrapped in linen <note>term: carbasinis (fine linen or flax)</note> volumes, and many were also made from the hides of sheep <note>term: parchment</note>. Only a few were noted down on the bark of the linden tree <note>term: philyræ (the inner bark of the lime or linden tree, used as a writing surface)</note>.

Then you may also find these words of Ulpian <note>Domitius Ulpianus, a celebrated Roman jurist whose writings made up a large part of the Justinian *Digest*</note> in the section of the *Digests* concerning legacies and trusts: "Under the name of 'books' are included all volumes, whether they are on paper <note>meaning papyrus</note>, or on parchment, or on any other material. But even if they are on linden bark <note>original: "philyra, aut tilia"</note>, as some people produce them, or on some other leather, the same must be said."

Finally, Eustathius <note>A 12th-century scholar and archbishop known for his extensive commentaries on Homer</note>, in his second commentary on Homer’s *Odyssey*, having first made mention of nautical ropes, says that the bark of the linden tree—which he calls *philyrā* in Greek—provides a great deal of material for that kind of rope.

However, it seems to have become accepted from this that they also call individual skins, or layers, or even small membranes of material different from the linden tree by the name *philyras* or *philuras* <margin>Philuræ.</margin>, with the Greek *y* changed into *u* according to the old and common custom of the Latin speakers. For example, Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*</note>, discussing papyrus in his thirteenth book, says: "From it, papers are prepared, divided by a needle into very thin but very wide strips <note>original: "philuras"</note>."

For that other mention by Pliny in book fourteen certainly does not pertain to explaining these things, where he says: "Linden bark <margin>Example of salt</margin> and fine flour <note>term: polline (fine mill-dust or flour)</note> can remove excessive salt from foods." <margin>removal.</margin>

THE WORD WHICH IS

### CHAPTER LXXIII.

**W**hat we are now about to relate will perhaps be mocked and hissed at by some as being uncertain, wavering, and not yet sufficiently investigated. But I believed I had to speak of it, so that this trace of antiquity—already nearly abolished—would not be extinguished, whatever its value may be. Four years ago in Rome, Manilius Rallus—a Greek man, but exceptionally well-versed in Latin literature—showed me a certain fragment of <margin>The work of Festus Pompeius</margin> Sextus Pompeius Festus (for so it was titled). It was certainly very old, but for the most part mutilated and gnawed away by mice. 

This work held my attention intensely, because those remains, such as they were, survived from the original, complete volume that the author Festus had composed. They did not come from this current abridgment <note>The author is likely complaining about the "Epitome" written by Paul the Deacon in the 8th century, which summarized Festus's much larger 2nd-century encyclopedia of Roman life and language</note> which we now have in our hands—a version that is forced, violent, and cut short, clearly by some ignoble and unlearned person who, for that reason, has not served the cause of literature very well. Pomponius Laetus <margin>Pomponius Laetus</margin> <note>A famous Italian humanist and founder of the Roman Academy (1428–1498)</note>—a man most expert in antiquity and fine literature—had also kept for himself some small pages from that same fragment, which he likewise [offered] to me for reading and transcribing...

...and gave that. From that reading, I certainly suspected that I would restore a word of Catullus to its rightful place, as if by right of return <term>postliminio</term> <note>A legal term referring to the restoration of rights to a person returning from exile or captivity; here used metaphorically for restoring a word to its proper text.</note> <margin>A passage of Catullus</margin>. In this place, the old manuscript has an explanation that is clearly gnawed away around the edges, but in such a way that it appears from the poorly connected remains of the letters that Catullus is also brought in after Ennius, and that verse is cited which is now erroneously read as follows:

*In the ditch of Ligurius he lies, separated by the axe.* <note>original: *In fossa Ligurii iacet separata securi.*</note>

For while I would not rashly claim anything for myself regarding the rest of the poem, I certainly seemed, by sniffing out each syllable at the time, to be able to offer as a certainty that *expernata* <note>A rare word meaning "hamstrung" or "deprived of legs," derived from *perna* (leg/ham).</note> <margin>Expernata</margin> should be read in it, not *separata*—at least as much as I could gather from those few scraps <note>original: *minutalibus*, literally "minced meat" or small trifles.</note> or shavings, and as much as I could divine from the almost obliterated tracks. But if the truth of this seems to anyone to still be hanging in the balance, I would rather it hang than fall or lie flat. We certainly place some firm support in it, and I do not think we do so unjustly.

Zeuxis, that most renowned painter from Heraclea, painted a portrait of Helen for the people of Croton <note>A Greek city in Southern Italy famous for its school of philosophy and athletes.</note> which was exceedingly praised; Cicero wrote most excellently about this in his work on rhetoric against Hermagoras <note>A reference to Cicero’s *De Inventione*, where he describes Zeuxis selecting the five most beautiful virgins of the city to model for the composite beauty of Helen.</note>. What we read there did not seem at all unworthy of being repeated here. 

<margin>Giovanni Lorenzo of Venice.</margin>
Aelian says that Zeuxis would admit no one to view the painting unless they had paid a fee <note>original: "manu pretium," a price in hand</note>. Because of this, even in those days, the portrait was commonly called "Helen the Prostitute," because she was being used for profit.

->MISCELLANIES. 92<-

...we expend effort on things so frivolous and slight, as if we were wasting time—that most precious commodity—on superfluities. Yet, if those critics had only read that the greatest men once composed entire volumes concerning even single letters, and that Caesar himself <note>Julius Caesar wrote a lost grammatical treatise titled *De Analogia* (On Analogy).</note> did not fear to descend into such minutiae, they might perhaps grant me pardon. I am not a man overwhelmed by business, yet if occasionally, among my higher studies, I should treat matters of this kind <margin>Of letters</margin>—small indeed, but matters by <margin>clarified.</margin> which even great things are assisted—I do not think I am rightly rebuked. I pursue these things not as one moving from leisure to study, but as one returning from study to leisure. I do not so much rush into them by profession as stumble upon them by chance; just as if, while walking comfortably along the seashore, I were to casually gather shells.

Nor, however, do I raise my eyebrows in pride over this, nor would I easily allow myself to be judged by it. I freely confess that these matters are not only "Apinae and Tricae" <margin>Apinae.</margin> <note>original: "Apinas... et tricas." A proverbial Latin expression meaning "trifles" or "nonsense," originating from two vanished towns in Apulia.</note> <margin>Tricae.</margin> as the playful proverb goes, but are indeed almost nothing, and nothing new at that. Yet, it is better to do even this "nothing" than to follow the example of my critics: yawning endlessly, grunting foully in the mud, or doing nothing at all—lest by doing absolutely nothing, we learn how to do things badly.

Therefore, I must provide evidence, lest my reliability falter anywhere, and so that nothing remains unready for those who wish to learn. Therefore, as long as neither weightier testimony nor stronger reasoning is presented, one must certainly stand by ancient custom rather than recent ignorance, regarding where this medley of speech <note>original: "fartago loquendi"; a colorful term for a confused mixture of language or dialects</note> entered into the languages. For indeed, a great many of us younger men are blinded to the sight of the truth. But to follow up on the rest: we also noticed the name *Verginius*—a name closely related to this one, not *Virginius*—recently carved on a marble basin in front of the temple <note>Referring here to the Basilica of Santa Maria Maggiore</note> of the Blessed Mary Major in Rome.

# A NEGATION FALSELY ADDED
### in the first volume of the Digests, in the chapter concerning the office of the proconsul and legate. Chapter 78.

<margin>Location in the Digest</margin> **P**aulus the legal expert <term>jurisconsult; an authorized interpreter of Roman law</term> writes as follows in the second book on the Edict: "A legate <term>an assistant to a provincial governor</term> to whom jurisdiction has been mandated has the right to appoint a judge." But these words in the first volume of the Digests, in the chapter where the office of the proconsul and legate is treated, you may find in most codices <note>handwritten manuscript books</note> with a negation wrongly added, in this manner: "A legate to whom jurisdiction has been mandated does *not* have the right to appoint a judge." However, in those <margin>The Archetypal Pandects</margin> Florentine Pandects <note>The *Littera Florentina*, a famous 6th-century manuscript of Justinian's Digest preserved in Florence, considered the most authoritative source for Roman law</note>, which we believe to be the originals <note>original: "archetypas"</note>, there is no negation at all. Thus it happens that the Florentine interpreter of laws, Accursius <note>A 13th-century jurist whose "Great Gloss" became the standard interpretation of Roman law in medieval Europe</note>, having himself obtained a faulty codex, tortured himself—I might almost say—miserably <note>The author suggests Accursius struggled to explain a law that was logically broken by a typo</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the philological evidence for the spelling of the name 'Vergilius' and identifies a significant transcription error in common legal texts of the Digest, where a 'not' was erroneously added, confusing generations of legal scholars including Accursius.</summary>
<keywords>Vergilius, Roman Law, Digest, Pandects, Accursius, philology, textual criticism, Florence, Santa Maria Maggiore</keywords>

**MISCELLANY. 94**

->**WHY KING LYSIMACHUS’S FACE**<-
->**is horned on coinage. Chapter 79.**<-

<margin>**Why Lysimachus’s face is horned.**</margin>The face of King Lysimachus on coins is shown with horns. The reason for this is found in Appian’s *Syriaca* <note>A history of the Syrian wars by the Roman historian Appian of Alexandria</note>. For he says that a wild bull from a sacrifice of Alexander the Great escaped its bonds, but was held back by Lysimachus with both hands and killed. And for this reason, he says, **"they add horns to his statues"** <note>original Greek: "προστιθέασι ἐπὶ τοὺς ἀνδριάντας ἐπὶ τῷ δὲ τὰ κέρατα"</note>. This means that, because of this feat, horns are ultimately placed upon his statues.

->**A LITTLE-KNOWN FABLE ABOUT TI-**<-
->**resias and Pallas, by which a passage of Pro-**<-
->**pertius is explained. CHAPTER 80.**<-

<margin>**A passage from Propertius.**</margin>Propertius, in the fourth book of his *Elegies*, says the following:
*Tiresias the prophet saw great Pallas,* <note>Pallas is another name for the goddess Athena</note>
*While she washed her strong limbs, her Gorgon-shield set aside.*

There still exists a hymn by the poet Callimachus titled **"On the Baths of Pallas"** <note>original Greek: "εἰς λουτρὰ τῆς παλλάδος"</note>. In this hymn, the story of Tiresias is treated in many elegiac verses to roughly this effect: there was a certain nymph in Thebes named Chariclo, who was dear to Pallas above all others and was the mother of Tiresias. <margin>**Chariclo.**</margin> But when once Pallas and likewise Chariclo <margin>**Why Tiresias was deprived of his sight.**</margin> were bathing naked during the heat in the Hippocrene spring on Mount Helicon, and then by chance Tiresias, the hunter, approached the same spring while thirsty,

**O f** <note>This "O f" is a catchword, indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

<margin>Saturnian laws</margin>
Having inadvertently caught sight of Minerva naked, he was immediately robbed of his sight. For it is so ordained by the **Saturnian laws** <note>original: "Saturnijs legibus"; referring to ancient, primordial divine decrees</note>, he says, that whoever beholds a god against their will does so at a great cost. 

<margin>Abammon</margin>
Abammon the Egyptian <note>Abammon was a persona used by the philosopher Iamblichus in his work "On the Mysteries"</note> also writes something similar regarding the sight of goddesses in his letter to the philosopher Porphyry—though Proclus believes this letter belongs to Iamblichus rather than Abammon—from which Virgil also draws.

<margin>A passage from Virgil</margin>
"Throw it over your head, and do not look back." <note>original: "Transq; caput iace, ne respexeris"; from Virgil's Eclogues 8.102, referring to a ritual act where looking back is forbidden</note> This is the same warning that the Homeric Leucothea <note>A sea goddess who helped Odysseus (Ulysses) during a shipwreck</note> gave to Ulysses. 

But let me return to where I left off. When Tiresias had fallen into this calamity, Pallas <term>Pallas: a common epithet for the goddess Minerva or Athena</term>, moved by the tears of the Nymph Chariclo <note>Chariclo was the mother of Tiresias and a favorite companion of Minerva</note>, granted that he be both a prophet and long-lived; she even granted that after his death he would remain wise and prudent. 

<margin>The staff of Tiresias</margin>
Furthermore, she gifted him a staff to serve as his guide, so that he might walk with steady steps. We also touched upon this in our poem *Ambra* <margin>A passage from Ambra</margin> <note>Ambra is one of the "Sylvae" poems by the humanist scholar Angelo Poliziano</note> when speaking of the poet Homer:

->Then she gives a powerful staff<-
->To great Tiresias, who once saw<-
->Pallas naked, and compensated for his stolen light with this gift,<-
->Accustomed to direct his steady steps with the staff as his guide.<-

<margin>A passage from the Nutricia</margin>
And likewise, in the *Nutricia* <note>Another poem by Poliziano celebrating the history of poetry</note>, we alluded to Tiresias through circumlocution:
"Why should anything be forbidden to be seen by him, after he beheld the limbs of the naked Pallas?" Indeed, because of this, in Book 5 of the *Dionysiaca* by Nonnus, Actaeon <note>Actaeon was a hunter turned into a stag and killed for seeing the goddess Diana bathing</note> is depicted crying out, calling Tiresias happy. He says Tiresias was fortunate because, although he lost his eyes after seeing Minerva naked, he at least escaped death, whereas Actaeon faced a darker fate.

Thus far we had written concerning this passage of the poet, when our friend **Pico della Mirandola** <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494) was a brilliant Renaissance philosopher and close friend of the author, Poliziano.</note>—acting as if he were another Cynthius <note>A name for the god Apollo. In classical poetry, Apollo was said to "pluck the ear" of a poet to warn or give them a better idea.</note>—at the very moment these things were already to be printed, plucked my ear and warned me that we should also include the elegy of **Callimachus** <note>An influential Greek poet and scholar from the 3rd century BCE.</note> itself. This is the only such work of his currently surviving among the Greeks (as far as I know), but it is very rare to find. He suggested that even though we were shut out by the unfair constraints of time, we should nevertheless attempt to translate it into Latin as well. 

I, however—who am usually not inclined to write according to another's whim, nor do I trust enough in my own ability to improvise—nevertheless did not delay in taking up that burden in some way. For whenever Pico demands something of me, I am neither permitted to be ignorant of what I do not know, nor to be unable to do what I cannot; this is either because I feel myself most dear to the man, or because I have convinced myself <margin>A witty saying.</margin> that he sees more in my own affairs than I see myself. 

We have translated it, therefore, almost word-for-word—or what is called in Greek *parapoda* <term>parapoda: literally "at the feet," meaning a translation that follows the original text very closely, line-for-line</term>—preserving not only the sense but even the meter (which is most difficult) and the stylistic "colors." And we only now advise the reader that a few things still seem to me to be faulty in the Greek copies, which it was not...

## POLIZIANO, FROM Callimachus,
## On the Bath of Pallas.

**I** Go forth now, whoever is about to wash the limbs of Pallas <note>Pallas is a common epithet for the goddess Athena.</note> in the flowing waters; go forth.
She comes herself; we hear the neighing of her sacred horses.
Go forth now, O golden Pelasgian girls <note>The Pelasgians were the indigenous inhabitants of the Greek lands.</note>.
The goddess did not wash her mighty arms
until she wiped the dust from the flanks of her horny-hoofed steeds.
Not even when she came back, terrifying, with armor bloodied from much slaughter
of the lawless earth-born giants;
even then, having unyoked them, she drenched the smoking necks of the horses
in the springs of the vast Ocean,
until the sweat and the foul droplets and all the foam
clinging to the mouths that champed the stiff bits had vanished.

Go forth, O Achaean <note>Greek</note> women, but bring no perfumes, nor alabaster jars <term>alabastros; small, narrow-necked vessels used in antiquity for holding precious oils and unguents</term>,
For I surely hear the sound of the creaking axle <note>The sound of the goddess's chariot approaching</note>,
For Pallas's <note>Pallas Athena</note> bath-attendants, bring no perfumes, nor alabaster jars,
For no mixed ointments please this goddess.
Neither bring her a mirror, for her face is always most beautiful,
For not even on Mount Ida long ago, under the Phrygian judge <note>A reference to the Judgment of Paris, where the Trojan prince Paris had to choose the most beautiful goddess</note>,
Did this great goddess look at herself in mountain-copper <term>orichalco; a legendary golden-colored metal, likely a high-quality brass or bronze</term>, nor in the waters of the Simois river,
Even though her reflection would have been clear from the heights.
Neither did Juno <note>Hera</note> look, but only Venus <note>Aphrodite</note> viewed herself in the gleaming bronze,
And arranged the same hair once again.
But having been spurred on her course through twice sixty stretches,
Like the Spartan stars <note>original: "stellæ apud Eurotan ceu Lacedæmoniæ"; refers to the Dioscuri, the twin brothers Castor and Pollux, who were associated with athletic vigor</note> at the Eurotas river,
The goddess rubbed herself only with the rich liquid
From the berries which her own tree <note>The olive tree, which Athena famously gifted to Athens</note> had produced.
O maidens, a blush instantly shone forth, like that which the morning
Rose, or the seeds of the pomegranate fruit bear.
Therefore, now bring only the masculine liquid of oil
With which Castor anoints himself, and the son of Amphitryon <note>Hercules</note>.
Bring also a comb made of solid gold, with which she may
Unfold her locks and part her thick hair.
Come out now, O Pallas! A virgin troop is ready for you,
The dear daughters of the great Acestoridae <note>A noble family or priestly caste in Argos</note>.
O Pallas, truly now the shield of Diomedes itself
Is carried, as the old custom of the Argives has maintained.
Eumedes taught this, a priest meditating on things pleasing to you,

When he saw a cruel fate approaching in an inevitable ruin, he fled, and indeed he bore your likeness to Mount Crion. Come forth now, you who conquer cities, you whose heart delights in the golden helmet, the neighing of horses, and the ringing sound of shields. Today, you water-bearers of Argos, do not dip your vessels; today, drink from the springs themselves and not from the rivers. 

Today, you handmaidens, carry your urns to Physadea, or to Amymone, the daughter of Danaus <note>Physadea and Amymone were famous springs in the region of Argos, named after two of the fifty daughters of King Danaus</note>. For Inachus <note>The river-god of Argos</note> himself will flow down from the joyful hills, his waters mingled with gold and many flowers, and he will bring pure water for her. 

Therefore, take care, O Pelasgian <note>original: "Pelasge"; a term used for the ancient inhabitants of Greece</note>, lest you unintentionally see your mistress while she is naked. For whoever looks upon Pallas—the protector of the city—while she is naked, will never again be able to look upon this Argos <note>This refers to the divine law that any mortal who sees a deity without their consent is struck blind</note>.

Now, venerable Pallas, come forth; meanwhile, I shall tell a story to these girls—though this tale is not my own, but belongs to another. 

Once in Thebes, O girls, the goddess Pallas loved one nymph <note>The nymph Chariclo</note> above all the other companions she had. She was the mother of Tiresias <note>A famous blind prophet of Greek mythology</note>, and they were never apart. Whether the goddess traveled to the walls of ancient Thespiae, or to Coronea, or to Haliartus in Boeotia, she was carried across the hills to visit those people, always with the nymph by her side.

->ON OCNUS AND THE LITTLE DONKEY, AND HOW IT IS INCORRECTLY READ AS *ORNO* IN PROPERTIUS.<-

### CHAPTER LXXXI.

**A**Pud Propertius, in the same fourth book of his elegies, <margin>A passage of Propertius.</margin> <note>Propertius 4.3.21.</note> two small verses are read as follows:
*More worthy is he who twists the rope by the slanted mountain ash,*
*And eternally feeds your hunger, little donkey.*
<note>original: "orno." The author is quoting a common but corrupted version of the text where "orno" (mountain ash tree) appears instead of the proper name "Ocno" (Ocnus).</note>
I confess that for a long time my mind had been goaded and pricked by a certain doubt, so to speak, until an ancient manuscript plucked it out. This manuscript Bernardinus Valla <margin>Bern. Valla</margin> <note>A noted legal scholar and member of the prominent Valla family in Rome.</note>—a most celebrated legal consultant and a man of the highest nobility—lent to me in Rome nearly five years ago. There, I read not *orno* [the mountain ash], but *ocno* [Ocnus]. I immediately showed what I had read to two most learned young men who had just then <margin>Just then.</margin> <note>The Latin 'commodum' here functions as an adverb meaning 'opportunely' or 'at that very moment.'</note> visited me at home. It is clear, moreover, that Propertius is alluding to the subject of that "Socratic" painting, about which Pliny narrates thus in the thirty-fifth book of his *Natural History*: "And the lazy man," he says,
<note>The reference is to the mythical figure Ocnus, who is depicted in the underworld eternally weaving a rope of straw while a donkey eats it behind him—a symbol of fruitlessly earned wealth or wasted labor.</note>
P v

<summary>The author identifies and corrects a textual error in the Roman poet Propertius, using an ancient manuscript to show that the word "orno" (mountain ash) should actually be "ocno" (the mythological figure Ocnus).</summary>

101  BOOK <unclear>[possibly 'IX' or 'I']</unclear>

<margin>Ocnus weaving</margin> ...who is called Ocnus weaving the rope <note>original: *Ocnos spartum torquens*. In Greek mythology, Ocnus is depicted in the underworld eternally weaving a rope of straw which is immediately eaten by a donkey; it is a proverb for wasted labor.</note>, which a donkey <margin>the rope</margin> gnaws away. But indeed, this lazy Ocnus (as I see), having suddenly become wandering and fickle due to the inducements of many, now changes his seat daily. Therefore, so that he may finally rest in a certain stable seat <term>siliquastrum: originally a seat made from a solid block of wood, used here metaphorically to mean a fixed or stable conclusion</term> at last, credit must be sought primarily from that manuscript which we have cited—that is (to speak truly) from the very root from which the occasion for this conjecture sprouted. For thus anyone will understand that I am by no means claiming the work of others as my own, but only my own, and that in my books nothing is more important than reliability.

->THAT IN THE **DIGEST** UNDER THE TITLE<-
->**on disclosure** IT IS READ **to another**,<-
->IT IS APPROPRIATE TO READ **to the album**.<- [A large decorative letter 'A' is printed to the right]

### CHAPTER LXXXII.

<margin>Digest, title [unclear]</margin> In the title *on disclosure* <note>original: *de edendo*. This refers to the legal requirement for a plaintiff to show the defendant the documents or legal formulas they intended to use in court.</note>, which is read in the second volume of the **Digest**, the words of Ulpian <note>Domitius Ulpianus, a prolific 3rd-century Roman jurist whose works form the core of the Digest.</note> in the fourth book *on the Edict* are as follows: "Labeo <note>Marcus Antistius Labeo, an influential jurist from the era of Augustus.</note> says that he also 'discloses' who leads his adversary to the **album**, and demonstrates what he is about to state." This is how it is written in the **Florentine Pandects** <note>Also known as the *Littera Florentina*, a 6th-century manuscript of Justinian's Digest that is considered the most accurate surviving copy.</note> themselves. And yet all the books currently in use have *to another* <note>original: *ad alium*.</note> instead of *to the album* <note>original: *ad album*.</note>. From this comes much hesitation on the part of Accursius <note>The 13th-century jurist famous for the 'Great Gloss' on Roman law; he struggled to explain the text because his copies were corrupted.</note>. However, you should understand this as the **Praetor’s album** <term>album: a white-painted wooden board on which the Praetor, a Roman magistrate, posted his annual edicts, legal formulas, and public notices.</term>

<summary>The author corrects a common textual error in Roman law manuscripts, arguing that the term "to another" should actually be "to the album" (the Praetor's notice board) based on the authoritative Florentine Pandects.</summary>
