# MISCELLANIES. 103
## CONCERNING HARPOCRATES.
### CHAPTER 83.

**E**pigram is a form used by Catullus. <margin>Passage from Catullus</margin> <note>original: "Catulli loc."</note>
*Lælius had heard that his uncle was known to scold*
*Anyone who spoke of, or indulged in, pleasures.*
*Lest this happen to him, he thoroughly seduced the uncle’s*
*Wife, and thus turned the uncle into a Harpocrates.*
*He did what he wanted; for even if someone were to now*
*Abuse the uncle himself, the uncle will not say a word.* <note>Harpocrates was the Greek adaptation of the Egyptian child-god Horus. Because he was often depicted with a finger to his lips, the Greeks and Romans transformed him into the god of silence. In this poem, Catullus mockingly suggests the uncle is silenced by his own domestic shame.</note>

It has long been asked who this Harpocrates might be, of whom Catullus makes mention both in this passage and likewise in another epigram, saying the following:
*If anything was ever entrusted to a faithful friend in silence,*
*Whose loyalty of soul is deeply known,*
*You will find me, Cornelius, to be one of those duly consecrated,*
*And you may consider that I have become a Harpocrates.*

However, both in Venice and likewise in Verona (as I have mentioned), nearly eight years ago, I gave public responses regarding him to several scholars. I call upon their good faith and conscience now, lest I should seem to be claiming the discoveries of others as my own. Plutarch, therefore, in the book he wrote *On Isis and Osiris*, <margin>Harpocrates.</margin> writes that Harpocrates was born of Isis and Osiris themselves, after the latter had relations with her even though he was already dead; <note>In Egyptian myth, Isis miraculously conceived Horus/Harpocrates after the death of her husband Osiris.</note> but he was born <margin>His significance.</margin> from a premature birth and with weak legs. <note>original: "debilitatisque cruribus"</note>

<summary>This page discusses the identity of the god Harpocrates, interpreting his role as a symbol of silence in the poetry of Catullus and citing Plutarch’s mythological account of his birth.</summary>

<meta>The previous page likely introduced the Egyptian god Harpocrates, as this page continues discussing his origins, symbolism, and the historical reception of his cult in Rome.</meta>

->BOOK [unclear]<-

Furthermore, they say he was brought forth around the time of the winter solstice <note>original: "brumæ tempus"</note>, incomplete and unformed, occurring when the offspring of the earth are just starting to bloom and turn green; for this reason, the first-fruits of lentils are offered to him. Nor, however, says the author, should we think the god is an imperfect being, or an infant, or even a type of legume. Instead, he is to be understood as a protector and moderator of unrefined and unformed speech, and of that which is not yet fully articulated. For this reason, he also presses a finger to his lips, a sign of taciturnity and silence.

Moreover, in the month they call Mesore <note>An Egyptian month roughly corresponding to late summer</note>, those offering the first-fruits of legumes say repeatedly: "The tongue is Fortune, the tongue is a Guardian Spirit." <note>original: "lingua fortuna, lingua dæmon"</note> Among Egyptian trees, they say the Peach tree <note>original: "Persica"</note> is especially sacred to him, because its fruit is shaped like a heart and its leaf is very similar to a tongue. 

<margin>The symbol of the Peach tree</margin>

Thus far from Plutarch <note>A Greek-Roman philosopher whose work "On Isis and Osiris" is the primary source here</note> concerning Harpocrates, gathered here and there, and at intervals. Lucilius, the Greek poet, also mentions him in an epigram to Dionysius, warning that if one has an enemy, one should not pray for Isis to be angry at him, nor Harpocrates, nor those gods who make men blind, so that he might learn the power of a god and the power of Simo <note>A reference to a specific person or perhaps a character known for being snub-nosed or ugly</note>. 

But Tertullian <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220 AD)</note> also says in his *Apologeticus*: "Serapis, and Isis, and Harpocrates, with his dog-headed companion <term>Cynocephalus<gloss>a baboon or dog-headed creature associated with the god Thoth or Anubis</gloss></term>, were forbidden from being brought into the Capitol—that is, they were expelled from the assembly of the gods. The consuls Piso and Gabinius, who were certainly not Christians, renounced them and even overturned their altars, restraining the vices of shameful and idle superstitions; yet you, having restored these, have bestowed the highest majesty upon them." Marcus Varro, however...

<meta>This page continues a scholarly discussion on ancient theology and the symbolic role of silence in religious worship across different cultures (Roman, Egyptian, and Hebrew).</meta>

->MISCELLANY. 104<-

...in the book *On the Latin Language*, he writes as follows: Heaven and Earth, these are the same gods as the Egyptian Serapis and Isis, and even if Harpocrates <margin>Serapis and</margin> <margin>Isis.</margin> signals with his finger that you should be silent. Ovid understood this in the ninth book of his *Metamorphoses*: <margin>Ovid passage</margin>
*And holy Bubastis, and Apis of various colors,*
*And he who suppresses his voice and urges silence with his finger.* <note>Harpocrates, the Greek adaptation of the Egyptian child-god Horus, was frequently depicted with his finger to his mouth, which Greeks and Romans interpreted as a command for silence.</note>
And likewise Augustine writes in the nineteenth book of *The City of God*: <margin>Augustine passage.</margin>
"And since," he says, "in almost all temples where Isis and Serapis were worshiped, there was also a statue which, with a finger pressed to its lips, seemed to warn that silence should be kept, Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE), an influential Roman scholar whose works are often quoted by later Christian writers.</note> thinks this signifies the same thing: that men should remain silent about the fact that they [the gods] were once human." So far, Augustine. But in the letter of Abammon the Egyptian to Porphyry—which the Platonist Iamblichus is thought to have either translated into Greek or certainly composed—mention is made of a certain god who is as if secondary, to whom the Egyptian language gave the name Icheon; he shows that this one ought to be worshiped in silence. <margin>Icheon.</margin> Porphyry also writes extensively about this same practice regarding the worship of the highest god in his work *On Abstinence from Animal Food* <note>Original Greek: ἐν τῷ περὶ ζώων ἀποχῆς (en tō peri zōōn apochēs)</note>. Furthermore, the prophet David likewise begins a certain psalm in this way: <margin>David's passage.</margin>
*To you, silence is praise, O God in Zion.* <note>Original Hebrew: לך דומיה תהלה אלהים ב ציון (Lekha dumiyah tehillah Elohim be-Tziyon). This refers to Psalm 65:1.</note>
In Latin this means: *To you silence is praise, O God in Zion.* However, the translators of the Septuagint <note>The influential Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> rendered this differently, that is: *A hymn is fitting for you, O God in Zion.*

<summary>This page explores the ancient religious requirement for silence, linking Egyptian deities like Isis and Serapis with Roman interpretations and Hebrew scripture. It cites classical authors like Varro and Ovid alongside Church Fathers like Augustine to argue that silence was a form of worship or a way to protect the "secret" human origins of the gods.</summary>

<keywords>Silence, Serapis, Isis, Harpocrates, Ovid, Augustine, Varro, Iamblichus, Porphyry, Psalm 65, Septuagint, Egyptian mythology, religious ritual</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on silence and religious symbols (Harpocrates) before transitioning into a legal commentary on Roman law, specifically regarding the status of freedmen (libertini).</meta>

->BOOK<-

This is why I believe the Egyptians included Harpocrates <note>The Greek adaptation of the Egyptian god Horus the Child, often depicted with a finger to his lips, which Greeks and Romans interpreted as a command for silence.</note> in their sacred rites: to show that the highest of the gods should be worshipped in silence. Pliny also mentions Harpocrates in the thirty-second book of his *Natural History* to this extent: "Now indeed, even men are beginning to wear Harpocrates and statues of Egyptian deities on their fingers." <note>This refers to the Roman fashion of wearing rings engraved with images of gods.</note> To return to Catullus’s mention of Laelius <note>original: "Lælium"; likely a reference to Gellius, the subject of several biting poems by the Roman poet Catullus</note>, he cleverly ensured he would not be scolded by his uncle. After neglecting the uncle's wife, with whom he was having an affair, he began to perform a lewd act <note>original: "irrumare", a vulgar Latin term used here as a metaphor for silencing someone through a degrading act</note> upon the uncle himself. In this way, he forced him to stay silent, since a person performing such an act <note>original: "fellator"</note> cannot speak.

->CONCERNING FREEDMEN WHO ARE CALLED *DEDITICII*. CHAPTER 84.<-

<margin>A passage from the *Institutes*.</margin>
**T**hese *Institutes* <term>Institutes: an introductory textbook on Roman law authorized by Emperor Justinian in the 6th century AD</term>, as they are called in civil law, were published under the name of the Emperor Justinian, though they were actually composed by Tribonian <note>a prominent jurist and advisor to Justinian</note> and other learned men. They are also found written in Greek with exactly the same meaning. However, certain rites and ancient Roman customs are related somewhat more fully and extensively in the Greek text than in the Latin codex. This was done so that the entire matter might become thoroughly known to foreigners and those unfamiliar with Roman ways and habits. 
<margin>The *Dediticii* Freedmen.</margin>
But regarding those freedmen who were called *dediticii* <term>dediticii: the lowest class of freedmen, consisting of former slaves who had surrendered unconditionally after taking up arms against Rome or committing serious crimes</term>, since they are discussed rather strictly and briefly in our *Institutes*, it did not seem out of place to draw from the Greek volume...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of legal statuses in Roman law, specifically focusing on the different grades of freedom granted to manumitted slaves, following a likely discussion of Roman citizenship in preceding pages.</meta>

->MISCELLANIES, Page 105<-

...the opinion of [the author], I shall explain more at length here, and for that reason more clearly. Among the ancients, those who were manumitted <note>The formal process of freeing a slave</note> sometimes attained a greater and "just" freedom and became Roman citizens; at other times they attained a lesser freedom and became **Junian Latins** <margin>Latins</margin> <margin>Junians</margin> <term>Latini Iuniani: A class of freedmen who possessed Latin rights—allowing them to trade—but not the full political rights of Roman citizens</term> under the **Junia Norbana law**, which was, as it were, the creator and author of their class. 

At other times they received an even lower status and became **dediticii** <margin>Dediticii</margin> under the **Aelia Sentia law**. <margin>Freedman</margin> For if anyone, while serving a long period of slavery, had been punished for a crime—such as being "inscribed," meaning he had received marks or brands burned into his forehead <note>original: "notas & stigmata inusta fronti"</note>, or had been thrown into a public prison, or whipped for an offense and had confessed his guilt—and was then later manumitted by the grace of his master, he was called a **Deditician Freedman** <margin>Deditician</margin> after the example of the "surrendered foreigners" <note>original: "peregrinorum dediticiorum"</note>. 

For when certain foreigners, who were taxpayers to the Romans, suddenly took up war against them in a fit of pride, and then, conquered by force and having thrown down their arms, surrendered themselves to the victors, they were treated humanely by the Romans to the extent that they were granted their lives; however, they were marked with this ignominy, being called **dediticii** thereafter because they had surrendered <note>original: "se dedidissent"</note>. Therefore, this class of freedmen was also named **dediticii** by Aelius Sentius, so that those who had been alike in committing crimes should also be called by the same name.

<summary>This page explains the three categories of freed slaves in Roman law: full Roman citizens, Junian Latins, and the Dediticii, the latter being a stigmatized class for former slaves who had committed crimes.</summary>
<keywords>manumission, Roman law, Junian Latins, Dediticii, Lex Junia Norbana, Lex Aelia Sentia, slavery, citizenship</keywords>

->What kind of games of Ceres are understood in Juvenal, and concerning Memmius, the founder of the Cerialia.<-

### CHAPTER 85

<margin>A passage from Juvenal</margin>
Juvenal, in his fourteenth Satire, says the following:
*Therefore, you may leave behind all the curtains of Flora, of Ceres, and of Cybele.* <note>original: "Ergo omnia Floræ, Et Cereris licet, & Cybeles aulæa relinquas." The "curtains" refer to the theatrical backdrops or stage sets used during the public festivals of these goddesses.</note>

None of the commentators on this poet have spoken at all about these games of Ceres—neither what they were called, what took place during them, who first celebrated them, nor where else they are mentioned. Yet each of these details is well within the duty of a good and learned interpreter. Therefore, we must provide all this information ourselves, so that the meticulousness of our research is not found lacking even in small matters.

<margin>The Cerialia</margin>
The games of Ceres, then, which are mentioned here, were called the **Cerialia**. <term>Cerialia: An ancient Roman festival held in mid-to-late April to honor Ceres, the goddess of agriculture and grain.</term> During these games, horse races were celebrated in the circus. They were first established by Gaius Memmius while he served as a <term>curule aedile: A high-ranking Roman magistrate responsible for public works and the organization of state games.</term>

Cornelius Tacitus, in the sixteenth book of his *Histories* <note>Note: This likely refers to the *Annals*, as the surviving *Histories* of Tacitus do not reach the reign of Nero.</note>, speaking of the Emperor Nero, says: "Finally, they decided to carry out their intentions on the day of the Circus games celebrated for Ceres, because the Emperor, who rarely went out and kept himself shut up in his palace or gardens, would frequently attend the spectacles of the Circus." 

<margin>The Circus Games</margin>
And a little later: "And so that the spectacle of the Cerialia in the Circus, with more horse races..."

<summary>This page discusses the historical origins and nature of the Cerialia (games of Ceres) as mentioned by the poet Juvenal, correcting previous commentators who overlooked these details. It identifies Gaius Memmius as the founder of the games and cites Tacitus to illustrate how the festival was a rare public appearance for the Emperor Nero.</summary>

# ON THE TENTH OR DECUMAN WAVE,
## which very many poets have mentioned.

### CHAPTER 86.

<margin>The tenth or decuman. A passage from Ovid.</margin>

There are verses by the poet Ovid in the first volume of his *Tristia* <note>a collection of poems titled 'Sorrows', written while Ovid was in exile</note>:
*The wave that comes from here towers over all other waves;*
*it follows after the ninth, and comes before the eleventh.*
He is referring to the "decuman" wave, which is said to be the largest. Similarly, eggs are also called "decuman" <note>from the Latin 'decimus', meaning tenth</note> when they are very large. For it is believed that the tenth egg laid is born larger than the others. From this, "decuman" shields, which were particularly large, were also named. So too is the "decuman gate" <note>the 'Porta Decumana'</note> in a military camp, which faces away from the enemy. And just as the "decuman" boundary line in a field runs from the east to the west...

<summary>This page discusses the identity of the Gaius Memmius to whom the poet Lucretius dedicated his work, and then explains the Roman concept of the "decuman" (tenth) wave as a metaphor for the largest or most significant of its kind.</summary>
<keywords>Gaius Memmius, Cicero, Lucretius, Ovid, Tristia, decuman wave, Roman military camp, Roman measurement, De Rerum Natura</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues a philological discussion on the origins of specific technical terms, specifically focusing on the word "decumanus" and its association with the number ten.</meta>

->MISCELLANIES. 107<-

I do not believe it was named for that reason—as if it were "two-handed" <note>The author is debating the etymology of *decumanus*. The term *duocimanus* suggested here is a linguistic stretch connecting "two" and "hands."</note>, as Marcus Iunius Nypsus <note>A Roman land surveyor (agrimensor) from the 2nd century AD.</note> opines—for that is too harsh and forced; rather, it is because it is the longest. For the *cardo* <term>the north-south axis in Roman surveying</term> is already considered halved by the *decumanus* <term>the east-west axis</term> crossing it. But concerning the tenth—or "decuman"—wave, the poet <note>Referring to Ovid.</note> also mentions it in these words in the eleventh book of the *Metamorphoses*:

> Rising more vastly, the force of the tenth wave rushes down. <note>original: "Vastius insurgens decimæ ruit impetus undæ." There was a common ancient belief that every tenth wave was significantly larger and more dangerous than the others.</note>

And Silius Italicus in his fourteenth book:

> Not otherwise does the North Wind rush headlong from the peak of Rhodope; when it has cast itself forth, it has driven the sea onto the lands with a tenth coil. <note>original: "Non aliter Rhodopes Boreas à uertice præceps, Cum sese immisit, decimoq́; uolumine pontum Expulit in terras."</note>

And also Seneca in the tragedy *Agamemnon*:

> This ship sinks under its load, that one submits its shattered side to the waves; the tenth wave covers this one. <note>original: "Hæc onere sidit, illa conuulsum latus Summittit undis, fluctus hanc decimus tegit."</note>

Valerius Flaccus also speaks thus in the second book of the *Argonautica*:

> How great and how often, even by the art of Pallas, the towering swell of the tenth wave fell in vain. <note>original: "Quanta quoties & Palladis arte Incassum decimæ cecidit tumor arduus undæ."</note>

Lucan also in the fifth book of the *Pharsalia*:

> This tenth wave—wonderful to tell—lifts the fated man along with his weakened ship. <note>original: "Hæc fatum decimus, dictu mirabile, fluctus Inualida cum puppe leuat."</note>

...so that even this fact itself is notable and remarkable: that this tenth wave is celebrated so greatly by what one might call a conspiring chorus of poets.

### THAT THE WORD IN CICERO’S
letter was *Philotheôron*, which was later
wickedly corrupted into the name
Philoctetes.

## CHAPTER 87.

<margin>A passage from Cicero.</margin> In Cicero's letter to Trebatius <note>Gaius Trebatius Testa was a prominent lawyer and a friend of Cicero who accompanied Julius Caesar to Gaul and Britain</note>, which begins with the phrase *You know that in the Trojan Horse* <note>original: "In equo Troiano scis esse." This refers to Cicero's Letter to Friends 7.16</note>, near the end, where the phrase *they learn wisdom too late* <note>original: "sero sapiunt," part of a common proverb about the Phrygians who only realized their mistakes after Troy was destroyed</note> appears, the following is found written in almost all copies: *Furthermore, I do not at all blame you for showing yourself in Britain to be no less than a Philoctetes.* <note>original: "Philocteten." In Greek myth, Philoctetes was a hero abandoned on a desert island because of a foul-smelling wound; the comparison makes little sense for a man on a military campaign.</note> I had tormented and exhausted myself for a long time trying to squeeze a meaning out of these words, but it led nowhere. For why would Cicero mention Philoctetes here? 

But afterwards, through the efforts of Lorenzo de' Medici, I obtained that very ancient manuscript which I also cited above, which is far less deformed, defiled, distorted, and muddled than the rest. For in that volume, it was written thus: *Furthermore, I do not at all blame you for showing yourself in Britain to be no less of a **philotheorus**.* 

<margin>Philotheorus.</margin> For a **philotheorus** <term>philotheorus: derived from the Greek word *philotheoros*, meaning a "lover of spectacles" or a "sightseer"</term> is one who is fond of watching or observing. This is exactly how Trebatius had behaved in Britain: while avoiding the battle, he claimed leisure for himself in the midst of arms. From this, Cicero found material for his jest and mockery. <note>Cicero is teasing Trebatius for being a "tourist" who preferred watching the war from a distance rather than participating in the dangerous fighting.</note>

<summary>This page corrects a long-standing error in Cicero's letters where a copyist mistook the Greek word for "sightseer" (philotheorus) for the mythological name "Philoctetes." The author uses an ancient manuscript from the Medici collection to restore the original joke Cicero made about his friend's reluctance to fight in Britain.</summary>

-> MISCELLANY. 108 <-

-> WHERE THAT PHRASE IS DRAWN FROM, <-
-> "We were Trojans," and what exists in literature <-
-> regarding the Spartan <-

### CHAPTER 88.

**W**e were Trojans: <margin>We were Trojans.</margin> this figure of speech is so effective, derived from the custom of that Spartan <term>chorus</term><note>In ancient Greece, a chorus was a group of performers who sang and danced in religious festivals or plays.</note> and adopted almost as a proverb. For as it is written in <margin>A passage from Virgil</margin> Plutarch’s short book, titled *How one may praise oneself without envy*, the Spartan chorus was divided into three parts: the old men, the boys, and the young men.

The old men would sing this: *We were once brave young men.* <note>original Greek: ἄμμες πότ' ἦμες ἄλκιμοι νεανίαι</note> By this, they signaled that they had once been robust youths. But the boys sang thus: *And we shall be much better than these.* <note>original Greek: ἄμμες δέ γ' ἐσόμεσθα πολλῷ κάρρονες</note> By which they declared that they would eventually be far superior to the older generation. Finally, the song of the young men was this: *But we are so now; if you wish, look upon us.* <note>original Greek: ἄμμες δέ γ' εἰμὲν, αἰδ' ἐ λῇς αὐγάσδεο</note> This indicated that they were already what the old men had been, or what the boys hoped to become, and they were ready to put that claim to the test. This same account is recorded in the collections of <term>Diogenianus</term><note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian known for compiling a collection of proverbs.</note>. Aristophanes uses a very similar proverb regarding the people of Miletus in his comedy *Plutus*: *Once, long ago, the Milesians were brave.* <note>original Greek: πάλαι ποτ' ἦσαν ἄλκιμοι μιλήσιοι</note> By this we are meant to understand that they are no longer the brave men they once were.

He speaks of Vulcan at the banquets of the gods, yet says he did not deserve divine honors, <margin>Vulcan as a selected god.</margin> even though Varro numbers him as the seventh among the <term>Selected Gods</term> <note>original: "selectos Deos"; Varro’s classification of the twenty primary Roman deities.</note>. And indeed, I would read and interpret the passage in this way: <margin>Explanation of the passage.</margin> The parents of the boy Saloninus <note>Saloninus was a name traditionally associated by early commentators with the child mentioned in Virgil’s "Messianic" Fourth Eclogue.</note>, whom we are now discussing—treating "parents" as the case used for calling out to someone—regarding this child who did not smile, neither did a god deem him worthy of his table, nor a goddess of her bed. This means that neither the <term>Genius</term> nor the <term>Juno</term> <note>In Roman belief, the Genius was the protective spirit of a man, and the Juno the spirit of a woman; here they represent the divine spark of life.</note> thought him worthy of the breaths of life. This refers to those who did not smile, used as a figure of speech (according to the testimony of Quintilian) for "he who did not smile," or "he who is from the number of those who did not smile." For it was believed that every person had their own god and their own goddess, <margin>Genius. Juno.</margin> that is, their own Genius and their own Juno as the presiding spirits of their life.

Therefore, Maro <note>Virgil's cognomen or family name.</note> indicated these <term>par excellence</term> <note>original: "Catexochen" (Greek: κατ' ἐξοχήν).</note>. For the table is appropriate for the Genius, as in the phrase: *Pour unmixed wine for the Genius*, while the bed is for Juno. Junius Philargyrius <note>A 5th-century commentator on Virgil.</note> also teaches this, interpreting this very passage thus: "When noble children are born," he says, <margin>The ritual of initiating children.</margin> "a bed for Juno Lucina <note>The goddess of childbirth.</note> is placed in the <term>atrium</term> <note>The central hall of a Roman house.</note>, along with a table of the measure of Hercules." But it is also read in Varro that children are initiated to the deities Educa, Potina, and Cuba—the gods of eating, drinking, and sleeping—as soon as they are transferred from milk and the cradle. ->MISCELLANIES. 110<-

Bringing forward the *Phormio* of Terence <note>A famous Roman comedy from the 2nd century BCE</note>, he also cited this very verse of Maro <note>original: "Maronis," referring to the poet Virgil</note>, and reported that Probus <note>Marcus Valerius Probus, a prominent Roman grammarian</note> had also noted the same. Therefore, the table and the bed are proofs of vitality <note>original: "vitalitas," referring to the capacity of a newborn to survive</note>, because they were employed at the very beginnings of life. <margin>Laughter is the beginning for man.</margin> Moreover, man has his own unique property of laughing. Indeed, for one who does not laugh, how can life be "vital life"? As Ennius <note>An early Roman poet, often called the father of Roman poetry</note> says. Truly, you could not refer these to Varro’s gods and goddesses such as Educa, Potina, and Cuba, because these are all female, as Augustine also declared in the sixth book of *The City of God*. <note>In Roman mythology, Educa, Potina, and Cuba were deities who watched over an infant's eating, drinking, and sleeping.</note>

But by a similar reasoning, one may look to Pilumnus and Pitumnus, the deities of marriage. Regarding them—as Martianus <note>likely Martianus Capella</note> writes that Varro says in the second book of *The Life of the Fathers*—if a child born was "vital" <note>meaning it survived the initial moments of birth</note>, as soon as it had been lifted up by the midwife and laid upon the earth so that it might begin its life upright, they also used to spread a bed in the house.

It remains, therefore, that what we have said concerning the Genius and Juno <note>The 'Genius' was the protective spirit of a man; the 'Juno' was the equivalent for a woman</note> should also be supported by the "pillars" <note>original: *tibicinibus*, used metaphorically here as architectural supports or props</note> of authority. Thus Pliny, in the first book of his *Natural History*, says: "Since individuals make just as many gods out of themselves by adopting for themselves Geniuses and Junos." Seneca, in the nineteenth of his *Letters to Lucilius*, says: "However, I want you to set this aside in such a way that you remember our ancestors, who believed as the Stoics did. For they gave both a Genius and a Juno to individuals." For this reason, Quartilla speaks in the works of Petronius Arbiter: "May I have my **Juno** <note>In Roman belief, a 'Juno' was the female equivalent of a man's 'Genius'—a personal protective spirit. Swearing by one's Juno was a solemn oath for a woman.</note> angry at me if I ever remember being a virgin. For as an infant I was defiled with my peers, and as the years went by, I applied myself to older boys until I reached this age." From this, I also think that proverb was born, which says that one who has carried the calf can eventually lift the bull. <note>This proverb refers to the athlete Milo of Croton, who reportedly gained his strength by carrying a calf every day until it grew into a full-sized bull. Here, the author uses it to describe how small vices grow into larger ones.</note> However, if it is permitted, it is not at all unjust for us to offer a critique; that old commentary by Servius <note>Servius was a famous 4th-century commentator on the works of the poet Virgil.</note>—which was held as an absolute truth before we published these findings—is now beginning to falter. We shall leave the remaining points to be judged freely by others (lest we seem to flatter ourselves).

Indeed, I think it should be read and corrected to "among the" <note>original: *en tois* (ἐν τοῖς)</note>, so that the literal sense is: *And in his children, a terror—or if you prefer to say, a departure of the mind—and before his own death on Mount Oeta, an eruption of ulcers.* As for why we preferred to translate it as "terror" or "departure of the mind" rather than "movement of the mind" (as he did), which in Greek is *Ecstasis* <note>The state of being "beside oneself"</note>, we follow the author in the former case... <margin>Ecstasy</margin>

<meta>This page appears to be part of a Renaissance-era treatise on the art of writing and translation, likely discussing the scholarly rigor required for humanist studies. It follows a discussion on the ethics of publication and the importance of thorough revision.</meta>

Let them not relax their focus, nor spare their industry or labor; let them not indulge themselves or yield to the burden of the work. They should look around everywhere, weigh, ponder, and demand every detail; they should sniff out everything, shake it out, examine it, and correct it again and again, returning the work frequently to the anvil. <note>The "anvil" is a classic humanist metaphor for the process of refining and "forging" a piece of writing through repeated editing.</note> Meanwhile, let them consult even those who are less learned, and not despise the modest abilities (if I may call them that) of the humble. For it is almost always the case that we are as sharp-eyed as Lynceus <note>In Greek mythology, Lynceus was an Argonaut renowned for his superhuman sight, able to see through trees and stones.</note> regarding the mistakes of others, yet bleary-eyed and blind toward our own, failing to see—as Catullus says—the bag that hangs behind our backs. <note>A reference to Catullus 22.21, based on Aesop’s fable that humans carry two bags: one in front filled with others' faults, and one behind our backs filled with our own.</note>

But above all, those who write should take care that, while they hurry too quickly toward the hope of praise, they do not thrust their still-unripe studies into the public eye. I confess I am surprised by one thing regarding Theodore: <note>Theodorus Gaza (c. 1398–1475), a renowned Greek scholar and translator whose versions of Aristotle became standard in the Renaissance.</note> why did he write in the preface to his books on Aristotle’s *History of Animals* (for which he is most highly praised) that he was helped by no one? He also claimed that he was not competing with other translators, whom he said it was no trouble to surpass. He said this <margin>George of Trebizond</margin> even though George of Trebizond <note>George of Trebizond (1395–1486) was a rival scholar whose earlier translations of Aristotle were often criticized for being too literal, though he is credited here with expanding Latin vocabulary.</note> had previously translated those same books so brilliantly. By either restoring words he had found among the ancients or by inventing and devising new terms himself—terms that truly reflect the Latin character—George was, I believe, the first of the modern scholars to teach us why we ourselves have fewer names for things than the Greeks do. Therefore, if anyone reads these books carefully, he will certainly praise Gaza less, for Gaza followed almost entirely in George's footsteps...

...he successfully translated it into the Greek language. But in this work also (not to say anything more severe), he is at times a bit too careless. Let this meanwhile serve as proof: where Cicero briefly mentions that Marcus Cethegus <note>A Roman consul and orator praised by early poets.</note> was called the **marrow of Persuasion** <margin>The marrow of Persuasion.</margin> by Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius, the "father of Roman poetry."</note>, Theodore translates that phrase "marrow of Persuasion" as **honey-voiced** <note>original: "μελίγηριν"</note>, as if you were to say "sweet-speaking." 

Yet Cicero himself, in his book *Brutus*, explains this passage of Ennius and the words more fully. He says: "**Marrow of Persuasion**—she whom the Greeks call **Peitho** <margin>Peitho.</margin> <note>The Greek goddess/personification of persuasion.</note>, whose master is the orator—Ennius called her **Suada** <note>The Roman name for the goddess of persuasion.</note>. He intends for Cethegus to have been her very marrow; just as the poet Eupolis <note>A famous writer of comedies in ancient Athens.</note> wrote that this goddess sat upon the lips of Pericles, so Ennius says our orator [Cethegus] was her marrow." 

Thus far Cicero in the *Brutus*. From this, it certainly appears that the passage was not understood by Theodore. For he ought not to have translated it as **honey-voiced**, but as **the marrow of persuasion** <note>original: "πειθῶς μυελὸν"</note>. We will not, however, argue this with more words, because the matter is not a dispute over vocabulary when Marcus Tullius [Cicero] stands as its judge and witness. 

Since Marcus Tullius also cites Eupolis, who wrote that this goddess of persuasion sat on the lips of Pericles—whom Cicero calls **Peitho**, but Ennius calls **Suada**—come then (if you please), let us provide the very verses from Eupolis’s **The Demes** <note>original: "δήμοις" – a lost comedy by Eupolis named after the townships of Athens.</note> (for that is the name of the comedy). I think it will be pleasing to students if we make a sort of gleaning and gathering, or if we present these as planks collected from this shipwreck of literature.

...let us restore some body <note>original Latin: "corpus aliqd' reſtituamus"</note> to the work. These are the ones we might find:

He was the most powerful of men at speaking;
whenever he stepped forward, like the best of runners,
he would overtake the other orators by ten feet. 
Quick in speech he was, but alongside that speed,
a certain Persuasion sat upon his lips.
Thus he cast a spell, and alone among the orators,
he left his sting behind in those who listened.
<note>original Greek: "κράτιστος οὗτος ἐγένετ' ἀνθρώπων λέγειν / ὁπότε παρέλθοι, ὥσπερ οἱ ἀγαθοὶ δρομεῖς / ἐκ καί δεκα ποδῶν ᾕρει λέγων τοὺς ῥήτορας / ταχὺς λέγειν μὲν, πρὸς δέ γ' αὐτῷ τῷ τάχει / πειθώ τις ἐπεκάθιζεν ἐπὶ τοῖς χείλεσιν / οὕτως ἐκήλει καὶ μόνος τῶν ῥητόρων / τὸ κέντρον ἐγκατέλιπε τοῖς ἀκροωμένοις." These famous lines by the comic poet Eupolis describe Pericles' legendary ability to convince and "sting" his audience.</note>

Nor did we draw these verses of the poet Eupolis directly from his own sources, as his works have been lost to time; rather, we received part of them from a very precise commentator on the orator Aristides, and part from a letter of Pliny the Younger. Indeed, Aristides himself, in the oration entitled *In Defense of Pericles*, calling upon the testimony of that same comic poet, speaks thus: "And only upon his lips did a certain Persuasion sit." <note>original Greek: "μόνον δὲ πειθώ τις ἐπεκάθιζεν ἐν τοῖς χείλεσιν."</note> What Ennius called <term>Suada</term> <note>The Roman goddess of persuasion.</note>, I believe Horace called <term>Suadela</term>. "But Persuasion <note>original Latin: "Suadela"</note> and Venus," he says, "grace the man of wealth." <margin>Persuasion</margin> For I do not agree with Porphyrio <note>An early commentator on Horace.</note>, who thinks that "Persuasion" is merely a descriptive title for Venus. For Cicero, in the third book of *On the Orator*, when speaking of Pericles, preferred to use the term <term>Charm</term> <note>original Latin: "Lepôrē." A quality of wit and grace in speech.</note> rather than "Persuasion," <margin>Horace speaks</margin> saying: "Upon whose lips the old comic poets said—even when they were slandering him, as was then permitted in Athens—that <margin>Charm</margin> Charm resided; and such was the power in him that in the minds of those who heard him, as if... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing the quote from Cicero about Pericles' ability to leave a lasting impression.</note>

### WHO THE *CAUSARII* ARE IN THE WORKS OF LIVY.

### CHAPTER 92.

Livy, in his seventh book from the founding of the city, <note>referring to Livy’s history of Rome, *Ab Urbe Condita*</note> says: <margin>Passage from Livy.</margin> "Let a third army be enrolled from the <term>causarii</term> <note>soldiers discharged for medical reasons</note> and the elders by Titus Quintius, to serve as a guard for the city and its walls." <margin>*Causarii*.</margin> You will not easily find someone who can explain to you who these *causarii* are. However, you may easily gather this from the words of the legal consultant Ulpian, in the sixth book of his commentary on the Edict. These are recorded in the third volume of the *Digest* <note>a massive collection of Roman legal opinions compiled under Emperor Justinian</note>, under the title concerning those who are marked with infamy. The words are as follows: "Dismissed for the sake of ignominy; this was added because there are many types of discharge. There is the 'honorable' <note>original: *honesta*</note> discharge, granted when one has completed their service or granted early by the Emperor; and the <term>causaria</term>, which releases one from the labors of military service on account of health." From these words, we can reason that *causarii* is the proper name for soldiers who have been discharged for the sake of their health. <note>In this context, *valetudo* refers specifically to ill health or physical inability to serve.</note>

->## THE VINDICATION OF PRINCE JUSTINIAN<-

### A certain preface cleansed of some vices and errors. Chapter 93.

I am sometimes ashamed of this age in which I was born, which is too unlearned and arrogant, as I repeatedly turn over in my mind what monsters of things and words have occupied even the books of the laws themselves by which we are governed. Just as I recently noted—doubting whether I was laughing or angry—in a letter of Justinian <note>Justinian I (r. 527–565 AD) was the Byzantine Emperor who codified Roman law into the *Corpus Juris Civilis*.</note>, which is placed before the books of the **Digests** <note>The *Digest*, or *Pandects*, is a massive compilation of juristic writings that serves as a cornerstone of Western legal tradition.</note>, written to the illustrious men Theophilus, Dorotheus, Theodore, Isidore, Anatolio, Thalleleo, and Cratino, all professors, and to the most eloquent Salaminius. For whereas the Emperor teaches there that students in their fourth year were called by the Greek name *lytae* <note>original: "lytas"; from the Greek *lytai*, meaning "resolvers" or "solvers" of legal problems.</note>—and likewise those in their fifth year *prolytae* <margin>prolytae</margin>—as it stands in those <term>archetypes|the original or most ancient source manuscripts</term> of the **Pandects**, all current copies now have "goats" <note>original: "hircos." This is a humorous and biting critique; the author notes that lazy transcribers turned the Greek-derived *lytas* into the Latin word for goats.</note> in the first place and "coloritas" in the latter. These are meaningless words, yet they are nonetheless explained by **Accursius** <note>Accursius (c. 1182–1263) was an Italian jurist whose "Great Gloss" became the standard commentary on Roman law, though later Renaissance humanists like our author often mocked him for his lack of Greek scholarship.</note> as if they were perfectly suitable. 

Furthermore, students of the first year were called not "dispondii" (as is common) but **dupondii** <margin>Dupondii</margin> <note>original: "dupondij." A *dupondius* was a low-value Roman coin; Justinian used it as a self-deprecating joke for freshmen law students.</note>, as I find in the original manuscripts—a name as frivolous, as the Emperor himself says, as it is ridiculous. But also in that same letter (to show this in passing as well), some words from the sixth book of the **Iliad** <note>Homer's epic poem about the Trojan War.</note> are missing where the Emperor mentions Glaucus and Diomedes exchanging unequal things <note>A famous scene in the *Iliad* where Glaucus exchanges his gold armor for the bronze armor of Diomedes—hence a "bad trade."</note>. For after that word, this verse of Homer ought to follow immediately, if we believe the original manuscript.

## A WITTY COMMENTARY BY SEVERIAN, <note>Severian of Gabala (c. 380–425 AD), a popular preacher and bishop known for his literal interpretations of Genesis.</note> regarding the creation of the moon and the sun, and the days of the months and years. CHAPTER 94.

He sifts and weighs everything, seeking a way to either elicit the truth, remove obscurity, strengthen faith, or refute impiety.

The <margin>The creation of the moon and sun</margin> Hebrew lawgiver <note>Moses, the traditional author of the Book of Genesis.</note> relates that God first made the sun and the moon on the fourth day, soon after He had begun the world. However, because He had set one over the day and the other over the night, it can be believed that He placed the sun at the boundary of the East and the moon at the boundary of the West. Nor should God (at least as Severian thinks here) have shaped the moon from the very beginning in such a way that it appeared diminished, or "clipped," or thinning, or divided,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the creation of the celestial bodies, specifically addressing the state of the moon at the moment of its appearance on the fourth day of Genesis.</meta>

->**B O O K**<-

as if it were empty, or faint, or drained of light—such as we see it now when it is new, or between its monthly cycles, or waning, or in <term>conjunction</term><note>original: "coitu"; the astronomical term for when the moon and sun are aligned, making the moon invisible from Earth</note>, or even in an actual eclipse. Rather, it was a full orb, rounded in its entirety; not <term>gibbous</term><note>more than half but less than fully illuminated</note>, not bulging, not curved, not crescent-shaped, not halved, and not "silent" <note>original: "silens"; a Roman term for the "dead" or new moon when it is invisible</note>. For that variety of phases was to come later, serving to mark the intervals and distinctions of times, nights, and days. Therefore, when the sun was newly brought forth and entering its morning chambers, the moon was exactly in the opposite region, grazing the western boundary of the horizon, performing its office as an orb then for the first time, and in what I might call its "apprenticeship." But as the sun itself was driven toward the west, the moon in turn was carried by its own course toward the border of the east. Thus, obeying the command, they presided equally over the day and the night.

But why did the moon not show the appearance of a four-day-old moon, seeing as it was brought forth on the fourth day? <margin>The fourth-day moon.</margin> Because, he says, a four-day-old moon would not have been able to hold the highest point of the western limit. Thus, appearing in a rounded form and with a completely full face—displaying a fifteen-day-old appearance on the fourth day—it had, as it were, claimed eleven days for itself. And by exactly that many days does the moon naturally outrun the sun. From this it happens that the moon, as if in good faith, restores those eleven days to the sun by right <note>This refers to the "Epact," the 11-day difference between the solar year (approx. 365 days) and the lunar year (approx. 354 days).</note>. For since the monthly calculation of the moon is twenty-nine and a half days, the annual count of twelve months results from exactly 354 days. This sum, joining to itself those other eleven days which the moon had taken in advance from the beginning as a surplus...

# AN ELEGANT GREEK PROVERB
## concerning guest-gifts, restored to the copies of the Digests. CHAPTER 95.

I wish, for the sake of the legal scholars, to restore a Greek proverb that has been completely removed from their books, but is still intact in the original source, just as it is found in a certain letter from the divine Severus and the Emperor Antoninus. This is cited by Ulpian <note>Domitius Ulpianus, a highly influential Roman jurist of the 3rd century</note> in the first book *On the Office of the Proconsul*, and it is placed under the same title in the first book of the *Digests* <note>The *Digest* is a massive compilation of Roman legal opinions commissioned by Emperor Justinian</note>. These are the words of the letter. Because they are most elegant and full of "good fruit" <note>meaning they are full of practical wisdom or moral value</note>, I believe they should be thoroughly learned by those who are to govern the state. "As for what pertains to *xenia* <term>xenia: ritual guest-gifts or tokens of hospitality, which in a legal context often refers to gifts offered to provincial governors</term>," it says, "hear what we think. There is a Greek proverb: <margin>Proverb.</margin> <margin>Greek.</margin> *Neither everything, nor at all times, nor from everyone.*" <note>original Greek: οὔτε πάντα, οὔτε πάντοτε, οὔτε παρὰ πάντων</note> For it is quite inhumane to accept from no one, but to accept from everywhere is most base, and to accept in all things is most greedy. Thus they spoke. Furthermore, you might interpret the Greek words in this manner: *Neither all things, nor indiscriminately, nor from all people.*

->ON THE GREAT LUSTFULNESS OF MICE, and an interpretation of Aelian's words, as well as a passage in Martial corrected and explained therein. CHAPTER 96.<-

IN the seventh book of the *Epigrams* <note>original: "epigrāmaton"</note>, in the commonly available manuscripts, it appears thus in Martial:

This has not yet been noticed or corrected by anyone until now. It may indeed be asked in what sense a girl, flattering her lover, would call him a "mouse." -> THE WORD 'AUTOMATON' SHOULD BE READ IN <-
-> Suetonius, where it is now commonly read as 'decorated' (*ornatum*); and what is signified by that word. <-

### CHAPTER 97.

[Large initial L] a passage in Suetonius' *Life of Claudius* <margin>Passage in Suetonius</margin> is incorrectly read in most volumes as follows: "If either a decoration <note>original: *ornatum*</note>, or a stage-machine <note>original: *pegma*; a large wooden structure used in Roman theaters to lift performers or scenery</note>, or any such thing did not work properly." However, the true and intact manuscripts have it thus: "If an Automaton <margin>Automaton</margin>, or a stage-machine." Inspect the old book in Bologna from the library of Saint Dominic, or likewise another in Florence from the library of Saint Mark, which the Medici family <note>original: *gens Medica*</note> opened to the public; both are ancient. But even older than both is the one we personally own at home—to say nothing of many others, lest I be caught being too ambitious or excessive in citing evidence—everywhere you will find this latter reading. It appears, therefore, that those things were usually called "automata" <margin>Automata</margin> which engineers <term>mechanici: ancient specialists in mathematics and engineering who designed complex devices</term> constructed so that they seemed to perform some action of their own accord, as if by an unknown cause. Such things as clocks are now...

...or versatile machines, in which we admire little figures running about and playing by some hidden power. Such are also either fountains or fires themselves, which, by a certain artful arrangement and a new appearance, shoot out water or flames at fixed intervals. For we can interpret **Automaton** in English <note>original Latin: "Latine," but translated here for the target audience</note> as "acting of its own will" or "spontaneous." Thus Homer in the second book <note>original: "II. Rapsodia," referring to the second book of the Iliad</note> says that Menelaus came to the feast **automatically** <note>original: "automaton"</note>, meaning of his own accord, not because he was called by anyone, but as if moved by himself. Just as Eustathius <note>A 12th-century scholar and commentator on Homer</note> also explains, who then says that younger writers call things **automata** <note>original Greek: αὐτόματα</note> that happen by some chance, as if "in vain" <note>original Greek: μάτην</note>, that is, recklessly and "just as it happened" <note>original Greek: καὶ ὡς ἔτυχεν</note>. And a little later the same author adds almost the same thing. Such a sentiment also gave the name **automata** to those devices of the engineers. Furthermore, the philosopher Heron <note>Heron of Alexandria, a mathematician and engineer of the 1st century AD</note> in his *Pneumatics*, says that water is pushed upward in a **spontaneous machine** <note>original: "automaton"</note>, that is, spontaneously, from a hollow sphere which he describes; just as he also fashions a trumpeter who likewise turns himself toward little imitation birds **automatically** <note>original Greek: αὐτομάτως</note>. Indeed, he also teaches how to construct a small shrine, whose doors—once a fire is lit—open **automatically** <note>original Greek: αὐτομάτως</note>, that is, of their own accord, and close when the fire is extinguished, so that all these things should be understood in the sense we have described.

->WHAT THE "SILENCES OF THE MOON" ARE IN VIRGIL, OR THE "SILENT VIRGIN" IN HORACE, AND THE REFUTED OPINIONS OF SERVIUS AND ACRON REGARDING THEM. CHAPTER 100.<-

<margin>Passage from Virgil.</margin> **V**irgil, in the second book of the *Aeneid*:
"Through the friendly silences," he says, "of the silent moon." <note>original: "Tacitæ... per amica silentia lunæ." Virgil is describing the Greeks returning from Tenedos to attack Troy under the cover of night.</note>

On this, Servius <note>A famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil.</note> says: "It must be known that Troy was captured during the seventh moon <note>meaning the seventh day of the lunar month</note>, a representation of which has been established at Argos. This is why the poet says, 'offered by the moon' <note>referring to a different passage</note>. And elsewhere he says: 'Through the blind night.' By 'silent moon,' he either refers to the night in a poetic manner, or he spoke based on physical reasoning. For there are seven celestial spheres: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, the Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon. The first, which is Saturn's, sounds loudly, while the others sound less so in their order. This is just as we hear in a lyre <note>original: "cithara"</note>, where the last string sounds the least." This is the extent of Servius's comment. I would therefore gladly ask him why "friendly"...

MISCELLANIES. 121

The Moon, for those attempting to hide or deceive, or whether the "silent Moon" <note>original: *Lunam silentem*; this refers to the New Moon, the phase when it is invisible from Earth</note> mentioned by Marcus Cato and likewise by Pliny refers to that same harmony of the planets. As for the claim that the last string of the lyre <term>cithara; an ancient Greek stringed instrument similar to a lyre</term> sounds the least, this logic holds only if one understands "last" not as the lowest in pitch, but as the highest in physical position, which is called <margin>Hypate.</margin> *hypate* in Greek, from which a deeper sound is produced. Just as from the other outermost and thinnest string—which I would more readily call the "last"—a high-pitched <margin>Sound of the strings.</margin> tone is produced; this seems to sound more because it is stretched forward, as Porphyry says Theophrastus wrote in his second book *On Music*, in those commentaries he left on Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*. He says the deep sound is heard less for this reason: because it is diffused all around.

Furthermore, we read in <margin>The words of Macrobius.</margin> Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Roman writer famous for his commentary on the "Dream of Scipio," which discusses the music of the spheres</note> and others that the <margin>The course of Saturn.</margin> sphere of Saturn, whose revolution is swifter, produces the highest sound. I do not think this would be approved by Plato, either in the *Timaeus* or in the *Republic*, as he writes that Saturn’s sphere is the slowest—though perhaps this refers to the longer circumference of its orbital path. But Aristotle certainly, in the second book of *On the Heavens*, says the higher spheres are slower because they are more held back by the force of the eighth, star-bearing sphere. 

Therefore, to pass over the opinions of Alexander <note>likely Alexander of Aphrodisias, a famous commentator on Aristotle</note>, Simplicius, or even more recent scholars on this matter, if you wish to grasp the clearly true meaning of Macrobius, you should refer it to the daily motion, which is undoubtedly fastest in the highest...

<summary>This page discusses the "Music of the Spheres," debating how the pitch of planetary sounds relates to their speed and position, referencing authorities like Plato, Aristotle, and Macrobius.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a scholarly commentary on Virgil's Aeneid, specifically focusing on the timing of the Greeks' return from Tenedos to Troy and the meaning of the "silent moon."</meta>

-># BOOK<-

...will be found most stirred up. But we, having dismissed this fictitious (as I believe) and least necessary subtlety of Servius <note>Maurus Servius Honoratus was a 4th-century scholar whose commentary on Virgil was the standard textbook for the Renaissance; here, the author disagrees with his over-intellectualized interpretation.</note>, prefer to understand <margin>The silence of the quiet moon</margin> "the silence of the quiet moon" <note>original: "tacitæ lunæ silentia"</note> as the moon itself, which they call "silent"—that is, not at all shining at that time—so that the ambush could be better hidden. This is surely a beautiful and elegant metaphor <term>translation: the author uses the Latin "tralatione," which refers to a metaphor or the "carrying over" of a word from its literal meaning</term> shifting from the ears to the eyes. For just as nothing is heard in silence, so nothing is seen in darkness. 

Pliny, therefore, in the sixteenth book of his *Natural History*, says: <margin>The silent Moon.</margin> "The day of the moon's conjunction <term>conjunction: the phase where the moon is between the Earth and the Sun, making it invisible from Earth</term> is called by some the 'silent moon,' and by others the 'interlunar period' <note>original: "interlunium"</note>." Then, in the eighteenth book, he also says that they order sowing to be done "while the moon is silent." Marcus Cato also writes in his book *On Agriculture*: <margin>Fruit.</margin> "Now dung should be hauled out during the silent moon; likewise, during the silent moon, figs, olives, apples, and pears should be grafted." Finally, he even orders that meadows be manured in early spring during that same silent moon.

It would be ridiculous to refer these passages from Cato and Pliny to the "music of the spheres" <note>The Pythagorean idea that the movement of celestial bodies creates a divine, inaudible harmony.</note>, or to require such convoluted explanations for a very ancient and commonly accepted expression. For my part, I take the "silent moon" here, as I have said, to mean "not at all shining at the time," which was the moment the Greeks had to observe. However, I do not interpret this so strictly <note>original: "ad vivum resecem," a Latin idiom meaning to "cut to the quick" or examine too minutely</note> as to mean the exact moment of the moon's conjunction—total darkness throughout the night—for that is certainly refuted by what the poet says shortly after: "encountered by moonlight" <note>original: "Oblati per lunam," referring to Virgil's Aeneid, Book 2, line 340.</note>. Therefore, the moon was not yet shining when they sailed from Tenedos <note>The island where the Greek fleet hid while the Trojans brought the wooden horse into their city.</note> toward evening; but it began to shine by the time they had already occupied the city. Thus, it was not [too] late...

<summary>The author explains the term "silent moon" in Virgil's Aeneid, arguing it refers to the darkness before moonrise rather than a mystical "music of the spheres." He supports this by citing agricultural and natural history texts from Pliny and Cato where "silent moon" simply means a new or dark moon.</summary>

it may have been, or an all-night moon, then I do not think it refers to the lunar <term>conjunction</term> <note>The moment when the moon and sun have the same ecliptic longitude, making the moon invisible</note> at all, but rather to the duration of time during which she did not shine. For indeed, a single word among the poets often encompasses more than it might seem. It is for this reason, therefore, that in our *Poem on Country Life* <margin>Angelo Poliziano in his poem *Rusticus*</margin> we wrote:
*What the silent one might warn, and what the moon <note>original: "Phoebe"</note> between her cycles.* <margin>The friendly silences of the Moon.</margin>
Yet it is not absurd, beyond the temporal reasoning of the epithet, if you take the "friendly silences of the moon" to mean what <margin>Silence in the auspices.</margin> Cicero writes—that in <term>auspices</term> <note>Ancient Roman religious practice of observing birds or celestial signs to determine the will of the gods</note> "silence" is also the term used for that which is free from every flaw. Now, how beautiful and how "clever" is the comment of Acron <note>An ancient commentator on the works of Horace</note>, <margin>A passage from Horace.</margin> who, in explaining a passage of Horace from the second book of his *Odes*:
        *While the priest*
*Climbs the Capitol with the silent virgin.—*
interpreted "the silent virgin" as if she were "renowned" for the <margin>The silent virgin.</margin> nobility of her priesthood. And so that he might persuade the unlearned of this, he even misuses the authority of Virgil <note>original: "Maronis"</note>, who spoke of "the friendly silences of the silent moon." As if the "silent moon" should also be understood here as "renowned." Evidently, Acron had not read in Dionysius <note>Dionysius of Halicarnassus, a Greek historian of Rome</note> (the author of Roman History), or in Plutarch and many others, that it was entirely unknown to everyone exactly which sacred objects or statues the Vestal Virgins were in charge of guarding. Thus, for this very reason—that she maintained such silence—she is called the "silent virgin," she who alone was permitted to see the Trojan Minerva <note>The Palladium, a wooden statue of the goddess Athena/Minerva believed to protect the city of Rome</note>.

### CHAPTER XV.

Ovid, in his letter to Augustus in the second book of the *Tristia*, speaks as follows:
"Nor did he escape who recently composed the *Sybaritis*." <margin>Sybaritis</margin> <note>original Latin: *Nec qui composuit nuper Sybaritida fugit.* Ovid is arguing that many authors wrote about scandalous topics without being exiled, unlike himself.</note>
Among students of literature, it is asked who this person was who composed the *Sybaritis*, and what the subject of that work might have been. I believe I have discovered both in Lucian, in the speech addressed to an unlearned man who was buying up many books. In that work, it is written to roughly this effect: "Tell me this also," he says, "if that Bassus of yours, the sophist, or Battalus the flute-player, or the <term>voluptuary</term> <margin>Hemitheon</margin> Hemitheon of Sybaris—who wrote those marvelous laws for you on how one should go mad, and how to desire, submit to, and perform those acts—if one of these were now to walk about wrapped in a lion's skin and carrying a club, do you think he would look like Hercules to the onlookers?" <note>Lucian is mocking a wealthy but uneducated book collector, suggesting that owning fine books doesn't make one a scholar any more than a lion's skin makes a weakling into Hercules.</note>

There is much debate as to who this Aristides was, and what the subject matter of his books might have been. We, however, refer the studious youth to the author **Plutarch**, in whose *Life of Marcus Crassus* there are words conveying nearly this meaning: When the senate of the Seleucians <note>The citizens of Seleucia, a major city in the Parthian Empire.</note> was assembled, he displayed the books of the *Milesian Tales* <note>original: "Aristidæ Milesiacon"</note> by Aristides, which were extremely <margin>Milesian tales</margin> immodest. He told no lie in this matter, since they had in fact been <margin>Of Aristides.</margin> found among the baggage of Rustius. This provided **Surena** <note>The commander of the Parthian forces who defeated Crassus.</note> with an opportunity for insult and mockery against the Romans, because even in the midst of war they were unable to restrain themselves from such matters and writings. So says Plutarch. Moreover, **Surena** is the name of a magistrate among the Persians, <margin>Surena.</margin> as if you were to say "praetor," <note>A Roman judicial officer and army commander.</note> as **Zosimus** also demonstrates. And you will find this passage of Plutarch clearly transcribed in many other places, as in the history of **Appian** as well. **Lucian** likewise, in his *Amores* <note>A dialogue titled "Affairs of the Heart."</note>, writes concerning Aristides in nearly this sense: "I was greatly enticed," he says, "by the charming and sweet persuasion of your very immodest little stories, so that I almost believed you were Aristides himself, and that I was being soothed by <term>Milesian tales</term> <note>A genre of ancient Greek and Roman short stories, often erotic or scandalous in nature, named after the city of Miletus.</note>." For this reason **Apuleius** also begins the very epigram <note>A short, witty introductory poem or statement.</note> which he placed at the front of his *Golden Ass* <note>original: "asini sui"</note> in this way: "But I shall weave together for you various stories in that <margin>Milesian</margin> Milesian style, and soothe your well-disposed ears with a charming <margin>speech.</margin> whisper." Furthermore, the people of Miletus were also known for their delights and luxury.

of Seneca corrected, and explained by a more obscure Greek

### CHAPTER XVII.

[Large initial I] In the tragedy of Seneca which is titled *The Mad Hercules*, <margin>Passage from Seneca</margin> this short iambic verse is read:
*High Atlas, may the moon conceive wild beasts.*
For an ancient manuscript from the public library of the Medici family has a different reading, not "Atlas," as in the common copies. <margin>Common versions</margin> The understanding of this passage should not be sought rashly from any source other than Achilles <note>Achilles Tatius, a 3rd-century scholar who wrote a commentary on Aratus's astronomical poem</note>, an author whom Julius Firmicus <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century Roman lawyer and astrologer</note> also cites and praises in his books on the *Mathesis* <term>Mathesis: Here referring to the science of astrology and celestial movements</term>. 

This author, therefore, in his commentaries on Aratus, when he speaks of the moon, writes word-for-word as follows: <margin>Habitations of the Moon</margin> "They say that there is another habitation upon the moon, and rivers, and everything that is found on earth; and they tell the myth that the Nemean lion <note>The first of the twelve labors of Hercules was to slay this lion, which was invulnerable to weapons</note> fell from there." <note>original Greek: εἶναι δὲ ἐπ’ αὐτῆς οἴκησιν ἄλλην ποταμούς τε καὶ ὅσα ἐπὶ γῆς, καὶ τὸν λέοντα τὸν νεμαῖον ἐκεῖθεν πεσεῖν μυθολογοῦσιν</note>. That is to say: *In the moon also there are inhabitants, and rivers, and other things, just as on earth. Furthermore, they tell the story that the Nemean lion fell from that place.*

From this source, we ourselves wrote in our poem *The Nurse*:
*And the Nemean wastes, which told of a*
*moon-born beast.* <note>The author, Poliziano, is quoting his own work, the *Nutricia*, where he used this obscure mythological detail</note>

# BOOK 27

## WHAT "LITTLE BROTHER OF A GIANT" MEANS IN JUVENAL
### Also regarding the phrase "Son of the Earth" in other authors, and the name Manius.

### CHAPTER XVIII.
### Why it is that I would rather be the little brother of a giant.

**V**erses of the poet Juvenal, specifically this one from his fourth Satire, have left every commentator until now stuck in the mud <note>original: "haeret in salebra," a Latin idiom for being stuck in a rut or facing a difficult obstacle</note>, as far as I can see. It seems to me they do not truly explain the text, but rather hunt for laughs with "old wives' tales"—or perhaps they are just making a mockery of it. Indeed, some invent stories about giants being turned into monkeys, while others claim that after the giants were destroyed, the Earth produced a monkey to show her contempt for the gods. Other explanations are even more "chilly" <note>The author is using "cold" in the classical sense of being dull, witless, or uninspired</note> than these, which, like that rhetorician mentioned by Martial, could <margin>To cool down the baths of Nero.</margin> cool down even the hot baths of Nero <note>A reference to Martial’s Epigrams 3.25, where a "cold" (dull) speaker is said to be able to turn boiling water freezing.</note>. Truly, these trifles are said with no sense that I can perceive, and (as far as I know) with no supporting authority.

To me, a <term>little brother of a giant</term> <note>original: "fraterculus gigantis"</note> <margin>Little brother of a giant.</margin> seems to signify a man born of no ancestors, of an unknown race, and obscure; this is because the ancients were once accustomed to commonly call those whose parents were unknown <term>Sons of the Earth</term> <note>original: "Terrae filium"</note>. Because of this, Persius writes:

*Manius is ready as my heir,*
*A child of the earth; ask of me who my fourth*
*Father might be, I could not tell you quickly; yet add one more.*
<note>Persius, Satire 6. Manius was a common name for a slave or person of low status; here it refers to someone with no traceable lineage.</note>

<summary>The author argues that Juvenal's phrase "little brother of a giant" is not a mythological reference to monkeys, but a metaphorical term for a person of obscure or low-born origin, equivalent to the term "Son of the Earth."</summary>
<keywords>Juvenal, Persius, Satire, Little brother of a giant, Son of the Earth, Manius, classical commentary, Roman idioms</keywords>

->MISCELLANIES. 27<-

There is also one who is now a "son of the earth." You see, therefore, even on the authority of Persius <note>Persius was a Roman satirist known for his complex and moralizing poetry.</note> alone, that a "son of the earth" is what one is called whose parents are unknown. Nor should you think that Manius was named there by Persius without purpose. For even if I omit what Pompeius writes concerning <margin>Manius.</margin> Manius of Aricia <note>Aricia was an ancient town near Rome; the "Manius of Aricia" likely refers to a proverb about people of obscure or ugly origins.</note>, even the Greek author Zosimus, in the third book of his *Roman History*, says the name Manius itself was given not because he was born in the morning <note>original: *mane*</note>, as others were, but was named after the *manes* <note>The *manes* were the spirits of the dead or ancestral deities in Roman religion.</note>. Indeed, Marcus Varro’s <note>Varro was a prolific Roman scholar and writer.</note> book is cited several times by Marcellus under the title *Manius*, even touching upon something regarding a funeral in one place, so that I might guess this also supports our view. Cicero likewise, in the first volume of his letters to Atticus, writes: "But I shall write about these things to you more precisely at another time, for they are not yet sufficiently known to me, and I do not dare entrust a letter concerning <margin>Son of the earth</margin> such great matters to this 'son of the earth,' whoever he may be." But he also says the following in a letter to Trebatius: <margin>A passage from Cicero.</margin> "Gnaeus Octavius, or perhaps Gnaeus Cornelius, your friend—born of the highest rank, yet a 'son of the earth'." This has been corrupted in certain manuscripts, and in its place "son of wax" <note>original: *zerae filius*, likely a misspelling of *cerae filius*</note> was substituted, although the truer reading still remains in a very old book, formerly belonging to the most learned man Philelphus <note>Francesco Filelfo (1398–1481), a famous Italian Renaissance humanist.</note>, <margin>Philelphus.</margin> now belonging to Lorenzo de' Medici, a patron of literature, and likewise in another book copied (as it appears) from an old one, which is now in the public library of the Medici family. You see that by Cicero, likewise, an entirely unknown man is called a "son of the earth." Lactantius also sa—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of classical etymologies and historical definitions of status, likely following a section on the origins of the Roman gods or the interpretation of ancient myths.</meta>

->**B O O K**<-

In the first book of the **Divine Institutes** <note>original: *uinarum institutionum*, likely a transcription of *Divinarum institutionum* by the Christian author Lactantius</note>, he uses these words:
**Minucius Felix**, in that book titled *Octavius*, argued in this way: When **Saturn** had been put to flight by his son and had come into Italy, he was called the **"Son of Heaven"** <note>original: *Cæli filium*</note>. This is because we are accustomed to say that those whose virtue we admire, or those who have arrived suddenly, have "fallen from heaven." On the other hand, he was called a **"Son of Earth"** because we name those born of unknown parents "sons of earth."
Thus far **Lactantius**.

But **Tertullian** also, in his *Apology* <note>original: *Apologeticus*, a famous 2nd-century defense of Christianity</note>, writes the following about the same **Saturn**:
"Moreover, he was called a son of earth <margin>Sons of Heaven</margin> and of heaven because those who are unknown, or who appear unexpectedly, are said to have come down from heaven, just as the common people call those of uncertain lineage 'sons of the earth.'"

For this reason, since he preferred to be an obscure man rather than a noble youth—because in former times, reaching old age was like a miracle among the nobility, seeing as the **Roman emperors** were venting their rage against them—he says he would rather be the **"little brother of a giant,"** <note>The author is likely referencing the Roman satirist Juvenal (Satire 4), who used this phrase to describe someone of low or obscure birth who survives while the nobility is purged by tyrants like Domitian.</note> which is to say a **"son of earth."** As we have shown, this signifies an obscure person rather than a noble or a patrician. For **giants** were born of the earth. Furthermore, he uses the "sweeter" diminutive **"little brother"** <note>original: *fraterculum*</note> rather than "brother," so that he would not seem to desire that vastness and massive bulk of a giant’s body, but only the **lowliness of their birth**.

<summary>This page explores the etymology of being a "Son of Heaven" or "Son of Earth," explaining that these terms were used in antiquity to describe people of mysterious or common origins rather than literal divinity. It specifically links these terms to a survival strategy used during the reigns of tyrannical Roman emperors, where being "obscure" was safer than being "noble."</summary>

<keywords>Saturn, Lactantius, Tertullian, Minucius Felix, Son of Earth, Son of Heaven, Roman Emperors, Nobility, Social Status, Giants, Etymology</keywords>

->CONCERNING CERTAIN CITED examples of aspiration, along with an explanation of Catullus’s noble epigram.<-

### CHAPTER XIX.

**Q**uintilian <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 AD), a celebrated Roman educator and rhetorician.</note>, in the first book of the *Institutes of Oratory*, dealing with the topic of **aspiration** <term>aspiration: the "breathing" or 'h' sound added to vowels or consonants</term>, says the following: <margin>The use of aspiration.</margin> "The ancients used it very sparingly even with vowels, as when they said *aedos* and *ircos* <note>original: "aedos, ircosq;". These are older forms of *haedos* (goats) and *hircos* (bucks), where the 'h' was omitted.</note>; thereafter, it was long observed that they should not aspirate consonants, as in *Graccis* and *triumpis* <note>original: "Graccis, & in triumpis". Standard Latin later added the 'h' to become *Gracchis* and *triumphis*.</note>. For a brief period, an excessive use of it erupted, such that words like *choronae* (crowns), *chenturiones* (centurions), and *praechones* (heralds) may still be found in certain inscriptions." These are his words. We believe, moreover, that the following epigram by Catullus is the one most especially cited on this matter:

*Whadvantage* <note>original: "Chommoda". The poet is mocking a man named Arrius for adding an unnecessary 'h' to the beginning of *commoda* (advantages/conveniences) to sound sophisticated.</note> Arrius used to say, whenever he wanted to say *advantage*,
And *hambush* <note>original: "hinsidias". Adding an 'h' to *insidias* (ambush).</note> when he meant *ambush*.
And then he hoped he had spoken marvelously,
When he had said *hambush* with as much breath as he could.
I believe his mother, his free uncle <note>original: "liber auunculus". Mentioning his "free" uncle is a satirical jab, implying that Arrius's family were recently slaves or of low social standing.</note>,
His maternal grandfather, and grandmother had all spoken this way.
When this man was sent to Syria, everyone’s ears had a rest;
They heard these same words pronounced softly and lightly.
Nor did they fear such words after that,
When suddenly a horrible message is brought:

<meta>This page continues a discussion on Latin grammar and orthography, specifically focusing on the history and usage of the letter 'h' and the concept of aspiration in speech and writing.</meta>

# BOOK 8

The Ionian waves, after Arrius had gone there,
Were no longer "Ionian," but "Hionian."
<note>original: "Ionios fluctus, postq̃ illuc Arrius isset, / Iam non Ionios esse, sed Hionios." This is a famous quote from Catullus 84, mocking a man named Arrius who added 'h' sounds where they didn't belong to sound more sophisticated.</note>

It must indeed be observed that one should use <term>aspiration</term> <note>The "h" sound, created by a breath of air.</note> in pronunciation just as one does in writing. Today, the Greeks have retained this only with consonants, while the Latins have not even kept it with consonants. Conversely, the ancient Latins used to aspirate even their vowels when speaking. Concerning this, Cicero writes the following in his work *The Orator*, dedicated to Brutus:

"Indeed, I myself, though I knew that our ancestors spoke in such a way that they never used aspiration except with a vowel, used to speak in a way that I said *pulcros* [beautiful], *Cetegos*, *triumphos* [triumphs], and *Cartaginem* [Carthage]. Eventually, and quite late at that, due to the 'protest of my ears' <note>meaning the public's preference for the sound</note>—once the truth had been wrung from me—I conceded the <margin>the usage of speaking for the people</margin> custom of speaking to the people, but I reserved the true knowledge for myself."

<margin>Triumpus</margin>
So says Cicero. But I myself recently saw a beautiful gold coin in the collection of Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent," the famous Florentine patron of the arts.</note> with the inscription **TRIVMPVS**. I also saw another silver one with the more common spelling **TRIVMPHVS**. Furthermore, Aulus Gellius, in the second book of his *Attic Nights*, testifies that the ancients inserted the letter 'h'—or the 'breathing'—to "strengthen and reinforce" (as he puts it) many pronunciations of words, so that their sound might be more vigorous and lively. Priscian <note>A famous 6th-century Latin grammarian.</note> also says that aspiration is added to vowels externally so that it sounds minimal, but to consonants internally so that it sounds most prominent. Therefore, if you want the wit of Catullus’s epigram to be clear to you, you must keep the aspiration in its proper place...
