I do not mention these things, however, in order to claim that my discoveries should be attributed to me alone, as if I had invited no one else to share in them (for I am not so arrogant). Rather, I do this so that no one might think I am feeding on the labors of others—like a crow or a vulture scavenging the carcasses left behind by an eagle. <note>The author uses this vivid metaphor to defend his intellectual honesty, asserting that he is an original researcher rather than a "scavenger" of other people's work.</note>

## A correction in Suetonius and an explanation of the word *ascopera*.

### CHAPTER XX.

The manuscripts of Suetonius's work regarding the life of Nero are found to be corrupted. For this is how it appears in almost all of them: *Around the neck of another, a broom was tied, along with a label: I have done what I could, but you have deserved the sack.* <note>original: "Alterius collo, & scopa deligata, simulq; titulus. Ego quod potui, sed tu culleum meruisti." This refers to a joke played on the Emperor Nero, mocking him for the murder of his mother.</note> 

However, in Latin, the word for broom—*scopa*—is not used in the singular number; and even if it were used that way, a broom has nothing to do with a leather sack. <note>In Roman law, those guilty of parricide (murdering a parent) were famously punished by being sewn into a leather sack (*culleus*) with a dog, a rooster, a viper, and a monkey, and then thrown into the sea.</note> 

But in the ancient copies, I believe a trace of the original, true reading still survives, written in this way: *Around the neck of another, an "ascopa" was tied.* Therefore, if you write the second-to-last letter, it becomes <margin>Ascopera</margin> **Ascopera**, which I believe to be the most correct version. An **ascopera** is a small leather bag, and the name is a compound word in Greek, derived from the words for "skin-bottle" and "pouch." Regarding this word, the ancient dictionary-writer Suidas writes: <term>askopera</term> <note>original: "ἀσκοπήρα"</note> means a little pouch or a small sack-purse. <note>original: "τὸ μαρσύπιον ἤτοι σακκοπήνιον"</note> Suidas indicates that *askopera* means a money-bag...

<summary>The author defends his scholarly independence and corrects a common error in manuscripts of Suetonius's "Life of Nero," arguing that the nonsensical "broom" mentioned in a joke about Nero's mother-killing should actually be "ascopera" (a leather bag).</summary>
<keywords>Suetonius, Nero, philology, ascopera, parricide, Latin translation, Greek etymology, book production</keywords>

This, therefore, was the traveler's bag <note>Latin: *ascopera*</note> tied to the neck of Nero's statue, a symbol of the leather sack <note>Latin: *culleus*</note>, because he was a murderer of his mother. ### WHAT THE MINERVAL
### or the Minervale gift is, and the opinions of Juvenal and Varro regarding it are explained.

### CHAPTER XXI.

**Q**uoever still worships Minerva, acquired for a single penny <note>Latin: *Quisquis adhuc uno partam colit asse Mineruam*</note>. Regarding this passage from the tenth Satire of the poet Juvenal, Domitius <note>Likely Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century scholar</note> explains it so mildly that he seems to have in no way fully perceived its meaning. He interprets "Minerva acquired" as one whose favor was obtained during the festival of the *Quinquatrus* <note>A five-day festival held in March in honor of Minerva, the goddess of wisdom and weaving</note> so that the student might be able to compete. What could be more absurd or awkward than this? For I ask you, can anyone achieve the ability to compete publicly in eloquence for the cost of a single penny? Furthermore, Juvenal seems to have meant the <term>Minervale munus: a small tuition fee or gratuity paid by students to their teachers</term>, which is also called a "Minerval," namely the small payment for instruction usually given by schoolboys, after...

"With a matching mule" <note>original: "Cum compare mulo"</note> is how it should be read, whereas most have "With a matching hump" <note>original: "Cum compare gibbo"</note>; the reasoning for this is explained at the same time, and the sense is truer.

**Martial**, *Epigrams*, Book Six.

For this is how everyone commonly reads it, and those who are more celebrated in the study of letters <note>Literally "in the cult of letters," referring to the leading humanist scholars of the Renaissance.</note> put it forward as the most correct version. *From these mules you need not fear a fall,* <note>original: "His tibi de mulis non est metuenda ruina / Altius in terra penè sedere soles." From Martial's *Epigrams* 14.198.</note>

*When ordered to perform soft dances.*

This fits the purpose and argument of the poet against a man who, though young, strong, and poor, was nonetheless repeatedly carried in a litter by six men—who were themselves also young, strong, and poor. And just like those mentioned above, he nonetheless gave everyone an occasion to mock him.

<margin>The sacred Hecalesian rites.</margin>

From this, the sacred rite of the *Hecalesion* <note>The Hecalesia was an ancient rural festival in Attica.</note> was celebrated throughout the districts in honor of Hecalean Jupiter <note>original: "Hecalo Ioui"; this refers to Zeus Hecalos, the protector of hospitality.</note>, not without honors dedicated to Hecale herself. They also used to call her *Hecalene* using the diminutive form, because she, in the manner of an old woman, was accustomed to greeting the still-youthful Theseus and receiving him with the affectionate endearments of such diminutives. Since she had vowed a sacrifice to Jupiter for Theseus’s safe return when he set out for battle, but died before he could return, she received this tribute by Theseus’s command in exchange for her hospitality, just as Philochorus <note>An influential Athenian historian and scholar of the 3rd century BCE.</note> has handed down to memory. Plutarch mentions this matter in his *Life of Theseus*. Furthermore, Callimachus <note>A renowned Hellenistic poet and scholar at the Library of Alexandria.</note> composed a proper poem about her titled *Hecale*, as indicated by a verse from a very well-known epigram:
"I sing of the ways of the hospitable old woman." <note>original: "Μέλπω δὲ γραὸς τῆς φιλοξένου τρόπους"</note>
And many authors are found here and there citing Callimachus's *Hecale*. For even Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of the "Natural History."</note> occasionally enumerates edible herbs which that rustic Hecale serves in Callimachus's poem,
<margin>Samphire. Sow-thistle.</margin>
such as Samphire <term>Crethmon: a succulent plant found on coasts, often pickled and eaten in antiquity</term> in book 26. Likewise, Sow-thistle <term>Sonchon: a wild leafy vegetable known for its milky sap</term> in book 22; although in newer manuscripts the name "Hecate" <note>The goddess of magic and crossroads.</note> appears there instead of "Hecale." "Envy spoke secretly into the ears of Apollo." <note>original: "ὁ φθόνος ἀπόλλωνος ἐπ᾽ οὔατα λάθριος εἶπεν"</note>

"I do not admire the singer who does not sing even as much as the sea." <note>original Greek: οὐκ ἄγαμαι τ' ἀοιδὸν, ὃς οὐδ᾽ ὅσα πόντος ἀείδει. This is a fragment from the poet Callimachus, suggesting that a great poem should be as vast and powerful as the ocean.</note>

For this reason, he was compelled to write the *Hecale*. <note>The *Hecale* was a famous "mini-epic" by Callimachus that focused on a humble old woman rather than grand battles, proving he could write long-form narrative.</note> The words of the commentator are as follows: "Through these lines he blames those mocking him for not being able to make a great poem, whence he was forced to make the *Hecale*." <note>original Greek: ἐγκαλεῖ διὰ τούτων τοὺς σκώπτοντας αὐτὸν μὴ δύνασθαι ποιῆσαι μέγα ποίημα, ὅθεν ἠναγκάσθη ποιῆσαι τὴν ἑκάλην.</note>

**Battiades** <term>Battiades: A common nickname for the poet Callimachus, referring to his ancestor Battus, the founder of Cyrene.</term> sounds forth the **Hecale**, and the deeds of Marathon, more loftily than his usual style.

"Nor did the hospitable old woman pour out vain tears." <note>From Statius, *Thebaid* 12.581, referring to the character of Hecale.</note>

And **Suidas** <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> says that *Hecale* is a certain heroine in the works of Callimachus. Finally, Apuleius, in the first book of the *Metamorphoses*, says of the same woman, I believe: "If, content with a small hearth, you emulate the virtues of that Theseus, your namesake, who did not despise the humble hospitality of the old woman **Hecale**." 

However, I am not unaware <margin>A passage of Apuleius corrected.</margin> that the text is corrupted almost everywhere; indeed, you will usually find it written not as "the old woman Hecale," but as "the old woman Ales," with several letters having been cut off. I mention this so that in passing we might also correct the manuscripts of Apuleius, and send the reader away laden, as it were, with an extra measure of knowledge.

**HOW MANY THINGS IN THE**
***Letters to Friends***, **which are now held to be Cicero’s,**
**are out of order, and into what order**
**they should be restored.**

**CHAPTER XXV.**

<margin>The Book of Cicero's letters.</margin>

I have come across a very ancient volume of Cicero’s *Letters to Friends*, <note>Latin: *Epistulae ad Familiares*, a collection of Cicero's private correspondence.</note> which I also mentioned above; furthermore, I found another copy transcribed from that very one, which some believe to be in the hand of Francesco Petrarch. <note>Petrarch (1304–1374) was a famous Italian scholar and poet credited with rediscovering many of Cicero’s lost works.</note> It is clear from many arguments, which I will omit for now, that it was copied from the original; however, this later <term>codex|a manuscript book</term> I mentioned has been bound together so poorly by a careless bookseller <note>The Latin *bibliopola* refers to a book-dealer or binder.</note> that a whole group of ten transposed pages is found, contrary to how they were numbered. Furthermore, the book is in the public library of the Medici family. <note>The Medici family of Florence were the primary patrons of the author; their library, the Laurentian, is still world-famous today.</note> From this single volume, as far as I can gather, it is clear that every manuscript of these same letters still in existence has flowed as if from a source or headwater; and in all of them, the order of reading is backward and distorted. This must now be restored to its proper place by me and, as it were, renewed. Therefore, I ask you to pay close attention, whoever you may be who embraces these liberal arts. <note>The "liberal arts" refers to the education of a free person, focusing here on literature and rhetoric.</note> Thus, in the eighth book, Caelius’s <note>Marcus Caelius Rufus was a politician and friend of Cicero.</note> letter to Cicero begins this way: "Certainly, I say, he has been acquitted; as I stood for him, the verdict was pronounced, and indeed by all the ranks, and even by every single vote in each class."

<summary>The author identifies a major error in the transmission of Cicero’s letters, tracing a widespread disruption in their order to a specific, poorly-bound manuscript in the Medici library once handled by Petrarch.</summary>

"Look now," you say, "up to this point the order has not yet been interrupted." But you will find that the words which immediately follow—*if you show by letters* <note>original: "literis ostenderis"</note>, and so forth—are entirely different from those preceding, and belong to another letter. Proceed further, counting the following letters from that very one, until you reach the twenty-fourth, which begins thus: *No, by Hercules, for nothing ever...* <note>original: "Non me Hercules, nihil unquam enim"</note>. This letter is actually continuous with the one above, so that you should read it as follows: "Certainly, I say, he has been acquitted; while I was present, the verdict was announced, and indeed by all ranks, but also by individual votes in every class." <note>Poliziano is demonstrating how to bridge two fragments that were separated by dozens of pages in the manuscripts.</note> "Look now," you say. *No, by Hercules. For nothing ever...* And so weave the rest together, until from that letter which we said belonged to another, you reach the tenth following, which begins: *So you, do you say, Hirtius?* <note>Referring to Aulus Hirtius, a correspondent of Cicero.</note> Read that until you come across these words: *if any hope* <note>original: "si ullam spem"</note>, which as soon as you encounter them, you shall delete this entire part. Or: *In a fault of the roof, the rest will be approved by me.* <note>original: "Aut in tecto vitii, caetera mihi probabuntur." These are specific corrupt fragments Poliziano identified as being inserted into the wrong context.</note>

All of this, I say, you shall delete or strike through <note>The Latin "induces" refers to the practice of drawing a line through text in a manuscript.</note>, since they are clearly foreign to this place. Soon you will return again to those things which were wrongly placed in that letter of Caelius <note>Marcus Caelius Rufus, a Roman politician and protégé of Cicero.</note>, of which we spoke first, and thus you will continue the reading. If you show any hope by letters, you will read continuously in this way until you reach the twenty-third letter from this one, beginning: *I will respond to two of his letters*, and end it with this conclusion.

...for it was an ancient custom that wine should be poured over the heads of victims while they were under the stroke and the knife. This is seen, for instance, in the works of Maro <note>The Roman poet Virgil, often referred to by his family name, Maro, in the Renaissance.</note>:

Therefore, they destined the Emperor Domitian to death through these short verses <note>likely referring to a famous Greek epigram about a goat threatening to eat a vine, which would still provide enough wine to pour over the emperor at his sacrifice.</note> because, as the same Suetonius writes, during a time of great abundance of wine but a shortage of grain, Domitian believed the fields were being neglected due to an excessive devotion to vineyards. He issued an edict that no one in Italy should plant new vines, and that in the provinces, the vineyards should be cut down, leaving at most half of them standing; however, he did not persevere in the execution of this order. Let scholars remember that in this discovery, we have served the common interest in more than one way. In most manuscripts of Suetonius, even several ancient ones, you will not find these Greek verses—indeed, there is not even a trace or a place where they might belong. But because we have possessed them for some time and found them most delightful, we have carefully tracked them down from obsolete and flawed copies, weighing each detail and gradually recognizing and restoring the letters.

<margin>Whitened hands</margin>
...the slow-moving noblewomen, whom she persuaded with those very whitened hands <note>original: "manibus gypsatissimis." This refers to hands whitened with gypsum, a cosmetic practice of the time often associated with women of status or perhaps used here to suggest artifice.</note> that they should not hold it against her as a fault that she was away from her homeland. For many have managed their own affairs well, and the public's, far from their fatherland. Many who spent their lives at home are for that reason disapproved of. None of those whose opinions have been published has suspected until now that those are not clearly Cicero's words, but rather those of some poet, who was permitted to use words and figures of that sort, such as: "those who held Corinth's high citadel," and "wealthy noble matrons." Nor at least did they see that two whole <term>Iambic</term> <note>A metrical foot consisting of one short syllable followed by one long syllable, the standard rhythm for dialogue in ancient drama.</note> verses still remain intact in these lines, while the scattered limbs of others can be recognized. We have gathered that these words are from the *Medea* of Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius (c. 239–169 BC) was considered the "father of Roman poetry." Most of his work survives only in fragments cited by later authors like Cicero.</note>, cited not indeed in their original order, but as was most convenient for Cicero. For since we knew that Ennius's *Medea* was translated into Latin from the Greek of Euripides, it pleased us to seek out the meaning of these words from Euripides' tragedy itself, since we have suffered the loss of Ennius's version. Thus, in that play, Medea is introduced speaking with the Corinthian matrons, so that she might persuade them that it ought not to be counted as a fault against her that she is absent from her homeland; for many, she says, even honorable men, were far from their country, while many others, on the contrary, remained at home without glory.

<margin>Terence in *The Girl from Andros*</margin>
For foreign women had a bad reputation; hence that line from the Comedy: *Is he so mad, after a foreign woman?* <note>original: "Adeon' est demens, ex peregrina?" A quote from Terence's play *Andria*, reflecting ancient Roman prejudice against non-citizens.</note> Similarly, Parmeno says he can keep silent about the fact that Thais's mother was a Samian <note>A woman from the island of Samos.</note>, then living in Athens. # THE BOOK

## WHAT ARE CALLED PANIC TERRORS, AND THE MOST ABUNDANT WITNESSES CITED FOR THEM.

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

In Cicero’s letter to Tiro, which is placed at the end of the volume of letters to friends, it is written thus: "Even if our friend Atticus—who once perceived me to be moved by **panic fears** <note>original Greek: πανικοῖς (*panikois*)</note>—always thinks the same, and does not see with what defenses of philosophy I am hedged in, by Hercules, because he is fearful himself, **he creates an alarm** <note>original Greek: θορυβοποιεῖ (*thorubopoiei*), meaning to stir up noise or confusion</note>." 

<margin>Panic.</margin> 

**Panic** terrors are what (as I believe) the Greeks call certain sudden terrors and consternations, such as **frantic fears** <note>original Latin: *lymphatici metus*. In the ancient world, "lymphatic" referred to a state of madness or frenzy believed to be caught from water nymphs or spirits.</note>, which are so uncontrollable that they lack not only reason but even sanity. Cicero uses the same word in a letter to Atticus in the fifth book. "For you know," he says, "that certain things are called **panic** <note>original Greek: πανικὰ (*panika*)</note>." 

Hyginus also makes mention of **Panic** terror in the passage concerning the constellation Capricorn <note>Hyginus was a Roman mythographer; Capricorn was often associated with the god Pan in star-myths.</note> with these words: "For this creature is said, when Jupiter was fighting the Titans, to have been the first to cast that terror against the enemies which is called **Panic**, as Eratosthenes says." Germanicus <note>The Roman general Germanicus wrote a famous Latin version of Aratus’s poem on the stars.</note> also alludes to this story in his commentary on Aratus. 

Euripides in *Medea* speaks thus:
"And some elderly servant, thinking that a **wrath of Pan or of some other god** <note>original Greek: ἢ πανὸς ὀργὰς ἤτινος θεῶν (*e panos orgas etinos theon*)</note> had come upon her, raised a cry of prayer." <note>This refers to a scene where a servant mistakes a character's physical agony for a divine frenzy sent by the god Pan.</note>

<summary>This page explains the origin of the word "panic," tracing it from Cicero's letters to the mythological battle between the Gods and Titans where the god Pan first used terror as a weapon.</summary>

<keywords>Panic, Cicero, Atticus, Hyginus, Pan, Mythology, Medea, Greek etymology, Titans, Capricorn</keywords>

->MISCELLANIES. 39<-

Euripides calls these the "wraths of Pan," as found in his most ancient commentator, <note>original: τὰ πανικὰ δείματα</note> **Panic terrors**; <note>original: ὅτι τὴν τῶν αἰφνιδίων φόβων καὶ ταραχῶν αἰτίαν τῷ πανὶ ἀνατιθέασιν</note> **because**, he says, **they attribute the cause of sudden fears and disturbances to the god Pan.** And in the works of Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene, a 4th-century philosopher and bishop</note> in his book *On Providence*, I find it written much like this: <note>original: καὶ πανικοὶ θόρυβοι μεθ' ἡμέραν τὸ στράτευμα κατελάμβανον</note> **And Panic tumults seized the army during the day.** In that passage, he further explains at length exactly what kind of terrors these finally were. Indeed, some commentator or other explains these very words of Synesius by saying that terrors are called "Panic" when men and horses in an army are suddenly thrown into confusion for no apparent reason. For he says that women, inspired by a divine power, are accustomed to celebrate the <term>orgia<note>A term for secret religious rites or festivals, particularly those of Pan or Dionysus, characterized by ecstatic shouting.</note></term> of Pan with shouts, which, being sudden, strike those who hear them with fear.

But also Nicetes Choniates <margin>Nicetes the orator</margin> <note>A Byzantine Greek historian and writer</note>, a Greek author by no means to be looked down upon, mentions "Panic phantoms" in one of his orations. Theon <margin>Theon</margin> <note>Theon of Alexandria, a scholar who commented on the works of Aratus</note>, the commentator on the poet Aratus, also says that the god Pan fought against the Titans, and that he seems to have been the first to discover that twisted and spiral shell <margin>Spiral Shell</margin> <note>original: Cochlos</note> which they use as a trumpet; and it was by his effort that his allies were given the opportunity to arm themselves, while the Titans were driven into flight by that sound—whatever it was—which is called "Panic." Moreover, the poet Nonnus, in the tenth book of his *Dionysiaca*, describing a person in a frenzy...

That too is a sport for the god, when he carries off the trembling
Livestock from their pens, and the fleeing bullocks trample the thickets."

...regarding the daughters of Thespius who were embraced by Hercules. As for why they are called the <margin>The Teuthrantian crowd.</margin> "Teuthrantian crowd," this is a fact so unexamined and unknown to most people that certain grammarians have the audacity to substitute "Thespeian" for that word "Teuthrantian"—an act of great ignorance and impropriety. We have found information on this matter in Eustathius, the commentator on Homer, specifically in the section where he explains the "Catalogue of Ships." <note>Eustathius was a 12th-century archbishop and scholar whose commentaries on the Iliad and Odyssey are vital for preserving lost ancient lore.</note> He says: <note>original Greek: "φησὶ δὲ ὁ τῶν ἐθνικῶν συγγραφεὺς ὅτι κτίσμα ἐστὶν ἡ θέσπεια θεσπιάδου κατὰ δ’ ἔνιους θεσπίου. υἱοῦ Τεύθραντος τοῦ πανδίονος."</note> "The author of the *Ethnica* <note>A reference to Stephanus of Byzantium, a 6th-century grammarian who wrote a geographical dictionary of names.</note> states that the city of Thespeia is a foundation of Thespiades, though according to some, it was founded by Thespius, the son of Teuthras and grandson of Pandion." On the authority of this writer who recorded the history of nations, <margin>Thespia.</margin> he reports that Thespia was founded by Thespiades, while others write that it was by <margin>Teuthras.</margin> Thespius, the son of Teuthras and grandson of Pandion. Therefore, the daughters of Thespius are called the "Teuthrantian crowd" after their grandfather, Teuthras. Finally, even Aeschylus, in his tragedy *Agamemnon*, referred to the city of Thespia as belonging to Teuthras.

->WHAT THAT PHRASE IN JUVENAL MEANS,<-
->"as if you had three names,"<-

->C H A P T E R XXXI.<-

And you would be thrown out of doors if you ever tried to mutter, as if you had three names. <note>The author is quoting Juvenal's *Satire 5*, lines 126–127. In the Roman world, having "three names" (the *tria nomina*) was a mark of full Roman citizenship.</note>
Certainly, the same Domitius <note>referring to Domitius Calderinus, a famous 15th-century Renaissance scholar and commentator on the classics</note> (for I pass over other interpreters, dealing with him as the more stubborn one) explained this passage from Juvenal’s fifth satire in this way: "If you wished," he says, "to speak as if you too were one of the nobles—as if the poet implies that only those who are noble are permitted to speak." 
<margin>To whom the three names belong</margin>
"For nobility was designated by the family name, the additional name, and the personal name. For example: Gaius Julius Caesar." This is the word-for-word explanation from Domitius. However, I believe that having three names was not exclusive to the nobles, nor did all of them use three; rather, I think it applied more to those who were <term>manumitted|manumission was the formal process of freeing a slave; the freed person typically took the first and second names of the master who freed them</term> from slavery. # MISCELLANY. 42

The sense is also more acute and harmonious for this reason: that the host may not even consider him a free man, nor think it right for him to speak as a free man, whom he has invited as his guest. If reliable authors <note>original: "autores haud lubricæ fidei," literally "authors of no slippery faith"</note> do not confirm this, then I admit that nothing is more carefully weighed by Domitius. <note>Domitius refers to Domizio Calderini, a scholar whom the author frequently critiques for his interpretations of classical texts.</note> Therefore, Artemidorus of Ephesus—or if he prefers to be called Daldianus—in the first book of his work *On the Interpretation of Dreams*, wrote in Greek, but to this effect: "I knew a certain slave," he says, "who dreamed he had three male organs. Indeed, having later been made a free man, he had three names instead of one, having taken two names from his patron." Thus says Artemidorus. <note>In Rome, a slave typically had only one name. Upon being freed, they adopted the *praenomen* and *nomen* of their former master (patron), while keeping their original name as a *cognomen*, resulting in the "tria nomina" or three names of a citizen.</note>

Septimius Florens Tertullian also, in his book *On the Resurrection*, says word-for-word: "I ask you," he says, "if you change your servant's status through freedom, because the same flesh and soul remain—which were previously subject to whips, chains, and brands—must they for that reason continue to suffer the same things? I think not. On the contrary, he is honored with the brilliance of white clothing, the dignity of a gold ring, his patron's name, his tribe, and a place at the table." <note>These were legal and social indicators of Roman citizenship: the gold ring was originally a mark of the equestrian rank, and the "tribe" refers to the voting district to which a citizen was assigned.</note>

From this, I believe, comes that line from Persius: *He leaves as Marcus Dama.* <margin>Passage from Persius</margin> <note>Persius, Satire 5.76. "Dama" was a common slave name; "Marcus" is a Roman praenomen. The line illustrates the instant change in status through manumission.</note> Cicero also confirms this custom in the fourth volume of his *Letters to Atticus*, writing as follows: "Regarding Eutychis, I am pleased that, keeping his old name as a first name, his new name will be Titus Caecilius; just as Dionysius is joined from me and you as Marcus Pomponius." <margin>Passage from Cicero</margin> <note>original: "De Eutychide gratum..." Cicero is explaining how freedmen took names from their patrons—in this case, Atticus (Titus Pomponius Atticus) and Cicero (Marcus Tullius Cicero).</note> But so that we may interpret this passage of Cicero's in passing—which is not without its obscurities—one must also know...

The author cites Artemidorus, Tertullian, Persius, and Cicero to illustrate the social and legal transformation from slave to freedman.</summary>

# ->BOOK<-

...that it was the custom for heirs to be adopted into the name and family by testament <note>A "testamentary adoption" was a Roman legal practice where a person designated in a will as an heir was also requested to take the deceased person's name to preserve the family line.</note>. This was the case with Gaius Octavius, adopted by [Julius] Caesar, just as Suetonius also writes in his *Life of Julius*. Therefore, after that day, he was always called "Caesar," as he is also addressed in the letters of Brutus to Cicero. The same was true for Quintus Scipio, a colleague in the consulship of Pompey; although he was the son of Nasica, he was adopted by will into the family of Metellus Pius and was even called by his name. As Dio writes in the fortieth book of his *Histories*. For this reason also, Titus Pomponius Atticus—having been left as heir to three-quarters <note>original: *ex dodrante*, meaning three-quarters of the estate</note> of the property by Quintus Caecilius, as Cornelius Nepos writes in the *Life of Atticus*—was afterwards called Quintus Caecilius Atticus instead of Titus Pomponius Atticus, as found in Varro’s second book *On Agriculture*. Because of this, he called his freedman <note>A "manumitted" or freed slave.</note> Eutychides "Titus Caecilius Eutychides," using his own old first name <note>original: *praenomine*</note> (for he was formerly called Titus Pomponius) alongside the new name, because he had only recently begun to be called Quintus Caecilius. In the same way, the slave Dionysius was called Marcus Pomponius Dionysius, having adopted the first name of Cicero and the family name of Atticus. But also Alexander of Miletus, whose surname was "Polyhistor" <note>original: *Polyhistori*, meaning "very learned" or "polymath."</note>, was called Cornelius by Cornelius Lentulus, by whom he had evidently been freed, as the collection known as the *Suda* <note>original: *Suidæ*; a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world.</note> bears witness.

MISCELLANIES. 43

### A word long abolished is restored to the manuscripts of Pliny; what "poppysmos" or "poppysma" signify, and the custom of worshipping lightning bolts with clicking sounds.

### CHAPTER XXXII.

A passage in Pliny must be corrected, from the twenty-eighth book <note>The Roman numeral in the text refers to Book 28</note> of the *Natural History*, second chapter, where it is discussed whether there is any power of words in healing; for a single word has been removed which indicates an ancient custom, confirmed also by even older testimony. Most manuscripts read as follows: "The worship of lightning bolts <margin>A passage from Pliny</margin> is a consensus among nations." But in two ancient copies from the noble library of the Medici family itself, it is written thus: "The worship of lightning bolts with **clicking sounds** <margin>Poppysmi</margin> is a consensus among nations." <note>The author is restoring the word "poppysmis," referring to a ritualistic smacking or clicking sound made with the lips.</note> Even the most witty poet Aristophanes confirms this reading in the comedy titled *The Wasps*. <note>original Greek: "σφῆκες"</note> For in that play, a certain old man named Philocleon speaks in such a way as to say that he will strike like lightning against those who are making clicking sounds <note>The author uses a Latinized version of the Greek verb: "poppyssantibus"</note> (if I may be permitted to use the word in this way). Then the most ancient commentator <note>A "scholiast," an ancient scholar who wrote explanatory notes in the margins of classical texts.</note> says this was the custom: to make clicking sounds at lightning bolts. These are the words of Aristophanes: "and I will flash lightning at those making clicking sounds." <note>original Greek: "κἀναστράψω ποππύζουσιν"</note> Then the commentator says this: "It is the custom to click at the lightning." <note>original Greek: "ἔθος ταῖς ἀστραπαῖς ποππύζειν"</note>

H ij <note>This is a printer's signature mark, used to ensure the gathered pages of the book were bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page argues for the restoration of the word "poppysmos" (ritual clicking sounds) in Pliny the Elder's Natural History, citing ancient manuscripts from the Medici library and a passage from Aristophanes' "The Wasps" to prove that ancient people used this sound to ward off or venerate lightning.</summary>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of specific Latin and Greek terms found in classical literature, specifically focusing on onomatopoeic sounds and metaphors for repetition.</meta>

-># BOOK<-

Just as in the poets Juvenal and Martial we read *poppyma* and *poppymata* <note>original: "poppyſma ... & poppyſmata"</note>, so in Pliny we find the form *poppysmis* from the nominative case, which is *poppysmus* <note>original: "poppyſmus"</note>. It is a word made up from a sound <note>term: onomatopoeia; a word that phonetically imitates the sound it describes</note>, signifying that specific noise we use to soothe horses that are not yet fully broken in, as noted by Maximus in his lexicon, and likewise by Nicas and Suidas. Sophocles also uses the verb: "the charioteer clucks to his team" <note>original Greek: "ποππύζεται ζευγηλάτρις"</note>, just as Pollux mentions in the seventh book of his *Onomasticon*. Indeed, in his first book, Pollux teaches that a horse is halted by a clucking sound <note>term: poppyma; a smacking or clucking sound used to command or calm animals</note>, using this same word found in Pliny. Pliny himself, in the thirty-fifth book of his *Natural History*, also writes: "A similar success is said to have followed Nealces regarding the foam of a horse, by likewise throwing a sponge at the painting when he was depicting a man clucking <note>original: "poppyzonta"; referring to a specific clicking or whistling sound made to control a horse</note> while holding the horse back." Dexippus the Platonist philosopher also writes in the dialogue he published on Aristotle’s *Categories*: "or if there is any unarticulated sound, as in the case of cluckings" <note>original Greek: "ἢ εἴτις ἄναρθρος ψόφος, ὡς ἐπὶ ποππυσμάτων"</note>.

...were drawn from histories, just as surely several are even up to now, either from truth and fact, if perhaps some recent event had occurred. Therefore, they were also usually presented with the names of locations added, and they are certainly maintained and published in such a collection. Therefore, *Cambre* is the title of a declamation <term>declamation: a formal oratorical speech practiced in schools of rhetoric</term> named after a place. For Cambre is a town in Teuthrania in the Troad <note>The Troad is the region surrounding the ancient city of Troy in modern-day Turkey</note>, as Pliny writes; and from the nearest region is Lesbos, in which tyrants seized Mitylene, as Strabo attests—namely Nigessilus and Megarogilus. The poet Alcaeus attacked them in song, for which he is said to have possessed a “golden plectrum” <note>original: "aureū plectrum"; a pick used to play a lyre, here signifying Alcaeus’s status as a supreme lyric poet</note>, according to the authority of Fabius <note>Referring to the Roman rhetorician Quintilian, whose full name was Marcus Fabius Quintilianus</note>. The declamation *On the Expulsion of the Tyrants from Cambre* was always to be repeated by the teachers.

This interpretation is pleasing, as it agrees with what he said:

->When a numerous fleet destroys cruel tyrants.<-

As for others who derive the *Cambren* declamation from Cambrus, King of the Scythians, whom Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus, a Greek historian</note> mentions, it is in no way fitting. Thus far, then, Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century humanist scholar and rival of the author</note> has argued with a completely convoluted and evasive discourse. From his words, this final, confused, and forced opinion is gathered: that the *Cambren* declamation is named after a town in Teuthrania, because names are given to declamations from places, and because tyrants occupied Mitylene, a town in Lesbos, so that the poet might signify it with that little verse:

->When a numerous fleet destroys cruel tyrants.<-

Whether or not these were proposed by the ancients...

...of the variations, with the names of places added, we do not labor over, although it was not Cornelius <note>Likely referring to the historian Cornelius Tacitus</note> (as he himself says) but Suetonius Tranquillus who wrote this in the small book he composed *On Famous Grammarians and Rhetoricians*. Thus, for him, everything is full of shadows...

MISCELLANIES. 45

Domitius casts forth shadows and fog whenever he cannot extricate himself like a little fish from a wicker trap <note>original: "nassa" — a conical basket used for catching fish</note>, and just as the philosopher Atticus <margin>Atticus the philosopher.</margin> (a quite elegant defender of Plato) teaches that Aristotle used to do: like a cuttlefish <margin>The nature of the cuttlefish.</margin> escaping the very eyes and hand of the fisherman by spraying its own ink, so this man, fearing to be caught, intentionally muddies his opinions with obscurities. This description fits no one better than him, for never is he more elusive than when he is almost grasped; he wraps himself up, veiling himself in ambiguities and vomiting out a kind of ink of perplexity. 

However, a certain man—otherwise quite learned and one who has served literature well through his own industry—brought forward a point in a recently published commentary regarding "Gambra" and "Anatho" <note>These appear to be corrupted names for 'Crambe' and 'Antho' occurring in the satires of Juvenal</note>. He cited Probus as his source, who he claims was an interpreter of Juvenal. I must admit that I do not know who this Probus is, nor do I think he is the same one mentioned by Gellius and other ancient writers. Let the credit for that discovery remain with the author. 

Nevertheless, I will briefly set forth my own view, for as a certain tragic poet says, "the language of truth is simple." I believe Juvenal was expressing this Greek <margin>A Greek proverb.</margin> proverb: "Cabbage twice served is death" <note>original: "δὶς κράμβη θάνατος" (dis krambe thanatos)</note>. This signifies that repeating the same argument or controversy <margin>Cabbage.</margin> over and over is "death," using the word "Cabbage" <note>original: "Crambe" — specifically sea-kale or cabbage</note> to denote the weariness of a topic frequently repeated. Indeed, even in common speech, "reheated cabbage" has passed into a proverb for something that causes disgust.

The poet preferred to use the Greek term rather than the Latin word, either because that proverb is more common among the Greeks than the Latins, or indeed because a certain species of cabbage <note>original: *brassica*</note> was properly called *Crambe*. As noted in the works of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, an influential Roman naturalist</note>, this variety has thinner and simpler leaves, but they are very dense; it is quite bitter but highly effective. **Suidas** <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia</note> mentions the Greek proverb itself and teaches that *Crambe* <note>A reference to the proverb "Crambe bis cocta," or "twice-cooked cabbage," used to describe something tedious or repetitive</note> was even served at banquets because it counteracts drunkenness, just as **Cato** <note>Cato the Elder</note> testifies in his book *On Agriculture*.

I ask that the learned, and those skilled in the choice of proper words and the reading of ancient texts, consult their own...

<margin>A passage in Varro.</margin> IN Marcus Varro’s first book on *Agriculture* <note>original: *Res Rusticae*</note>, there is a faulty reading of that famous line: "The beard and the grain are known to almost everyone, the husk <term>gluma <note>the leafy outer envelope of a grain of corn or wheat</note></term> to few." <meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient sources and their translations into Latin, specifically focusing on the transmission of Euhemerus's ideas through the poet Ennius.</meta>

->MISCELLANEOUS WORKS. 47<-

There is no doubt that Varro refers to Ennius’s *Sacred History* <note>Ennius (c. 239–169 BCE) was a foundational Roman poet; his *Sacra Historia* sought to explain the origins of the gods as human kings.</note> as being translated from the Greek of Euhemerus into Latin; this is the same work cited repeatedly by Lactantius, Eusebius, and Augustine. <margin>Euhemerus</margin> <margin>and his books.</margin> The words of Lactantius from the first book of his *Divine Institutes* are as follows:

"The ancient author Euhemerus, who was from the city of Messana, collected the deeds of Jupiter and the others who are thought to be gods. He wove together a history from the titles and sacred inscriptions found in the most ancient temples, and especially in the shrine of Jupiter Triphylius, where an inscription indicated that a golden column had been placed by Jupiter himself. On this column, he recorded his deeds so that they might remain as monuments to posterity of his achievements."

Ennius translated and followed this history, and these are his words: "Where Jupiter gives the empire of the sea to Neptune, so that he might rule over all the islands and all the places along the sea." 

Then the same Lactantius cites several passages from Ennius regarding this same history again and again, which I, for the sake of brevity, shall pass over. Moreover, Sextus Empiricus also cites the words of Euhemerus himself, particularly in the fourth volume of the *Pyrrhonists*, and says he was called "The Atheist" <margin>The Atheist.</margin>, just like Diagoras of Melos, Prodicus of Ceos, Theodorus of Cyrene, and several others. And about him, Timon of Phlius (I believe) says the following:

"Euhemerus, an old braggart, scribbling wicked books." <note>original Greek: "Εὐήμερος γέρων ἀλαζὼν ἄδικα βιβλία ψύχων."</note>

<summary>This page discusses the transmission of Euhemerism—the theory that gods were originally historical human kings—from the Greek author Euhemerus to the Roman poet Ennius, citing various Church Fathers and skeptics as evidence.</summary>

But setting these things aside, we must certainly look more closely at a passage in <term>Columella</term> <note>Lucius Junius Moderatus Columella (4–70 AD) was a prominent Roman writer on agriculture.</note> as well, specifically in Book 10, Chapter 3. Where he treats the subject of <margin>Columella’s text</margin> bees, the name of **Homer** is cited twice instead of **Euhemerus** in the commonly available manuscripts. This is proven by a very ancient book from the private library of the **Medici family**, which was written in <term>Lombardic script</term> <note>original: "literis Langobardis." In the Renaissance, this term was often used to describe early medieval scripts like Beneventan or Carolingian minuscule which were difficult for later readers to decipher.</note>. It is also confirmed by another copy from the public library of the same <margin>Niccolò Niccoli</margin> family, which <term>Niccolò Niccoli</term> <note>An influential Italian Renaissance humanist (1364–1437) known for his collection of manuscripts and his role in developing the italic script.</note>—a learned and diligent man—transcribed with his own hand from an ancient original. Furthermore, logic itself supports this conclusion, for no barriers can resist reason: indeed, no such fable about bees is found in the works of **Homer**. It is far more likely that it was written by **Euhemerus**, who composed a history of **Jupiter**, whose nurses those bees are said to have been. The poet <term>Callimachus</term> <note>A Greek poet and scholar at the Library of Alexandria (c. 310–240 BC).</note> found it written by this same Euhemerus (as I believe), of whom we spoke above, in that same "Sacred History," that <margin>The Tomb of Jupiter</margin> **Jupiter** ended his life in **Crete**, and that his tomb is located there in the town of **Knossos**. Upon that tomb, it is said, was inscribed in ancient Greek letters: **Zeus, son of Cronus** <note>original: "Ζεὺς κρόνου", translated in the text as "Iupiter Saturni."</note>. Because this most learned poet knew it had been written by Euhemerus and was held as a confessed fact by the Cretans, I believe he testified to it in his *Hymn to Jupiter*. "The Cretans," he says, "are always liars, for they have even fabricated a tomb for **Jupiter**, though he never died, but exists forever." These are the verses:

Cretans are always liars, for they have even built a tomb for you, O King... <note>original: "Κρῆτες ἀεὶ ψεῦσται, καὶ γὰρ τάφον ὦ ἄνα σεῖο". This famous paradox is known as the Epimenides paradox.</note>

<summary>This page discusses a persistent scribal error in the works of Columella, where 'Homer' was mistakenly substituted for the mythographer 'Euhemerus.' The author uses ancient manuscripts from the Medici library and the testimony of Callimachus to argue that Euhemerus was the true source of the story regarding Jupiter's tomb in Crete.</summary>
<keywords>Columella, Euhemerus, Medici Library, Niccolò Niccoli, Callimachus, Jupiter's Tomb, Crete, textual criticism, Lombardic script, bees in mythology</keywords>

->MISCELLANY, Page 48<-

"The Cretans built it. But you did not die, for you exist forever."
<note>original Greek: "Κρῆτες ἐτεκτήναντο. σὺ δ' οὐ θάνες, ἐσσὶ γὰρ αἰεί." From Callimachus’s Hymn to Zeus. The poet argues that because Zeus is immortal, the tomb the Cretans built for him proves they are liars.</note>

Because of this very thing, I believe Statius <note>A Roman poet of the 1st century AD.</note> also says in the first book of the *Thebaid*: "And Crete, which lied about your ghost." <note>original: "Mentitaq́; manis Creta tuos." Referring to the supposed remains or spirit of a god who cannot die.</note>

Cyprian the martyr <margin>Of Cyprian</margin>, a most eloquent man, also says in his book *On the Orgin and Kinds of Idols* <margin>The passage</margin>: "Jupiter's cave is visited in Crete, and his tomb is shown," although these words are found written with little correction in the newer manuscripts. <margin><unclear>damaged text</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>damaged text</unclear></margin>

Furthermore, the Cretans were always considered such liars that there is a Greek proverb <margin>A proverb</margin>—"to play the Cretan against a Cretan"—which means nothing other than to use a lie against a liar. <note>original Greek: "πρὸς κρῆτα κρητίζειν." To "Cretanize" became a technical term for lying or being deceitful.</note>

But also this verse by that famous Cretan, Epimenides <note>A semi-mythical 6th-century BC seer and poet from Crete.</note>, was cited by Paul the Apostle and because of that, it has even been sanctified: <note>Referring to the Epistle to Titus 1:12 in the New Testament.</note>

"Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, lazy bellies."
<note>original Greek: "Κρῆτες ἀεὶ ψεῦσται, κακὰ θηρία, γαστέρες ἀργοί."</note>

This means: Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, and slow to work. For Callimachus took the first half-line of that verse from Epimenides. Therefore Ovid also wrote this elegantly in his *Amores*:

"The Cretans shall be witnesses, and even the Cretans do not invent everything." <note>original: "Cretes erunt testes, nec fingunt omnia Cretes."</note>

And elsewhere:

"I speak of well-known things; not even Crete, which sustains a hundred cities, is accustomed to deny this, though she be a liar." <note>original: "Nota loquor, non hoc quæ centum sustinet urbes, Quamuis sit mendax, Creta negare solet." From Ovid’s Heroides.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of classical riddles and philological puzzles, specifically addressing a mystery left unsolved by Aulus Gellius in his Attic Nights.</meta>

# B O O K

->**SOLVED AND UNTIED HERE**, the riddle from Varro’s book cited by Gellius.<-

## CHAPTER XXXVI.

<margin>A passage from Aulus Gellius</margin>
I profess that I myself will attempt to solve—even at my own risk—that riddle from <term>Varro|Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE), a prolific Roman scholar</term> which <term>Gellius|Aulus Gellius, a 2nd-century Roman author known for 'Attic Nights'</term> presents and leaves unexplained, so that he might sharpen the reader's own guesses. It is presented in three <term>senarii|A common meter in Latin poetry consisting of six iambic feet</term>:

*Whether it be **once-less**, or **twice-less**, I do not quite know; or both of these, as I once heard it said, he refused to yield even to King Jupiter himself.*
<note>The original Latin riddle is: "Semel minus ne, an bis minus sit, non sat scio, An utrunq; horum... Ioui ipsi regi noluit concedere."</note>

It seems to signify **Terminus**, the god who refused to yield to
<margin>The god Terminus.</margin>
Jupiter when the <term>Capitol|The Temple of Jupiter Optimus Maximus on the Capitoline Hill in Rome</term> was being <term>deconsecrated|The ritual of 'exauguratio,' where existing shrines were removed to make way for a new temple structure</term>. Ovid writes in the second book of the *Fasti*:

*What happened when the new Capitol was built? Truly, the whole crowd of gods yielded to Jupiter and gave up their place. Terminus, as the ancients recall, remained where he was found within the temple, and he holds the sacred space along with great Jupiter.*
<note>Ovid's 'Fasti' is a poetic calendar of Roman myths and festivals.</note>

Therefore, the author of this <term>scirpus|A Latin term for a riddle, literally meaning a 'rush' or 'bulrush' woven into a complex knot</term> wonders whether it was "once-less" or "twice-less," or both of these. From both—that is, from "once" and "twice"—the result is "thrice."
<note>In Latin, 'once' is 'semel' and 'twice' is 'bis.' Adding them together (1+2) equals 'thrice,' which is 'ter.'</note> 
Therefore, it was not "once-less" or "twice-less," but **Terminus** <note>A pun: 'Ter' (thrice) + 'minus' (less) = Terminus</note> who refused to yield even to King Jupiter himself.

<summary>The author provides a solution to a linguistic riddle by Varro, identifying the answer as the god of boundaries, Terminus, based on a mathematical pun.</summary>
<keywords>Terminus, Jupiter, Varro, Aulus Gellius, Ovid, Roman mythology, wordplay, Capitol Hill, riddles</keywords>

->**THAT SERVIUS ERRED FOULLY**<-
->**concerning the river Oaxes and Crete.**<-

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

**A**nd we shall come to the rapid Oaxes of Crete. <note>original Latin: "Et rapidum Cretae veniemus Oaxem." This is a line from Virgil’s first *Eclogue* (line 65), where a character describes the far-off places where exiles might be sent.</note>

Servius <note>Maurus Servius Honoratus, a 4th-century grammarian whose commentary on Virgil was a standard textbook in the Renaissance.</note> explains this word for word from the first Eclogue of the *Bucolics* <note>The *Bucolics* or *Eclogues* are Virgil's pastoral poems about the lives and songs of shepherds.</note> as follows: "The 'rapid of Crete,'" he says, "means muddy, because it carries away [*rapit*] chalk [*creta*]. <note>Servius is making a pun: the Latin word *Creta* refers to the island, but the common noun *creta* means chalk or clay. He argues Virgil is describing a river that "washes away chalk" rather than a river on the island of Crete.</note> 'Creta' is the name for white earth. For the Oaxis is a river of Mesopotamia, which, by its speed, carries away white earth and becomes turbulent." Thus far Servius.

But indeed, I would rather believe that the Oaxis is understood to be a river on the island of Crete itself. For Vibius Sequester <note>An ancient geographer whose work was often used to identify the places mentioned by poets.</note> also says the Oaxis is a river of Crete, from which the city Oaxia is named. Therefore Apollonius <note>Apollonius of Rhodes, author of the *Argonautica*, an epic poem about Jason and the Argonauts.</note> also called the island of Crete the "Oaxian land" in the first book of the *Argonautica* with these words:

**The Idaean Dactyls <term>Dactyls: mythical ancient smiths and healers who lived on Mount Ida; their name means "fingers."</term> of Crete, whom once the nymph Anchiale, in the Dictaean cave, grasping the Oaxian earth with both hands, brought forth.** <note>original Greek: "δάκτυλοι ἰδαῖοῖ κρηταέες οὕς ποτε νύμφη ἀγχιάλη δικταῖον ἀνὰ σπέος ἀμφοτέρῃσι δραξαμένη γαίης οἰαξίδος ἐβλάστησε."</note>

Moreover, Varro <note>Varro Atacinus, a Roman poet who wrote a Latin translation of Apollonius’s Greek epic.</note> translated this last part as follows...

## ->JUVENAL’S POEM EXPLAINED,<-
### ->contrary to all opinions held until now, regarding the Spartan circle and *Pitylisma*.<-

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

<margin>Passage from Juvenal.</margin>

**"Who makes the Spartan circle slippery with a *pitysma*."**
<note>original: *Qui Lacedæmonium pitysmate lubricat orbem.* This line comes from Juvenal’s *Satire 11*, which critiques Roman luxury and excess.</note>

This verse from the eleventh Satire of Juvenal has been interpreted in various ways up to this point, and (as I see it) quite carelessly. For **Domitius** <note>likely Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century Renaissance humanist known for his commentaries on Latin poets</note> says that a *pitysma* <note>original: *pitysma* (from the Greek *pitysma*), referring to the practice of tasting wine and spitting it out on the floor</note> is a spraying of light spit, derived from the verb *pityo* <note>to spit or sputter</note>. He takes the "Spartan circle" to mean a finger-ring, because, he says, the use of rings originated with the Spartans; thus, senators and knights are signified by the mention of the ring.

Furthermore, another most learned man—whom I nonetheless refrain from naming out of respect, despite our disagreement—thinks it refers to a Spartan dance. However, he candidly admits that he has read nothing of the sort elsewhere, except for a single mention of a "Spartan dance" found in the works of **Pollux** <note>Julius Pollux, a Greek grammarian of the 2nd century who wrote the *Onomasticon*, a dictionary of Greek terms</note>.

Finally, a third man, also very learned, understands it to mean a hoop <term>trochus: a metal hoop driven by a hook, used by Romans for exercise</term> made from a pine tree, since a pine is called a *pitys* <note>Greek: *pitys* (πίτυς)</note>, from which the word *pitysma* would be derived. 

Out of these three opinions, and with due respect to their authors, I clearly approve of none. In the first place, I deny that the "Spartan circle" can signify a ring, since it is nowhere read that the origin of the ring flowed from the Lace— <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "Lace," likely continuing to "Lacedaemonians" on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page begins a scholarly critique of previous interpretations of a line from Juvenal’s Satires, specifically debating the meaning of "Spartan circle" and the Greek-derived term "pitysma." The author rejects theories that the passage refers to Spartan rings, dances, or pine hoops.</summary>
<keywords>Juvenal, Satire 11, Spartan circle, Pitysma, Domitius Calderinus, Julius Pollux, philology, classical commentary, Roman customs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a philological discussion concerning the correction of specific Greek and Latin terms found in ancient manuscripts, specifically focusing on the transition from discussing Spartan customs to medical terminology.</meta>

->MISCELLANIES. 50<-

To the Spartans. Pliny, or rather the source Domitius <note>Likely Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century scholar</note> cites, reports that even at that time in Sparta, they only used iron rings. But if it is established that the "Spartan circle" cannot be interpreted as a ring—as is certainly the case—then that fabricated "sprinkling" <note>original: "irroratio," likely referring to a metaphorical or textual interpretation he finds flimsy</note> will carry no weight at all. Nor should *pitysma* <note>A disputed term the author is analyzing</note> be derived from *pityo*—a word you will never find—but rather from *pityzo* <note>original Greek: πιτυζω, meaning to making a 'pity' sound or to spit</note>, as is explained by Nicander the Grammarian. Whoever thinks that a *pitysma* is a Spartan dance, since they can find nothing about it, will easily (as a candid person usually does) yield to a better opinion.

I believe the same will apply to anyone who thinks a *pitysma* is a hoop made from pine <note>original: "pityo," from the Greek for pine tree</note>, since that is nowhere recorded, nor does it fit the context. Furthermore, the rules of poetic meter <note>original: "syllabæ ratio," referring to the length of syllables in verse</note> argue against it, because the word for pine has a short first syllable, as is clear from the very first verse of Theocritus <note>Theocritus was a famous Greek pastoral poet</note>.

We therefore believe the word should be read as <term>pitylisma</term> <note>A term referring to rapid, jerky movements or a specific type of medical exercise</note>, and that through the ignorance of scribes, a syllable was removed from the middle. This is hardly surprising in such murky textual conditions. By <term>pitylisma</term>, we understand a type of exercise, which the excellent physician Galen discusses in the second book of his *On Hygiene* <note>original: "ὑγιεινῶν" (Hygeina)</note>. <margin>See Poliziano recanting these points in his *Letters*, book 11, letter six.</margin> Although in Galen’s work, the verb *pitylizein* <note>original: "pitylizin," Greek: πιτυλίζειν</note> appears rather than the noun *pitylisma*. Nevertheless, *pitylisma* is correctly formed from that verb, following a very common grammatical pattern. Nor is it inappropriate to first write out Galen’s Greek words and then interpret them to the best of our ability. Since he notes various kinds of exercises and also teaches about those which are indeed fast, but...

<summary>The author uses philological evidence to argue that a corrupted word in an ancient text should be read as 'pitylisma' (a medical exercise) rather than 'pitysma' (a pine hoop or dance). He supports this by citing the physician Galen and the rules of Greek poetic meter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of Roman bathing habits and architectural terminology, specifically focusing on the "Laconicum" or Spartan-style dry sweat bath. It follows the previous page's discussion of classical poetic references to hygiene and luxury.</meta>

# MISCELLANEOUS. [space] 51

If the rites of the Spartans please you,
You can be content with dry steam,
The raw Virgin water, and being plunged into the waters of Mars.
<note>original: "Ritus si placeant tibi Laconum..." This is a quote from the Roman poet Martial (Epigrams 6.42), referring to the "Aqua Virgo" aqueduct and the "Martian" waters of the Tiber or the Baths of Agrippa, contrasting Spartan austerity with Roman luxury.</note>

But also Columella, in his first book on agriculture, writes thus:
"We watch in amazement the gestures of effeminate men, who mimic with feminine movement the sex denied to men by nature, and deceive the eyes of those watching. Then soon after, so that we might come to the taverns <term>ganeas (taverns or eating-houses)</term> ready for gluttony, we cook off our daily indigestion in the Spartan sweat-baths, and seek out a thirst by burning off our sweat."
<note>Columella was a 1st-century writer whose work *De Re Rustica* often critiqued the perceived moral decay of Roman society compared to traditional farming life.</note>

Now, therefore, even from these words of Columella, it can be clear to anyone whom the poet is most especially signifying: namely, those who without a doubt spent the whole day in those Spartan baths <note>Latin: *Laconicis*</note> and gymnasiums, anointed with oil and naked. For this reason, he also says they are "slippery" on account of the oil and ointment with which they were rubbed and dried.

Seneca also, in his seventh book of letters to Lucilius, suggests nothing other than these Spartan baths, relating it thus: "What do I have to do with those hot pools? What with sweating-rooms, in which dry vapor is enclosed to exhaust the body, so that all sweat might be forced out through effort?"

It remains that now, since he has mentioned a "circle," we should also teach that these Spartan baths were built in a circular shape, which is clear from the fifth book of *Architecture* by Vitruvius, in these words:
"The Spartan sweating-rooms, which are to be joined to the <term>tepidarium (the warm room of a bathhouse)</term>, should be as high as they are wide, up to the lowest curve of the hemisphere <note>the domed ceiling</note>, with a central light in the middle of the hemisphere..."
<note>Vitruvius was the premier Roman authority on architecture; his descriptions here define the physical structure of the *Laconicum* as a circular, domed room.</note>

->THE RIDDLE OF THE POET AUSONIUS EXPLAINED<-
*by the poet, concerning the daughters of Cadmus, Melos, cuttlefish ink, and*
*Cnidian knots.*

**CHAPTER 39**

The riddle <note>original: "Scirpum," literally a rush or reed, used metaphorically to mean a complex puzzle or enigma</note> which the ingenious and highly learned poet Ausonius proposed to Theon the Grammarian in acatalectic iambic dimeters <term>acatalectic iambic dimeters: a form of poetry with four iambic "feet" per line, where the final foot is complete</term>, we shall now attempt to explain. But first, let us set down the verses themselves.

<margin>In the words of Ausonius</margin> Or let your interpreter be present, who was the one to understand my riddles, when he revealed to you the little black daughters of Cadmus <note>A poetic metaphor for the letters of the alphabet, which tradition says were brought to Greece by the Phoenician prince Cadmus</note>, the white page of Melos <note>A reference to fine writing material; Melos was known for its earth used in pigments and potentially its parchment</note>, the marks of dark cuttlefish ink <note>original: "sepiæ"; the ink of the cuttlefish was used as a writing fluid in antiquity</note>, and the Cnidian knots <note>Cnidus was a city in Asia Minor; the "knot" refers to a complex puzzle or a specific type of riddle</note>.

Among these, the last verse is read incorrectly in the more common editions of the book, which use the words "measures" <note>original: "modos"</note> or "mine" <note>original: "meos"</note> instead of the word we have restored: "knots" <note>original: "nodos"</note>. I have found this correct reading in several other copies, as well as in a book written by the hand of Giovanni Boccaccio <note>The famous 14th-century Italian humanist and author of the Decameron</note>, which is currently kept in the library of Santo Spirito <note>An important Augustinian monastery and library in Florence</note>.

<meta>This page continues a philological commentary on the poet Ausonius, specifically focusing on his use of riddles and metaphors to describe the tools of writing.</meta>

### MISCELLANY, Page 52

...is preserved in the Florentine <note>likely referring to the *Codex Florentinus*, a celebrated manuscript</note>. Furthermore, the rules of poetic meter do not allow for either "modes" or "mine" <note>original: "modos, uel meos"</note>. This is because the second foot of the line must be an <term>iambus|a metrical foot consisting of a short syllable followed by a long one (u –)</term>, not a <term>pyrrhic|a metrical foot consisting of two short syllables (u u)</term>. Therefore, it seems to me that Ausonius has hidden away under these metaphors the gift of Cadmus the Phoenician—specifically, Nilotic papyrus, writing ink, and the scribe’s reed pen, which is the true companion tool of the man of letters. For Cadmus <margin>Cadmus brought letters into Greece.</margin> was the first to bring letters into Greece from Phoenicia.

Herodotus bears witness to this in his book *Terpsichore* <note>This is Book 5 of Herodotus’s Histories, named after the Muse of dance.</note>, stating that those Phoenicians who came to Greece under the leadership of Cadmus first showed their native, ancestral letters—that is, Phoenician letters—to the Greeks. But as time flowed on, he says, the shapes of the characters gradually changed along with the spoken language. The Ionians were the first to use them. For this reason, they were also called *phoinikeia* <note>original: "φοινίκηα", meaning "Phoenician things."</note>. Herodotus also writes that he himself saw "Cadmean letters" in the temple of Ismenian Apollo at Thebes in Boeotia, engraved upon certain tripods; he cites three epigrams from these, which were (as he himself says) very similar to Ionian letters. 

Consequently, if the ancient Greek letterforms were none other than those of the Phoenicians, and the Latin letters were none other than those of the Greeks—as Pliny also indicates by citing the Delphic inscriptions (for that mention of a "tablet" is a recent addition and was inserted by the unlearned)—it is manifest that the Latin letters are also none other than those we learned from Cadmus. For this reason, Ausonius calls them the "daughters of Cadmus." And because they are pressed onto the page as small letters in ink, therefore sweetly... <note>The sentence continues on the following page.</note>

->I iii<- <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to ensure the pages were bound in the correct sequence.</note>

<summary>This page explores the Phoenician origins of the Greek and Latin alphabets, citing Herodotus and Pliny to explain why the poet Ausonius refers to letters as the "daughters of Cadmus."</summary>

### CHAPTER XLIII.

<margin>Ancient diphthongs.</margin> **Diphthongs** <term>Diphthong: two vowel sounds joined in one syllable, like "ae" or "au".</term> among the Latins are said to be no more than four, though we find at least seven of them in ancient marbles or coins, of which Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" (1449–1492), the Florentine ruler and famed patron of arts and classical scholarship.</note> provides us with a vast abundance. And indeed, some always pronounced *ai* in place of *ae* with the letter *i*, not *e*, after the fashion of the Greeks, as Quintilian <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 AD), a celebrated Roman rhetorician and teacher.</note> shows. ->### That one should read **Pegasean Nectar** in Persius, not melody.<-

CHAPTER XLIIII.

<margin>A passage of Persius</margin> Persius, in the epigram which serves as his prologue, says:
"The crow-poets and magpie-poetesses, you would believe they sing the **Pegasean melody**." <note>original: "Pegaseium melos." The author is quoting Persius’s Satires, Prologue 13–14, where the poet mocks uninspired writers.</note>
In a very ancient commentary written in the script they call **Lombard** <note>original: *Langobardas*. This refers to an early medieval handwriting style, likely Beneventan script, which the author views as a sign of antiquity.</note>—which we even displayed publicly to our students—we found the text worded exactly like this: "**Pegasean nectar**," whereas others have "melody" (*melos*). From this, we believe that the old and pure reading was "nectar," while the "melody" reading is more recent and faulty.
Furthermore, Pomponius Laetus <note>Julius Pomponius Laetus (1428–1498), a prominent Italian humanist and founder of the Roman Academy.</note>, the leader of the Roman Academy and a most diligent student of antiquity, affirmed to many listeners that he possessed an ancient manuscript of Persius that supports our reading. 
Moreover, the rules of the verse prove the point. For the final foot of a **choliamb** <term>choliamb: also known as a 'limping iamb,' a poetic meter where the final rhythm 'stumbles' by ending on a long syllable instead of a short one.</term> must be either a spondee or a trochee. However, the word "melody" (*melos*)—neither among the Greeks nor among our own writers—is ever used unless the first syllable is taken as short. Therefore, it would certainly be a mistake in the verse if you admitted the word "melody."

<margin>Hephaestion, author on meters</margin> As for what some claim—that the letter **lambda** [λ] has the power to lengthen a syllable among the Greeks—this was entirely ignored by Hephaestion <note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian whose manual on meters became the standard reference for scholars.</note>, the preeminent authority on meters; I find that...

<summary>The author argues for a corrected reading of a line in Persius’s prologue, claiming "nectar" is the original word instead of "melos" based on ancient manuscripts and the requirements of poetic meter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a philological discussion regarding the correct pronunciation and poetic "quantity" (the length of vowels) in classical literature, specifically comparing the linguistic freedom of Greek poets to the stricter rules followed by Latin authors.</meta>

->MISCELLANIES. 57<-

I know this, but only in certain obscure and insignificant papers. An example is cited from Homer, where we read the word *heloria* <note>original: "heloria"; referring to "spoils" or "prey" in Book 1 of the Iliad</note> with the first syllable stretched long. <margin>*heloria* in the first book of the *Iliad*</margin> Yet how frivolous, how slight, and how trifling this argument is! This is most clearly shown by the fact that it is now accepted by the usage and authority of every learned man that this word in Homer should also be written with a double "l" <note>The Greek letter lambda (λ); doubling the consonant was a way to justify the syllable being long in poetry</note>.

Furthermore, even if we were to grant that this occasionally happens among the Greeks, we must not immediately believe that Persius <note>A Roman satirist known for his dense and difficult style</note> would use such a license for a single Greek term—a term the Greeks themselves are hardly found to use even once in such a vast forest of literature. This is especially true since neither among the Greeks (as far as I have observed) nor likewise among the Latins is the word *melos* <note>meaning "song" or "melody"</note> found with anything other than a short first syllable. 

For the ancient Latin writers greatly avoided this license that was granted only to the Greeks. The poet Martial <note>A Roman poet famous for his short, witty epigrams</note> demonstrates this when he agonizes so much over the name *Earinus* <note>The name of a favorite attendant of Emperor Domitian; the name, meaning "spring-like," was difficult to fit into Roman poetic meter because of its vowel lengths</note>. He does not even allow himself to say *eiarinon* <note>original: εἰαρινὸν</note> instead of *Earinon*, despite the common Greek license of inserting a letter <note>The Greeks often added letters like 'i' to change the length of a word to fit the rhythm of a poem</note>, as seen in Homer: "upon the flowers of spring" <note>original: ἐπ’ ἄνθεσιν εἰαρινοῖσιν</note>. Indeed, listen to Martial himself:

->Poets say "of spring," however,<-
->But they are Greeks, to whom nothing is denied,<-
->And who are allowed to sound "Ares, Ares" <margin>*Ares, Ares* in the fifth book of the *Iliad*</margin> <note>In this famous Homeric line, the name of the war god Ares is pronounced with a long 'A' first, then a short 'A', showing Greek rhythmic flexibility</note> with different lengths;<-
->It is not permitted for us to be so eloquent.<-

<summary>This page discusses the strictness of Latin poetic meter compared to the flexibility of Greek verse. The author argues that Latin writers like Martial and Persius avoided the "Greek license" of changing vowel lengths to fit their meter, even when dealing with difficult names or borrowed words.</summary>

### That Patroclus was younger than Achilles, contrary to what Aeschylus has recorded, or what is commonly believed.

->CHAPTER XLV.<-

An opinion has long since been spread among all and accepted: that Patroclus was younger than Achilles, and was loved by him much like Hylas was by Hercules. This is what Martial is believed to suggest when he says:
*The light friend was closer to the descendant of Aeacus.* <note>original: "Aeacidæ propior levis amicus erat." The "descendant of Aeacus" is Achilles. In Roman poetry, "light" often carried a connotation of youth or a specific role in a romantic pairing.</note>
For this reason, a certain very naughty verse from the books of *Hermaphroditus* <note>A reference to the *Hermaphroditus*, a famous and controversial collection of erotic poems by the 15th-century humanist Antonio Beccadelli.</note> was also commonly applauded. However, Statius, in his *Achilleid*, declares that their ages were essentially equal, saying:
*Patroclus follows, already then bound by a great love, and stands out as a rival in such great deeds. Equal in pursuits and the measure of their age, but in strength far [surpassed]...* <note>original: "Inſequitur magno iam tum connexus amore / Patroclus... Par ſtudijs æuiq; modis, ſed robore longe."</note>

And yet he was destined to see Troy <note>original: "Pergama," a reference to the citadel of Troy.</note> with an equal fate. 
Plato, however, in his *Symposium*, argues a very different point. For he declares both that Achilles was much younger, and that it was rather Achilles who was loved by Patroclus, since he was as yet beardless <margin>Patroclus was older.</margin>—indeed, he was more beautiful not only than Patroclus himself, but than all the other heroes. Truly, he says, for that reason the gods bestowed exceptional honors upon him, so as to send him to the Fortunate Isles <note>The "Islands of the Blessed" in Greek mythology, a paradise reserved for heroes.</note>, because he valued his lover so highly that he chose not only to die for him, but even to die upon his body, rather than grow old in his homeland. Furthermore, this same Plato rebukes Aeschylus as a trifler <note>original: "nugacem," implying someone who writes nonsense or trifles.</note> because Aeschylus also claimed that Achilles had been the lover of Patroclus. Plato cites Homer as a witness to the age of both men; if anyone asks to be shown these words—for Plato certainly did not include them—let him read in the eleventh book <note>original: "Rhapsodia," referring to a book or canto of the epic poem.</note> of the *Iliad*, in the speech given by Nestor <note>The elderly, wise Greek counselor of the Trojan War.</note>, the commands with which the father Menoetius <note>The father of Patroclus.</note> sent his son Patroclus away to the wars.

### THE WORD *CACOETHES* IN JUVENAL 
### is a four-syllable word, not *cacethos*. 
### And "stored in Lyde's box," 
### not "un-stored" should be read.

->B O O K<-

"An incurable itch for writing, and it grows old in the sick heart." <note>original: "Scribendi cacethos, & ægro in corde senescit." A famous quote from Juvenal's Satire 7, describing the compulsive urge to write.</note>
However, the word "cacethos" is never used by the Greeks anywhere, nor by the Latins; furthermore, the meter of the poem does not allow it. Therefore, we suspect it should be read as "cacoëthes," <margin>Malignant habit</margin> so that it matches what the Greeks call *the malignant habit*. <note>original: "τὸ κακόηθες" (to kakoēthes). In a medical context, this refers to a malignant tumor; in a literary context, it refers to a persistent bad habit or "itch."</note> Indeed, by that very frequent and common word, a "bad habit" is signified. We found this same reading in an ancient codex written in <term>Lombardic script</term>, <note>A medieval script used in Italy from the 7th to the 11th centuries, often difficult for later Renaissance scholars to read.</note> which Francesco Gaddi of Florence gave me the opportunity to read; he is the secretary to the highest magistrate, a wise and kind man, and well-versed in Literature. But that verse is also written this way in the same book:
<margin>Juvenal, book...</margin> "Nor does swollen Lyde, stored in a small box, do any good." <note>original: "Turgida nec prodest condita pyxide lyde." This refers to an old woman selling medicines or cosmetics.</note>
Which elsewhere is incorrectly written as, "Lyde stored in a box." <margin>Lyde</margin> This is so you might understand the old woman Lyde who sells medicines of that sort—a thick and (as Plautus says) <term>barrel-shaped</term> woman. <margin>Barrel-shaped</margin> <note>original: "doliaris," meaning shaped like a *dolium* or large storage jar.</note> Perhaps Martianus Capella was looking at this when, playing very wittily upon the drunken Silenus <note>In mythology, Silenus is the elderly, jovial, and usually intoxicated tutor of the wine-god Bacchus.</note>, he said: "And while he desires to move his step, with Lyde calling him away." For just as if you hear the names of Canthara, Pyrrhia, or Baucis, you would immediately imagine from the reading certain stuffed-up, wine-soaked, and wrinkled women. So, having heard the name of Lyde, you soon recall this "swollen" woman of Juvenal; thus, these characters are wonderfully introduced to provoke laughter: Silenus, his veins swollen with wine, and the pot-bellied Lyde, who (if the gods please) calls him away from mockery even as he is staggering and slipping.
