### MISCELLANIES. 56

#### What Pliny shared regarding the **pending titles** of ancient artists, those things also recognized in ancient monuments at Rome, as well as the appearance of the Virgilian **aegis** <note>The "aegis" is the legendary shield or breastplate associated with Zeus and Athena, famously described by the poet Virgil.</note>.

### CHAPTER XLVII.

**R**ecently in Rome, in the courtyard of the Mellini house, we observed a certain marble base, upon which was written in Greek: **"King Seleucus; Lysippus was making [this]"** <note>original Greek: σέλευκος βασιλεὺς λύσιππος ἐποίει. Lysippus was a famous Greek sculptor of the 4th century BCE.</note>. This translates into Latin as, *King Seleucus, Lysippus was making [this]*. There was with us at that time John Lawrence of Venice, secretary to the High Pontiff <note>Likely referring to the Pope's inner circle of humanist scholars.</note>, a man most learned in both languages <note>Latin and Greek</note>, and a most devoted student of all such refined curiosities. He therefore pointed out to us—which we immediately grasped—and we recognized that it was not by chance that **"was making"** <note>original Greek: ἐποίει (epoiei)</note> was used there rather than **"made"** <note>original: ἐποίησεν (epoiesen)</note>; that is, "he was in the process of making" rather than "he finished it." <margin>Was making.</margin>

Indeed, as Pliny says in the preface to his books of *Natural History*, those greatest artists inscribed even their finished works with a "pending" title, such as "Apelles was making [this]" and "Polyclitus [was making this]," as if the art were always just begun and incomplete. This was done so that, in the face of varying opinions, the artist might have a path to forgiveness, as if he intended to correct whatever was found wanting, had he not been interrupted <note>By using the imperfect tense "was making," the artist humbly suggested the work was still being perfected, shielding himself from criticism.</note>.

K ii <note>A printer's signature mark used to guide the bookbinder in assembling the pages.</note>

Why, he says, it is a sign of great modesty that they inscribed all their works as if they were just begun <note>original: "nouißima"; this refers to the Plinian anecdote that Greek artists used the imperfect tense ("was making") rather than the perfect ("made") to suggest the work was still a work-in-progress</note>, and as if something from each work had been snatched away by fate. However, there are three works—and no more, as he believes—that are handed down as being inscribed with an absolute signature <note>original: "absolute tradūtur inscripta"; meaning the artist used the completed "fecit" (he made it) rather than the ongoing "faciebat" (he was making it)</note>. "He *made* it," he records in the appropriate places. From this, it appeared that the author’s supreme confidence in his art was evident. For that very reason, all those works were the subject of great envy. Furthermore, John Lawrence of Venice <note>original: "Ioannes Laurentius Venetus"; likely the Byzantine scholar Joannes Lydus</note> asserted that similar inscriptions could be found elsewhere in Rome. We also saw something which we thought should be included in these *Miscellanies* of ours: a certain marble statue <margin>The Aegis of Pallas</margin> in that same city of Pallas <note>Pallas is another name for the Greek goddess Athena (Minerva in Rome)</note> wearing the Aegis <term>Aegis: the protective breastplate or shield of Athena, often fringed with snakes</term> covered in the scaly skin of dragons, so that small dragons likewise enclosed the furthest edge of it, in the manner of a border or fringe. Julian of Bologna <margin>The Antiquarian</margin> was there with us at that time, to whom the surname "The Antiquarian" now more rightly belongs because of his expertise. Together with him was George the Italian of Genoa, a most experienced investigator of hidden things. We immediately recalled the verses of Virgil, in which that incomparable poet seemed to have wonderfully expressed the engraving of the shield for Pallas, writing thus in the eighth book of the *Aeneid*:

And the terrifying Aegis, the armor of agitated Pallas,

Turning her eyes, though her neck was severed. <note>These lines from Virgil (Aeneid 8.435-438) describe the Cyclopes forging the Aegis for Minerva in their volcano workshop.</note>

CHAPTER XLVIII.

**P**liny the Elder <note>The text refers to "Plinius Secundus," the author of the famous encyclopedic *Natural History*.</note> in the thirty-fifth volume of his *Natural History*, states that Alexander of Macedon <margin>Passage from Pliny.</margin> used to frequently visit the workshop <note>original: *officinam*</note> of the most excellent painter Apelles, being delighted not only by his artistry but also by the man’s courtesy. Pliny says that when Alexander would discuss many things in the workshop without skill <note>original: *imperite*</note>, Apelles would kindly urge him to be silent, saying that he was being laughed at by the boys who ground the colors.

This story which Pliny writes concerning Alexander, Plutarch <note>A Greek biographer and philosopher; the work cited is *How to Tell a Flatterer from a Friend*.</note> attributed to Megabyzus <margin>Megabyzus.</margin> in that volume where he discusses the differences between a flatterer and a friend. If his words are interpreted into English, they are roughly these: Apelles the painter, he says, spoke to Megabyzus who was sitting with him and attempting to say something about line and shadow: "Do you see how the boys grinding <term>melida</term> <note>original: *melida*; likely referring to 'Melinum,' a white pigment made from earth found on the island of Melos.</note> (a type of color) were lately paying attention to you while you were silent, marvelling at your purple and gold <note>Referring to the expensive, regal clothing worn by the wealthy Megabyzus.</note>? But now they laugh at the very same person because you are beginning to speak on matters you have not learned." Furthermore, Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus, author of *Varia Historia* (Various History).</note> also, in his books of *Various History*, attributed this anecdote to Megabyzus himself and not to Alexander.

## CHAPTER XLIX.

But indeed, I wonder greatly why he attributed the work to Phidias, when it actually belonged to Lysippus <note>Phidias and Lysippus were two of the most famous sculptors of ancient Greece, though they lived in different eras</note>. Furthermore, the Greek author says the figure moves on the tips of his toes, whereas the Latin author says he stands upon a small wheel. Both authors attach winged sandals to the feet; both cover the face with hair; both leave the back of the head bald <note>This imagery signifies that "Opportunity" must be seized by the forelock as it approaches, for once it has passed, there is nothing to grab onto</note>. However, that Greek "Right Moment" carries a razor in his right hand, and Penance is joined as a companion to the Latin "Opportunity." The figures are similar in both poets, yet the Greek versions are somewhat more pleasing; for in all these <note>original: "mangonissandis," a colorful term meaning to polish or "furbish up" goods for sale</note> re-fashionings, I know not how the Greeks are more elegant than our Romans, and it was inevitable that the <margin>Original. / Copy.</margin> <term>copy<note>original: "apographon"</note></term> should fall short of the <term>original<note>original: "antigraphon"</note></term>. ### A passage in the works of Pliny corrected regarding the name of the *Proetides*, and the history of their madness recounted therein.

#### CHAPTER 50.

<margin>A passage in Pliny.</margin> In the twenty-fifth book of the *Natural History*, in the chapter where hellebore is discussed, the words of Pliny Secundus <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, better known as Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD)</note> are found in the most common manuscripts as follows: "The fame of Melampus is well known for his arts of divination; from him, one type of hellebore is called *Melampodion* <note>original: "Melampodion"</note>. Some relate that a shepherd of the same name discovered it, noticing that goats were purged by it, and by giving their milk, he cured the raging parotids." 

However, in one very ancient manuscript from that most noble library of the Medici family, we found not "by a shepherd" <note>original: "pastore"</note> but "by feeding" <note>original: "pasto"</note>, and not "parotids" but "the *Proetides*" <note>original: "Prœtidas," referring to the daughters of King Proetus who were famously driven to madness</note>. 

Now, regarding the choice between "shepherd" and "fed," the matter is easily settled. But that one must read "*Proetides*" and not "*parotids*" is a matter of manifest evidence. <margin>Parotids.</margin> Beyond what effective reason itself teaches (for what are "raging parotids"? <note>The author is pointing out that parotids—inflammations of the glands near the ear—do not "rage" or go "mad" like a person does</note> or what does <margin>Hellebore.</margin> <term>Veratrum<gloss>The Latin name for the hellebore plant, which was the primary treatment for madness in the ancient world.</gloss></term> have to do with parotids, which are an ailment of the ears?), <margin>Melampodium.</margin> Dioscorides, a fine authority, also declares this most clearly, writing as follows: "Black hellebore—some call it *Melampodium*, others *ectomon*, which..."

<summary>The author corrects a common error in Pliny the Elder's Natural History, arguing that the text should refer to Melampus curing the mad Proetides with hellebore, rather than "raging parotids."</summary>

<margin>A divinely inspired dance</margin>
Proetus agreed to pay the requested reward, but Melampus declared he would not perform the cure until an equal portion of land was also granted to his brother, Bias. At this point, Proetus—fearing that if he rejected the current terms, even more would be demanded shortly thereafter—consented to the price. Then, finally, Melampus took the strongest of the young men as his companions and, with shouting and a certain <term>divinely inspired dance <note>original: "enthea chorea"; refers to a ritualistic or ecstatic dance used in ancient healing rites.</note></term> (for so he describes it), drove the women from the mountains all the way to Sicyon. <margin>The purification of the daughters of Proetus</margin>
during the pursuit, the eldest daughter, Iphinoë, perished; the remaining sisters regained their senses through the use of purification. Then Proetus gave the daughters to Melampus and Bias <note>Implicitly as wives, which was the standard conclusion to this myth.</note>, and he himself later fathered a son named Megapenthes. This is essentially the account given by Apollodorus.

<margin>The names of the daughters of Proetus</margin>
Aelian, however, in his <note>Referring to the *Varia Historia* (Various History), a collection of anecdotes by the Roman author Claudius Aelianus.</note> *Various History*, gives the names of the daughters of Proetus as Elege and Celene. Furthermore, Sextus Empiricus, in the fourth book of his *Pyrrhonian Outlines*, writes that Polyanthus of Cyrene says in his book on the lineage of the <term>Asclepiads <note>Descendants or followers of Asclepius, the Greek god of medicine.</note></term> that the daughters of Proetus were freed from their madness—into which they had fallen because of the goddess Juno’s anger—by Asclepius himself. Ovid adds that to purify their minds, Bias sent them to the fountain of Clitor, the water of which makes those who drink it abstain from wine.

<margin>Probus, the commentator on Virgil</margin>
But indeed, the man commonly identified as Probus—a by no means "bad" <note>A play on words by the author: "Probus" means "good" or "upright" in Latin; he says he is "haud improbus" (not at all bad).</note> commentator on <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, known in English as Virgil.</note> Virgil—also cites Hesiod as a witness to the story. He touches upon the tale almost exactly as Apollodorus does, though he treats it very briefly. He notes that such was the delusion of their minds and spirits that they believed themselves to be <unclear>cows</unclear> <note>The text cuts off, but the myth traditionally states the sisters believed they were cows, a condition sometimes called "bovine madness."</note>

## WHY "SOFT HONORS" WERE NAMED BY MARTIAL IN THE TEMPLE OF EPHESIAN DIANA.

### CHAPTER 51.

**C**oncerning why Martial <margin>A passage from Martial</margin> <note>The Roman poet Martial, known for his witty and often biting epigrams</note> called the honors "soft" in the temple of Trivia—that is, the Diana of Ephesus—these points can also be offered. First, we might think this was said in reference to the <term>many-breasted</term> <margin>Many-breasted</margin> <note>original: "multimammijs"; referring to the famous cult statue of Artemis/Diana at Ephesus</note> statue. For as Saint Jerome writes in his commentary on Paul’s *Epistle to the Ephesians*, there was a temple of Diana at Ephesus containing a "multimammia" (that is, an image with many breasts), by which her worshippers were deceived into believing she was the nourisher of all living things. <margin>The Festival of Diana.</margin> He says that a solemn festival of Diana <note>original: "Dianæ"; refers to the goddess Artemis, for whom the great Temple at Ephesus—one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World—was built.</note> was being held in those places, leading from the city to the temple, a distance of exactly seven stades <note>A stade was an ancient unit of length; seven stades is approximately 0.8 miles or 1.3 kilometers.</note>. It was required for all the local young women to celebrate in the procession, most splendidly adorned, and furthermore the <term>ephebes<gloss>young men of military age, typically 17 to 20 years old, undergoing civic training</gloss></term>, including the peers of Abrocomas. Abrocomas was then about seventeen years old and was present among the youths, taking the leading role in that procession. A great multitude of people attended the spectacle, both locals and visitors; indeed, it was the custom that during this celebration, husbands were found for the young women and wives for the young men.

The procession therefore moved forward in an orderly fashion: first the sacred objects, namely torches, baskets, and incense; then horses, dogs, and hunting equipment, and even some weapons of war, though most were for times of peace. Each of the women had composed herself as if for the eyes of a lover, and <margin>Antia.</margin> **Antia** led the line of young women.

<margin>Xenophon of Ephesus.</margin> So Xenophon writes—not that famous Athenian <note>referring to Xenophon of Athens, the soldier and historian who wrote the *Anabasis*</note>, but another from Ephesus who is no less pleasant. A little later, he adds: "When the procession was finished and the whole crowd had come into the temple to sacrifice, all the finery of the procession was set aside. And there the men, women, youths, and maidens gathered together in the same place."

It is not out of place to hear of the "soft honors" <note>likely referring to the luxurious or effeminate nature of the ritual service</note> regarding the priest of Diana, about whom Domitius <note>likely the scholar Domitius Calderinus</note> also mentions something from the geographer Strabo. <margin>Heraclitus.</margin> Furthermore, there is mention of **Heraclitus of Ephesus**, that greatest of philosophers—specifically the one who, because of the obscurity of his books, was called <margin>Scotinos.</margin> <term>Scotinos<gloss>The Obscure</gloss></term>.

He who was called the "Dark One" <note>original: *tenebricosus*; Heraclitus of Ephesus was famously known as "The Obscure" because of his difficult, riddling philosophy.</note>, and who wept over all human actions—that Heraclitus, I say—wrote a letter to Hermodorus <note>Hermodorus was a friend of Heraclitus who was expelled by the Ephesians.</note> in which he mocks the Ephesians roughly like this: "How much better are wolves and lions than the Ephesians! They do not enslave one another, nor does an eagle buy another eagle, nor does a lion serve drinks to a lion. Neither does a dog castrate <note>original: *exsecat*</note> another dog, as you have castrated Megabyzus, the priest of the goddess <margin>A castrated priest</margin>, out of fear that a man might perform sacred rites for a virgin <note>The goddess Artemis (Diana) of Ephesus was a virgin deity; her high priests, the Megabyzi, were required to be eunuchs.</note>. In what way are you impious against nature, yet claim to be pious toward an idol? For in the first place, you arrange it so that the high priest first prays to the gods having lost his virility. Indeed, you even accuse the goddess of impurity, as if fearing she might be served by a man."

### WHAT THE "ALTAR CROWDED WITH HORNS" IN MARTIAL SIGNIFIES.
### CHAPTER 52.

In the previous epigram <note>Referring to Martial's *Liber Spectaculorum* (Book of Spectacles), Epigram 1.</note>, there is this other passage <margin>A passage from Martial.</margin> which, as far as I can see, has been understood neither by Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century Renaissance scholar and commentator on Latin poetry.</note> nor by others.

*And let the altar, crowded with horns, conceal the god.*

For while Domitius takes this to mean the temple of Ammon in Libya <note>The god Ammon was often depicted with the horns of a ram.</note>, this interpretation is in no way successful. For since the most magnificent buildings in the world are being recounted here—those which are counted among the wonders—so that by comparison with them, [the Roman works may be praised...]

<margin>Cicero’s little errors.</margin> Aulus Gellius, in those most brilliant *Attic Nights* <note>A famous 2nd-century collection of essays on grammar, philosophy, and history</note> of his, notes a very obvious error by Cicero. Nor is there any reason for anyone to be annoyed that such things are found in Cicero, or to be surprised, since it appears from his own letters that memory errors of this kind occurred in Cicero’s books, and were noticed and corrected by either Atticus or Brutus. <note>Titus Pomponius Atticus and Marcus Junius Brutus were close friends and literary advisors to Cicero.</note> This is just like that instance in the speech *For Ligarius* regarding Lucius Corfidius <note>The OCR reads "L. <margin>Distracted/Anxious.</margin>

## WHO THE "HORNED SYLLOGISMS" ARE <note>original: "Ceratinae"</note>
## in Quintilian.

### CHAPTER 54.

**C**ratinae—those who are called "Horned Syllogisms" in the first book of Quintilian <margin>Quintilian, Book 1</margin>—are not something you would easily find someone to explain to you. <margin>Horned Syllogisms</margin> However, those who have consulted Greek and Latin commentaries will clearly understand that this is a type of inextricable reasoning, which introduces certain "handles" and "hooks" of dialectical entanglement. I believe it is named from the very subject matter used in the phrasing, just as it appears in Seneca's fifth book of letters to Lucilius: "I might sit," he says, "idle, posing little riddles of this sort: 'What you have not lost, you have. You have not lost horns; therefore, you have horns,'" <note>This is the "Cornutus" or "Horned Man" fallacy, a classic sophism designed to trap the listener in a logical paradox.</note> and other things fashioned after the example of this "sharp delirium." But you may also find discussion of this in the *Attic Nights*. <note>A work by Aulus Gellius (2nd century AD) that preserves many fragments of ancient knowledge.</note> Now, "Horns" are called *kerata* in Greek. <note>original: κέρατα</note>

Who does not know this? Even Lucian mentions this in his *Symposium of Philosophers* <note>original: "philosophorum symposio"</note>, just as he does with the <term>Sorites</term> <note>The "Heap" paradox: if you remove one grain from a heap, is it still a heap? At what point does it cease to be one?</note> and the <term>Therizon</term> <note>The "Reaper" paradox, another logical puzzle concerning the possibility of action.</note>. But also in the *Dialogue of the Dead* <note>original: "dialogo mortuorũ"</note> between Castor and Pollux, he says roughly this: "And they produce horns among themselves, and create crocodiles, and teach how to ask such perplexing questions," so that from these "horns" you might understand this <term>Ceratine</term> <note>From the Greek word for "horn"; a famous sophism: "What you have not lost, you have. You have not lost horns, therefore you have horns."</note> reasoning—though you should take it as a snare <note>original: "tendiculam"</note> rather than true reasoning.

Furthermore, in another dialogue, titled either *The Dream* or *The Cock*: "And teaching," he says, "that from two negations one affirmation is constructed, and that it is not night if it is day. Sometimes he even told me I had horns, and other things of that sort." In these kinds of dialectical little questions—the sort that the sharpness of minds frequently devises—Eubulides of Miletus <note>A 4th-century BCE philosopher known for his mastery of logical paradoxes.</note> also made great use, as recorded by Diogenes Laertius in the *Life of Euclid of Megara*. To attempt a translation of these terms <note>The original text notes an attempt to translate into Latin; here they are provided in English.</note>: The Liar, The Deceiver, The Electra, The Veiled, The Heap, the Horned <note>original: "ceratine, uel cornea"</note>, and likewise The Bald. 

Because of this, a certain comic poet (whose name I do not know) says, as the same Laertius reports: "Eubulides, questioning with his 'Horned' puzzles and engaging orators with lying and boastful speeches, departed with the fluency of Demosthenes." The *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia and dictionary.</note> also cited this passage. But even Zeno of Citium <note>The founder of the Stoic school of philosophy.</note>, among the other quibbles of the dialecticians, also counted the "Horned" and the "Nobody" <note>original: "utidas", referring to the "Nobody" paradox, which plays on the name Odysseus used to trick the Cyclops.</note>—

### WHO THE CROCODILITES ARE
### in the same author.
### CHAPTER LV.

**A**lso in the same first book of Quintilian <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 AD), a celebrated Roman educator and rhetorician.</note>, mention is made of the **Crocodiline**, a term that is obscure and unknown to most. However, we find information about this in Doxapatres <note>John Doxapatres, an 11th-century Byzantine commentator.</note>, the Greek commentator on Aphthonius, although in his work it is called *Crocodilites* rather than *Crocodiline*, which I believe to be the more accurate term. We translate his words into English as follows: The **Saw**, he says, and the **Crocodilites** are like the Egyptian fable. A certain woman was walking with her son along the riverbanks; a crocodile snatched the son from her, saying he would return him if the woman answered truthfully <insert>regarding what he intended to do</insert>. She denied that he would be returned, and for that very reason, she argued it was fair that the child be returned. <note>The paradox: If the crocodile returns the child, her statement was false, so he shouldn't have returned him. If he keeps the child, her statement was true, so he was bound by his word to return him.</note> Thus far his account. Soon after, he says: <margin>Crocodili-</margin> "They call this proposition the **Crocodiliten** <margin>tes.</margin> because of this crocodile," which he indicates is also called a *Saw* <margin>Saw <note>original: "πρίωνα"</note></margin> by the Greeks. Lucian <note>A 2nd-century satirist known for mocking philosophers.</note> supports this in the dialogue titled *The Sale of Lives*, where he depicts Chrysippus <note>A Stoic philosopher famous for his complex logic and paradoxes.</note> roughly thus:

L iij <note>Printer's signature mark for the third leaf of the 'L' gathering.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the "Crocodile Paradox," a classic logical dilemma used in ancient rhetoric to illustrate self-referential or "sawing" arguments. It cites sources such as Quintilian, Doxapatres, and Lucian to explain the origin of the term from an Egyptian fable.</summary>

<keywords>Crocodile Paradox, logic, Quintilian, Doxapatres, Chrysippus, rhetoric, logical fallacies, Greek philosophy, Martianus Capella</keywords>

->B O O K<-

...introducing a speaker, and someone responding to him who claims to be a buyer. Consider it this way: "Do you have a son?" "Why do you ask?" "If by chance a crocodile should find him wandering near the river, snatch him, and then promise to return him if you tell the truth, do you think he should be returned or not?" What would you say his opinion is? You ask a truly perplexing thing. From these examples, as I see it, it is clear that the <term>Crocodile Paradox</term> <note>original: "Crocodiliten"; a famous ancient logic puzzle: if a crocodile steals a child and promises to return him if the parent correctly predicts what the crocodile will do, a paradox arises if the parent says "You will not return him."</note>, just like the <term>Horned Paradox</term> <note>original: "ceratinen"; another logic trap, such as: "What you have not lost, you have; you have not lost horns, therefore you have horns."</note> mentioned above, is a type of logic trap <note>original: "sophismatos"</note> that is difficult to untangle, which the ancient logicians, and especially the Stoics, used to employ. This is the category that Quintilian elegantly named "exquisite ambiguities."

### CHAPTER 56

<margin>A passage from Martial.</margin>

There is an epigram by **Martial** in his *On Spectacles* <note>A series of poems by the Roman poet Martial celebrating the opening of the Colosseum in 80 AD.</note> regarding the Rhinoceros, of which these are the final lines:
*For with its double horn it lifted the heavy bear,*
*Just as a bull tosses straw dummies to the stars.* <note>original: "Nanq; graue cornu gemino sic extulit ursũ / Iactat ut impositas taurus in astra pilas." Martial refers to the rhinoceros tossing a bear in the arena like a bull tosses training targets.</note>
**Domitius** <note>Likely Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century Renaissance scholar who wrote famous commentaries on Martial.</note> explains it thus: "By 'double horn,'" he says, "he means a strong and powerful horn." **Virgil** used a similar phrase: "With double-backed plowshares." <note>original: "Gemino dentalia dorso."</note> Or perhaps it is because the Rhinoceros actually has two horns—as I have read only in **Pausanias** <note>A 2nd-century Greek traveler and geographer.</note>—the first of which is of remarkable size on the nose...

<summary>This page discusses the "Crocodile" logic paradox used by ancient Stoics and begins a philological inquiry into why the poet Martial described a rhinoceros as having two horns.</summary>

Cornua unicornis, cornua eius, in eis nationes uentilabit ad ſummū uſq; terræ."</note> He states that certainly the one-horned Rhinoceros was not what was meant <margin>Rhinoceros</margin>, nor the two-horned Minotaur, and so on.

For his words are: *for these creatures, on the tip of their nose* <note>Original Greek: οἷς σφίσιν ἐπ’ ἄκρᾳ τῇ ῥινὶ</note>—as his actual words go—*each has a single horn, and another above it which is not large; but on the head they have no horns at all.* <note>Original Greek: ἑνὶ ἑκάστῳ κέρας καὶ ἄλλο ὑπὲρ αὐτὸ οὐ μέγα, ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς κεφαλῆς οὐδ’ ἀρχὴν κέρατά ἐστιν</note> Therefore, Pausanias does not say that the second horn is of remarkable size, nor that the second is the strongest, which...

Domitius <note>Likely Domitius Calderinus (1446–1478), a prominent Renaissance scholar known for his commentaries on Martial.</note> perhaps recalls this, so that the bear might be more easily lifted up by these means. For although bulls toss straw-stuffed decoys <note>original: "pilas," these were stuffed effigies used to provoke animals in the Roman arena.</note> into the air with their horns, they could not lift a bear, which is "heavy for a twin-horned" creature; for that reason, the Rhinoceros lifted it more miraculously with but a single horn.

# WHAT THE *TETRAONES* ARE IN SUETONIUS,

### CHAPTER 57.

**T**etraones <term>Tetraones: a genus of birds today known as the grouse family, including the black grouse and capercaillie.</term>. ->MISCELLANIES.        70<-

...for what we read in him <note>The author is referring to Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), whose "Natural History" was a primary source of scientific knowledge in the Renaissance.</note> in the tenth book of his *Natural History*, where he discusses geese: "The *erythrotaonas* <note>original: "Erythrotaonas"; a corrupted Greek-derived term in the manuscript</note> are graced by their own brilliance and absolute blackness." In that most ancient Plinian manuscript from the public library of the Medici family, this is read almost correctly—that is, with only one letter changed. Such altered <note>original: "interpolata"; words that were corrupted or mistakenly changed by copyists over centuries</note> terms are common in all ancient volumes; for although they are very faulty in many places, they still preserve clear traces for tracking down the true reading, which are completely erased from newer copies by careless scribes. In that manuscript, then, it is written: "And the *traonas* are graced," so that if you swap the order of the first two letters, "*tetraonas*" <term>Tetraonids: A family of birds that includes grouse, heath hens, and ptarmigans. The author is correcting a misspelling to restore the correct name of the bird.</term> leaps out. Therefore, according to Politiano <note>Angelo Poliziano (1454–1494), a brilliant Italian humanist scholar famous for his "Miscellanea," which used philology to correct classical texts.</note>, you should read the passage in his work as: "The tetraonids are graced by their own brilliance and absolute blackness. There is a scarlet redness on their eyebrows."

    The other kind of these birds exceeds the size of vultures. It shares their color, and no <margin>Ostrich.</margin> bird except the Ostrich <note>original: "Struthocamelo"</note> carries a greater weight of body; it grows so large that it is even caught while immobile on the ground. The Alps and the northern regions produce them. Thus far he <note>Pliny</note> speaks concerning the tetraonids.

**ORIGIN AND RITES OF THE SECULAR GAMES,**
*and in addition other matters related to this; and the Sibyl’s oracle cited shortly thereafter, with certain points explained in passing, and others refuted to good effect.*

**CHAPTER 58.**

<margin>Origin of the secular games.</margin>
**I** believe it will be worth the effort to collect here the origin of the <term>Secular Games</term> <note>original: "secularium ludorum"; these were grand religious celebrations involving sacrifices and theatrical performances, theoretically held once every "saeculum" or long lifetime, usually defined as 100 or 110 years.</note>, the reason for their name, and likewise the interval of years between them, how often they occurred, by what ritual, and for which gods they were celebrated. For the subject itself is not unworthy of being known by anyone with a passion for antiquity, and it provides great assistance in understanding several passages of the poets. 

<margin>Deceptions of demons.</margin> 
Therefore, when the Romans and the Albans <note>The inhabitants of Alba Longa, an ancient city in central Italy and a rival to early Rome.</note> were waging war against one another, and both battle lines were already standing ready for action, there suddenly appeared a certain figure of monstrous appearance, clad in a dark skin, shouting that <term>Father Dis</term> <note>original: "Ditem patrem"; the Roman god of the underworld, also known as Pluto.</note> and the goddess Proserpina commanded a sacred rite be performed for them before the battle began. Terrified by this sight, the Romans immediately built an altar underground, and having performed the sacrifice at once, they covered it with a twenty-foot mound of earth, so that it might be unknown to everyone except the Romans themselves. 

<margin>Valesus Valesius.</margin> 
But it so happened that Valesus Valesius—from whom the name and origin of the <term>Valerian family</term> <note>One of the most distinguished patrician families in ancient Rome, whose members held numerous consulships.</note> is derived, a most illustrious and wealthy man among the Sabine people—living a rustic...

However, because he knew that the city of Tarentum was a very long distance away—specifically in the furthest reaches of Iapygia <note>The southeastern heel of Italy</note>—and that no Tiber river was found near that city, he began to entertain the worst fears. He was also terrified because that voice had mentioned the powers of the underworld in particular. Nevertheless, he placed his children in a small boat and, heading toward Ostia, soon drifted down to the region of the Campus Martius. Desiring to refresh the children, who were thirsty and suffering from the heat and fever, he set the sick ones out on the bank where the river flows most calmly. While he was preparing a makeshift little mat <margin>Little mat</margin> for them and his small fire had gone out, he was advised by the pilot <margin>Tarentum.</margin> that they should instead head for Tarentum (for that was what the nearby place was called), as the pilot could see smoke rising there. Then indeed, Valesius, becoming more joyful upon hearing the name of Tarentum, began to worship the gods; now almost in good faith promising himself the recovery of his children, he ordered them to push on immediately toward that spot.

Furthermore, they were called "Secular" <margin>Why they are called Secular.</margin> not because they occurred in the hundredth year or even the hundred-and-tenth—though both views have their supporters, the facts themselves refute them. Rather, it is more believable that they were generally held once in a human lifetime <term>lifetime: The Latin word 'saeculum' refers to the maximum span of a human life, usually 100 or 110 years.</term>; just as many other rare things are commonly said in everyday speech to happen "after an age."

They were called "Tarentine," however, after the place itself, which is named Tarentum <margin>Tarentum.</margin> for this reason: either because the altar of Father Dis <note>The Roman god of the underworld, similar to Pluto.</note> (as we mentioned above) was hidden in the earth there, or because the river Tiber wore away the bank at that spot <note>original: "tereret," which the author links to the name Tarentum.</note>. For this reason, the ancients also called the river "Rumon," <margin>Rumon.</margin> and in sacred rites it was named "Serra," <margin>Serra.</margin> because it "ruminated"—that is, it consumed and cut away the banks. -> Grazing the banks and cutting through the rich fields. <note>original: "Stringentem ripas, & pinguia culta secantem" (Virgil, Aeneid 8.63).</note> <-

On the other hand, Valerius Antias, Titus Livy, and likewise Marcus Varro are said to have testified that the games return after a hundred years. ...I believe it should be read with indulgence. Furthermore, if you were to count the eras, you should not entirely conclude either how great the intervals were in the past, or how great they ought to be. <margin>The institution of the Secular Games.</margin> It is believed that the first **Secular Games** <note>The *Ludi Saeculares* were a religious celebration involving sacrifices and theatrical performances, held once every "saeculum" (an age or century), intended to mark the end of one era and the start of the next.</note> were established by Publius Valerius Publicola, who was the first consul, after the kings had been expelled. He held games and *lectisternia* <term>A sacred banquet offered to the gods, where their images were placed on couches as if they were guests at the meal.</term> for three nights, and he hid the altar by piling up earth just as it had been before, inscribed with this title: *Publius Valerius Publicola dedicated the grain field to Dis and Proserpina, and held games for Dis and Proserpina for the sake of the safety of the Romans.* <note>original: "P. Valerius Publicola frumentarium campū Diti & Proſerpinæ cõſecraui, & ludos Diti & Proſerpinæ Romanorum ſalutis ergo feci."</note>

The historian Zosimus, writing in Greek, records that the second games were held in the fifty-second year after the founding of Rome, following the advice of the **Sibylline Oracles** <note>A collection of oracular utterances, purchased by the last king of Rome, which were consulted by the state during times of crisis.</note>. Men were chosen for the specific purpose of consulting the books while the city was gripped by disease. Following their response, the earth was again dug up at this very altar at the edge of the **Campus Martius** <note>The "Field of Mars," a public area in ancient Rome used for military training and certain religious festivals.</note>; the traditional sacred rites and games were completed, and safety was once again restored to the Romans. However, this writer—perhaps due to a flawed manuscript—named only one consul, Marcus Publius Quintius, while Censorinus records the consuls as Marcus Valerius and Spurius Virginius. He also records that the third and fourth games occurred during the times of the consuls whom he cites by name...

as if by a white line <margin>A white line.</margin> <note>original: "alba linea"; an ancient proverb referring to a mark that is difficult to distinguish, or an undiscriminating judgment. Poliziano uses it here to suggest that as a "great lover" of literature, he can spot the subtle signs of his critics' envy immediately.</note>, I immediately recognized the habits of envy; for while it maneuvers to cause harm, it turns itself into every shape, just like that Proteus described by Licentius:
*The boar foams, the wave flows, the lion roars, the snake hisses.* <note>original: "Spumat aper, fluit unda, fremit leo, sibilat anguis"; these lines describe the shapeshifting of the sea-god Proteus, used here as a metaphor for the changing tactics of envious critics.</note>
But because I had become toughened against its blows, I was not for that reason angry with my own studies—though they repay such great labor with such a [bitter] reward—nor did I condemn myself for not having soothed this most foul plague with more idleness. Nor did I, as some perhaps desired, tear the book to pieces. Instead, I set it aside for a little while and, putting away for the present any care for publishing it, I only occasionally watered the book—just as one might water a garden <note>The author uses a "hydroponic" metaphor here, suggesting he nurtured the text in private while waiting for the right time.</note> through little diversion-channels. Eventually, I transferred my fuller thoughts **from husks to bread** <note>original: "à siliquis ad panes"; a metaphor for moving from the "chaff" or "husks" of light, playful poetry and grammar to the "solid bread" of serious Philosophy.</note>—that is, from playful trifles to the serious matters of Philosophy. Thus, I resolved to occupy my mind with somewhat higher studies and work of a weightier nature, which in their own time (if the matter is handled with mastery) will appear for the public good, all the more to make the eyes of those petty cavilers ache.

For indeed, while I was still at a tender age, I studied under two most excellent men. First, Marsilio Ficino <margin>Praise of Marsilio Ficino.</margin> <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was the leader of the Platonic Academy in Florence and a central figure of the Renaissance.</note> of Florence, whose lyre was far more successful than that of Orpheus of Thrace; for he (unless I am mistaken) called back the true Eurydice—that is, the Platonic wisdom of the most profound judgment—from the underworld. And also John Argyropoulos <margin>Argyropoulos.</margin> <note>John Argyropoulos (1415–1487) was a Byzantine scholar who fled to Italy and became a primary teacher of Aristotelian philosophy and Greek literature in Florence.</note> of Byzantium, by far the most famous of the <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy, so named because Aristotle used to walk ("peripateo" in Greek) while teaching.</note> of his time. Under both, I truly applied myself to philosophy.

He is already most learned in all of philosophy, and further fortified and equipped in various literatures of languages and all honorable arts beyond belief. Finally, to sum up, he is greater than any praise. He, then, who is accustomed to share with me his sweetest cares and sometimes to play pleasantly, and who has chosen me as his nearly constant companion in studies (which is the highest honor), he urged me to explore philosophy, not with sleepy eyes as before, but with vigorous and wakeful ones, as if animating me by the trumpet-call <term>classicum: a Roman military trumpet signal used to rouse soldiers</term> of his voice. Therefore, having found in him such a great authority or leader, I by no means [missed] the opportunity...

**MISCELLANIES. 124**

I have not failed, but rather I have run toward and seized opportunities, so that by the almost constant handling of the greatest authors—as if by repeatedly striking flint—I seem to have struck out at least a few sparks of truth, and to have caught so much flame in the kindling that in this blind and **moonless night**, <margin>**Moonless night**</margin> something of a brightness at last begins to shine for me. But behold, meanwhile, as I was entirely occupied with this, suddenly that *Cornucopia* <note>The *Cornucopia* was a massive commentary on Martial by the scholar Niccolò Perotti, published posthumously in 1489.</note> appeared in public. A crowd gathers. It is in everyone's hands. It is poured out. It is examined. What more can I say? **The Cornucopia.** <margin>**The Cornucopia.**</margin> Calumny sets me free. You would have immediately seen the faces of some people silently expressing something mournful, and—though they were never accustomed to it before—I know not how (like that Thrasymachus in Plato’s work) they were blushing. But what I might have seen within that "Horn" <note>A pun on the title *Cornucopia* (Horn of Plenty).</note>, although it is certainly not empty, is not for this time to judge. **The Republic,** <margin>**The Republic, Book 1.**</margin> Let it suffice for now that I did not have the same aim as the author of that volume, **Book 1.** <margin>** **</margin> nor was the same goal or peak intended for both of us. For if anywhere we happened to treat the same passages as the subject arose (and this was bound to happen in some places), there is a thicker wall between us than there was between Pyramus and Thisbe. <note>A reference to the mythological lovers who whispered through a thin crack in a wall; the author argues that his work and Perotti's are fundamentally different even when they discuss the same topic.</note> Let that Cotytto of Juvenal <note>A reference to the goddess of lewdness mentioned in Juvenal's Satires.</note> be proof for now—which in his work is "Cocytus"—about which he fights with Domitius <note>Domizio Calderini, another rival scholar known for his aggressive style.</note> as if over a poor kingdom; or likewise the Altar of Martial, frequently mentioned with its "horns," or Philyra, or anything else of that sort, which you might choose to entrust to my rank-and-file soldiers and **reserve troops**. <margin>**Reserve troops.**</margin> Indeed, it has happened that by this very means...

...while there was a pause in miscellaneous works, a hundred learned—by Hercules! (who would deny it?)—and painstaking annotations were published by a man very dear to us. This man is not only well-aware of our current project but is himself a fine man of letters; he even mentioned us with kindness and gratitude in his work. In these notes, I believe one or two points, by chance, share almost the same reasoning as mine. Certainly, several are nearly identical to those lessons which I dictated in a public lecture hall—before a great crowd, as everyone knows—either nine years ago when I was interpreting Ovid’s <note>The original text refers to "Naso," the cognomen of the Roman poet Ovid.</note> *Fasti* <note>The *Fasti* is a poetic treatise on the Roman calendar and its religious festivals.</note>, or later when I was interpreting the *Satires* of Horace <note>The original text refers to "Flaccus," the cognomen of the Roman poet Horace.</note>. I would swear most solemnly (if there were a need), even while holding the altar, that I immediately thought to concede these points to my close friend—if not as my own right, then certainly out of a shared intellectual right—and not to claim any of those things for which he had claimed the title, and I think not unjustly. For I am not so swollen with pride, nor do I favor myself so much, that I wish to seem the teacher of such a learned man in any matter, lest that old proverb be thrown in my face: "the pig teaching Minerva" <note>original: "sus Minervam". This was a common Latin idiom used when an ignorant person attempts to instruct an expert, as Minerva was the goddess of wisdom.</note>. 

But since he claimed to have been the first to discover these things, asserting that they were entirely unknown (or so I believe he thought) and unexplained by others until that day, I am forced to confess—which he himself, given the refined character <term>humanitas: the Renaissance ideal of "humanity" or "refinement" involving kindness and scholarly cultivation</term> he professes, will easily tolerate—that I must simply and candidly clear my professional reputation of those suspicions of general ignorance. For I could rightly be considered either uncivil or arrogant if I did not believe that for a man who is so diligent and erudite, these findings and even better ones could be born from his own mind...

<summary>The author addresses a potential conflict over intellectual property, noting that a colleague recently published annotations similar to the author's earlier public lectures on Ovid and Horace. While wishing to remain humble and avoid the "pig teaching Minerva" proverb, the author feels compelled to assert his prior knowledge to protect his scholarly reputation.</summary>

**C** when the poet Accius <note>Lucius Accius (c. 170–86 BC) was a Roman tragic poet and literary scholar.</note> happened to visit Pacuvius <note>Marcus Pacuvius (c. 220–130 BC), the leading tragic poet of the generation before Accius.</note> at Tarentum—who was already advanced in age and suffering from a long physical illness—he read his tragedy titled *Atreus* at the elder man's request, though Accius himself was much younger. # ON ANGER

...indeed as one who hoped to write better things hereafter. For **human natural talents** are almost like **apples**: those which are born somewhat hard and bitter to the taste nevertheless ripen in time and become mild and pleasant. But if they are born soft and moist from the start, like those that ripen too early, they become withered and rotten long before they can truly mature. Therefore, one must allow the intellect, just like fruit, that which is mellowed by time and age.

"Whither does this lead?" you ask. Since indeed, **Lorenzo de' Medici**, many things exist in the human intellect which, though they display the appearance of a faulty and depraved nature, are yet judged by wise men as signs of future virtue and specimens of an excellent character. 

Such perhaps is this very thing which I recently declared—regarding your little son <note>Likely referring to one of Lorenzo the Magnificent's sons, such as Piero or Giovanni (the future Pope Leo X), who were tutored by humanists like Poliziano.</note> and our pupil—that the anger which arose from a rebuke and a received disgrace was not at all to be condemned. Since you have asked for the reason behind our judgment, it will certainly not be out of place to discuss a few things on that matter for the present. 

Nor is it our purpose at this time to praise anger, though the <term>Peripatetics: Followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy, who believed that passions like anger were useful if moderated by reason.</term> think it was not uselessly granted to us by nature, inasmuch as it incites courage with certain "spurs" (as they say); indeed, as **Aristotle** himself says, it may even be considered a part of courage itself. But since out of every kind of vice, that should certainly be held the least which wanders least from the path of reason; and anger, if indeed...

# TO L[ORENZO] DE' MEDICI. 126

We must believe Aristotle, or in a way listen to reason itself and follow in its footsteps as much as possible; it is right to believe that anger <note>The author is arguing that a capacity for anger is a sign of spirit and potential in a child, rather than a pure vice.</note> is deservedly the least of all vices. Since, indeed, as we find in the works of Flaccus <note>The Roman poet Horace (Quintus Horatius Flaccus).</note>, "no one is born without vices, and the best man is he who is weighed down by the fewest," we must admit that anger in a young boy is certainly not entirely detestable. On the contrary, that state of mind which is completely devoid of anger—which the Greeks call <term>aorgēsia</term> <note>original: ἀοργησίαν; a lack of spirit or the inability to feel rightful anger.</note> <margin>Lack of anger.</margin>—seems to me as blameworthy in a boy as sluggishness is in a young horse. Because at such a weak and helpless age, since this lack of anger cannot proceed from reason <note>Meaning the child isn't being "peaceful" out of a rational choice to be virtuous.</note>, it must necessarily proceed from a stupor and a dullness of mind. For those in whom there is a great deal of cold black bile, as Aristotle says in his *Problems*, are dull-witted, stunned, and lazy. But those in whom the bile is plentiful and warm, they are quick-moving, talented, sharp, and prone to every kind of flare-up. Indeed, natural philosophers consider heat to be far more in harmony with nature than cold. The proof of this is clearly understood from many other things, but especially from the seasons of the year. For "when sharp winter is loosened by the pleasant change of spring and the West Wind," <note>original: "Cū enim ſoluitur acris hyems grata uice ueris, & fauoni," a quotation from Horace, Odes 1.4.1.</note> soon those most beautiful ornaments of the world and nature revive: the woods are clothed in leaves, the fields are distinguished by flowers and grasses of every kind, from which an incredible sweetness of scent is wafted. From here, the now-clearer air is soothed by the most charming harmonies of little birds; from here, the waters in the streams...
                                        
<summary>The author argues that a hot-tempered nature in a boy is a sign of vitality and future intelligence, comparing a lack of spirit (aorgēsia) to the coldness of winter and a lively temperament to the rebirth of spring.</summary>

->TO LORENZO DE' MEDICI. 127<-

Quintilian, <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 AD) was a celebrated Roman educator and rhetorician whose work "Institutio Oratoria" shaped Western education.</note> that most excellent of teachers, demands that a boy be given to him who weeps when defeated and boasts when victorious. Indeed, he is not offended by such play in a child, because this very thing we are discussing—anger—is evidence of a vigorous mind and a noble nature. For this reason, among those ancient philosophers (as found in Plutarch), it had already passed into a proverb that "he who has no anger, has no mind." <note>original: "iram non habere, qui mentem non habeat." This suggests that a capacity for emotion is a prerequisite for intellectual capacity.</note>

Perhaps this is why, when Aristotle <note>The Greek philosopher Aristotle was often cited in the Middle Ages and Renaissance as an authority on "physiognomy," the pseudoscience of judging character from physical features.</note> judges a person's character from the appearance of their face, he says that those with a wide forehead are dull-witted, while those with a round forehead are prone to anger. It is an obvious argument that nature herself wishes us to be warned by the features of the face that nothing differs so greatly from stupidity as a quickness to anger. 

But truly, since two different emotions, though springing from the same head and source, are identified by different names—the one which, while it has milder initial impulses, is much more long-lasting and permanent, driving its roots deeper into the soul; the other indeed sharper and more stirred up at first, but more easily pacified in its own time—the former, which the Greeks call sometimes *wrath* <note>original: μῆνιν (mênis)</note> and sometimes *a grudge* <note>original: κότον (kotos)</note>, and which I believe our own people call *rancor*, is considered by wise men to be a sign of an ignoble and stubborn nature. For it tends toward hatred, toward vengeance, toward injury, and toward insult. Nor does it travel the straight path of taking revenge, but more often relies on craftiness and snares. When it has mostly cooled down, then most...

that anger in a boy is proof of a noble, upright, and free nature. Years will come, along with literature and discipline. Everything

it withers. But perhaps you do not

even be shameful and detestable. Yet surely the same sword, 
if it is carried in the hand of an executioner, seems detestable. If it is at 
the side of a soldier or a Commander, it displays an image of courage and an 
excellent deed.

<meta>This page continues a series of addresses or letters, likely part of a pedagogical or moral treatise dedicated to a member of the Medici family. The previous context likely discussed the development of character or the education of youth.</meta>

->TO LORENZO DE' MEDICI. 129<-

Vices often sprout in a noble mind mixed with virtues, just as in a rich soil that has not yet felt the farmer’s hand, brambles and thorns grow. Nor do those who toil in the study of agriculture value that appearance of fertile soil any less for it. Even if it brings forth mandrake flowers from the "half-man's" <note>The mandrake root was often thought to resemble the human form, hence "half-man."</note> wild grass, and mournful hemlock; even if it offers no gentle stalks for the hands, nor spares the legs from the backs of brambles and the sharp spines of the Christ-thorn. <note>original: "paliuron," a prickly shrub often identified as the Jerusalem thorn.</note>

Therefore, just as good farmers are nonetheless accustomed to pluck and root out those plants and herbs harmful to the crops because they recognize the potential of a fertile soil through them, so indeed parents and teachers should no more indulge vices while they are still tender, even though those vices provide evidence of an excellent and noble nature. One must "resist the beginnings," <note>original: "Principiis obstandum," a famous maxim from Ovid’s *Remedia Amoris* suggesting that problems should be stopped before they take root.</note> and those things which stand out must be blunted. 

Like Tarquin, the highest heads of the poppies must be struck off with a rod, <note>This refers to a story about the Roman king Tarquin the Proud, who silently instructed his son to execute the leading men of a city by striking off the heads of the tallest poppies in his garden with a stick.</note> and one must always resist the more powerful impulses. Yet, meanwhile, it should not be considered foreign to the pattern of a well-ordered nature if the boy—who already knows how to speak, marks the ground with a steady step, longs to play with his peers, and gathers anger only to cast it aside heedlessly, changing by the hour. <note>The author is quoting Horace’s *Ars Poetica* (lines 158–160) to describe the natural, fickle behavior of children which, while requiring guidance, is a normal stage of development.</note>

Farewell.

<summary>This letter to Lorenzo de' Medici uses agricultural metaphors to explain that while a child's early vices may actually signal a "fertile" and noble mind, they must be disciplined early—just as a farmer weeds rich soil to protect his crops.</summary>
